A monoidal category of dependently sorted algebraic theories II: categorical aspects
Daniel Almeida

Abstract
This is the second of a pair of papers where we construct and investigate a closed monoidal structure on the category of generalized algebraic theories (in the sense of Cartmell). Having presented the tensor product of theories in a syntactic way, we now study the same structure from the perspective of contextual categories. We define the exponential 𝒜 ℬ \mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{B}} between two contextual categories 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , ℬ \mathcal{B} , and show how this yields, as a particular case, a cotensor 𝒜 B \mathcal{A}^{B} by a small category B B . We also introduce a concept of multimorphism ( 𝒜 1 , … , 𝒜 n ) → ℬ (\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{B} for contextual categories 𝒜 i \mathcal{A}_{i} , ℬ \mathcal{B} , and describe a bijective correspondence between bimorphisms ( 𝒜 , ℬ ) → 𝒞 (\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C} and morphisms 𝒜 → 𝒞 ℬ \mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{B}} . We give an abstract proof that there exists a contextual category 𝒜 ⊗ ℬ \mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B} such that bimorphisms ( 𝒜 , ℬ ) → 𝒞 (\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C} are in natural bijection with morphisms 𝒜 ⊗ ℬ → 𝒞 \mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C} . We extend ⊗ : Cont × Cont → Cont \otimes:\text{Cont}\times\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Cont} into a closed symmetric monoidal structure and give a description of certain pushout-tensor maps that, in particular, allows us to prove that the tensor product of theories from [ 5 ] is functorial and presents the one constructed here.



1
Introduction

Generalized algebraic theories (
gat
s for short), introduced by J. Cartmell in the 1970s (
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
), expand the framework of classical universal algebra, focused on the study of equational theories, by allowing the introduction of dependent sorts. Similarly to how equational theories were translated into categorical language in Lawvere’s 1963 thesis (
[
29
]
), Cartmell modelled generalized algebraic theories using a class of structures called contextual categories. The main result of
[
11
]
is the proof that, writing
GAT
(resp.
Cont
) for the category of generalized algebraic theories and equivalence classes of interpretations (resp. of contextual categories and contextual functors), the functor

\mathcal{C}(-):\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{Cont},

associating with each theory its syntactic category is an equivalence of categories.

In
[
5
]
, we defined the tensor product product
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
of two generalized algebraic theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, extending Freyd’s tensor product (or Kronecker product) of algebraic theories (
[
17
]
), and in analogy with Kelly’s tensor product of finitely (co)complete categories (
[
28
]
). This was done in an algorithmic and purely syntactic way: the axioms of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
are exhibited mechanically from those of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, a feature that is helpful in performing explicit calculations and comparing our construction with previously known ones. As a downside, we had very little access to information allowing us to work with that construction in a categorical way. In particular, our techniques were not sufficient to prove that
\otimes
defines a (symmetric) monoidal structure on the category
GAT
of generalized algebraic theories and equivalence classes of interpretations: we made limited progress towards turning
\otimes
into a functor
\text{GAT}\times\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{GAT}
, and we sketched the construction of isomorphisms
\mathbb{A}\otimes(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{C})\cong(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathbb{C}
. One of the main obstructions was the lack of a presentation-independent formulation of the tensor product of
gat
s, in the sense that a
gat

\mathbb{A}
can be thought of as a presentation of the structure exhibited by the syntactic (contextual) category
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
— see
[
12
]
, §13, 14. Alternatively, we had not been able to describe what exactly
\otimes
amounts to once we move across the equivalence
\mathcal{C}(-):\text{GAT}\simeq\text{Cont}
, where
Cont
denotes the category of contextual categories and contextual functors.

The present text aims at filling these gaps in the study of the tensor product and, simultaneously, exploring other closely related structures, such as multimorphisms and exponentials of contextual categories. In fact, aside from the characterization of the tensor product of
gat
s in terms of contextual categories, most of this article does not use the syntax of
gat
s and can be read independently of
[
5
]
.

For contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
, a bimorphism
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
1

1

In fact, in the definition of a bimorphism
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
we allow
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
to be, more generally, precontextual categories; see the discussion below.
from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{C}
will be a special kind of functor
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
where
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
denotes the cartesian product of the underlying categories.
2
2

2

Which differs, generally, from cartesian product of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in
Cont
.
The definition is designed, in particular, to capture the main features of the canonical functor
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
from
[
5
]
, §6; and indeed, the latter is verified to be a bimorphism in Proposition
3.28
. But more generally, we would like a bimorphism
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
to decompose as

\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\overset{\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}

for a unique contextual functor
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. In other words, we want
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
to be a
universal
bimorphism. This means that the axioms for a bimorphism should capture, even if indirectly, the same kind of interaction between
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
as
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
does (including the apparent asymmetry imposed by the choice of lexicographic order; see
[
5
]
, §8).

This universal property of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
, which then gives
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\in\text{GAT}\simeq\text{Cont}
a universal property, will be crucial in filling some of the gaps from
[
5
]
. In particular, it immediately implies that the tensor product defines a functor
\otimes:\text{GAT}\times\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{GAT}
, while from syntactic approach it was not clear even whether
\mathbb{A}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
yield
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
. This also means that we can talk about
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
(as a structure defined up to canonical isomorphism) for contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
.

The definition of a bimorphism that we adopt is inspired by the ‘‘cofibration part” of the concept of a Quillen bifunctor from categorical homotopy theory: after passing to opposite categories
3
3

3

A clearer analogy is achieved by working with “co-contextual categories”.
, we require that certain colimits are preserved in each variable, and that a pushout-product operation sends display maps to display maps. However, ensuring that
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
has a
1
-categorical universal property requires asking bimorphisms to be strictly compatible with the non-categorical structure present in contextual categories: lengths of objects, display maps, and distinguished squares.
4
4

4

Recall that the latter two are not closed under isomorphism in the categories of arrows and of commutative squares, resp., of the contextual category.
Our list of axioms is discussed in §2.3.

In fact, bimorphisms
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
can be described in a recursive way once we present
\mathbb{A}
, resp.
\mathbb{B}
, as the increasing union of an ordinal sequence of theories where at level
\nu+1
we add as an axiom a judgment that is well-formed at level
\nu
. This will be a key point in proving that
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
is a universal bimorphism. To have a slightly more precise idea of how this will work, consider theory extensions
\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
, each adding a single axiom, say
J
and
J^{\prime}
, respectively. Then we have a commutative diagram
5
5

5

Note that this does not require functoriality of the tensor product, which we currently have no access to. We only use that, by definition,
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
is an extension of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, and so on.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime}}}

and also a theory
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B})
– which is a pushout
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\sqcup_{\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B})
in
GAT
– obtained by taking the union of the two sets of axioms. Then, by construction, either
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
is
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B})
itself, or it is obtained from the latter by adding an axiom
J\odot J^{\prime}
, in which case we have a pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}((\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

where
\kappa
is a “free-standing axiom”: it only depends on what kind
J\odot J^{\prime}
is of (sort, term, sort equality, or term equality) and on the length of its context. For example, if
J\odot J^{\prime}
is a sort judgment whose context has length
n
, then
\kappa
will be the canonical embedding
\mathcal{O}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n+1}
of the contextual category freely generated by a length-
n
object into the one freely generated by a length-
(n+1)
object.
6
6

6

See the discussion below on precontextual categories.
On the other hand,
\chi
will be obtained by expressing
J\odot J^{\prime}
in terms of objects and morphisms in the images of

\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\overset{\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\overset{\otimes_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime},\mathbb{B}}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}).

Now, the problem of classifying contextual functors
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
splits into two:

(i)

classifying morphisms
\mathcal{C}((\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, hence commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E};}}

(ii)

classifying, for a given morphism
F:\mathcal{C}((\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, extensions of
F\circ\chi
along
\kappa
.

If we assume that
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
,
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}^{\prime}}
and
\otimes_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime},\mathbb{B}}
are universal bimorphisms, then diagrams as in (i) correspond precisely to pairs consisting of bimorphisms
G:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and
H:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

commutes. These determine a class of functors
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime}))\sqcup_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})}(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, namely, those that give bimorphisms after restriction to the two components. This is the point where we are able to establish the desired bijective correspondence between morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and bimorphisms
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
: consider a morphism
F:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\sqcup_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})}\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, and let
F^{\prime}:(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime}))\sqcup_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})}(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be the corresponding “componentwise bimorphism”; then we can explicitly classify

•

extensions of
F\circ\chi
along
\kappa:\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
(which is straightforward once we know how to describe
\chi
), and

•

extensions of
F^{\prime}
to a bimorphism
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
,

and construct a bijection between these data.

Generally, suppose given
gat
s
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
. Consider a sequence of theories
(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})_{\mu\leq\alpha}
, where
\alpha
is an ordinal, such that

•

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{A}
;

•

for
\mu<\alpha
, the theory
\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}
is obtained from
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}
by adding a single axiom;

•

for a limit ordinal
\mu\leq\alpha
, we have
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}=\bigcup_{\mu^{\prime}<\mu}\mathbb{A}_{\mu^{\prime}}
(in particular,
\mathbb{A}_{0}=\varnothing
).

Similarly, present
\mathbb{B}
via a sequence
(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})_{\nu<\beta}
. Then we can obtain by recursion on the well-founded poset
\{\mu+1\}\times\{\nu+1\}
, using the aforementioned construction as the recursion step, a bijection between the set of contextual functors
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and that of bimorphisms
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
.

We will also introduce (Definition
3.11
) an exponentiation operation
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})\mapsto\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
for which we have isomorphisms

\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}})

natural in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}^{\text{op}}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
. Note that, by the Yoneda lemma, knowledge of the exponentiation functor characterizes the tensor product up to canonical isomorphism. On the other hand, it turns out that
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
can be described explicitly and independently of the tensor product, and even of the concept of a bimorphism: it will be presented by a certain category with attributes
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
whose dependent types encode a relative notion of morphism
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(which we call an
indexed sort
). This construction takes place naturally in the framework of categories with attributes, hence in that of contextual categories, and it is not clear to me how to express it via the syntax of
gat
s in a simple way.

When
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}(D)
for a locally finite direct category
D
, we expect
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
to be isomorphic to (the contextual category presented by) the
cwa

\mathcal{C}^{D^{\text{op}}}
in the sense of
[
26
]
. While our exponent
\mathcal{A}
can be any contextual category, our construction doesn’t generalize theirs since they work with a base
cwa

\mathcal{C}
that is not necessarily contextual. Also, working with
\mathcal{C}(D)
instead of directly with
D
simplifies the presentation at an abstract level at the cost of hiding much of the interesting combinatorics of
D
itself.
7
7

7

This is related to the fact, mentioned above, that we have no canonical embedding
D^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(D)
up to equality of functors. We only do up to canonical isomorphism, which is why using
D
directly requires choosing some additional structure on it, such as a suitable linear order on the arrows of each slice
D/x
. See
[
26
]
, Def. 3.17 and the remark that follows.

Although the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{B}}
is not as direct as that of a bimorphism
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, we believe it can be illuminating to understand how the former, seen as a structure of independent interest, leads to the latter. The connection between the tensor product and the exponentiation is useful in justifying the definition of the tensor product as an appropriate one, while parts of the definition of a bimorphism might seem, at first glance, arbitrary. For instance, the asymmetry between the roles of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in the definition of a bimorphism
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
becomes evident when the latter is viewed as a contextual functor
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
. Because of that, we chose to first introduce
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
, and then define bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
in such a way that these are in one-to-one correspondence with morphisms
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
(Proposition
3.26
).

We also note that the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
spanned by its length-
1
objects — denoted by
\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}})
in §
10
— is isomorphic to the category of contextual functors
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and natural transformations between them.

Poset-shaped cellular diagrams, and multimorphisms

Bimorphisms are, as they are defined, difficult to work with. Note that although a contextual category
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
is characterized, up to isomorphism, by the statement that we have a universal bimorphism
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
, part of the structure of
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
is only captured implicitly by
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
. There are a few instances where this will be relevant in the text:

(1)

We want to recover the symmetry isomorphism
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
from
[
5
]
, §8 in terms of a correspondence between bimorphisms
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and bimorphisms
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. However, this correspondence cannot be expressed purely in terms of the images of
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
and
\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}
. Indeed, for contexts
X
,
Y
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, the isomorphism
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
sends
[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]
to
[\textbf{Y}\otimes\textbf{X}]
up to isomorphism, but in general not strictly. Accordingly, if
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{E}
, then its composite with the canonical isomorphism of categories
\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
is not a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
to
\mathcal{E}
. Thus we need another strategy for transforming bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
into bimorphisms
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
.

(2)

Similarly, constructing an isomorphism
(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})
is equivalent to obtaining a bijection, natural in
\mathcal{E}
, between bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. But these are certain functors of the forms
(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{A}\times(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, respectively, and so cannot be compared directly. To deal with that, the idea is to show that the two kinds of functors correspond, by restriction along
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\times\text{Id}_{C}:(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}
and
\text{Id}_{A}\times\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{A}\times(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\times(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})
, resp., to the same class of functors
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
.

(3)

We want to be able to characterize bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
in terms of suitable generating data of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
. More precisely, if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
) is a
precontextual category
, as discussed below, that is completed into the contextual category
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
) via a reflection morphism
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
), then we expect to have a bijection between bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. But the former are often much easier to describe, and this will be fundamental in the recursive proof that
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
is a universal bimorphism. The point is that if
\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
is an extension by a single axiom, it can be hard to explicitly describe
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D}^{\prime})
in terms of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})
. But it will be straightforward to factorize
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D}^{\prime})
as
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D}^{\prime})
where
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a precontextual category and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D}^{\prime})
is a reflection morphism.

Accomplishing these goals will be possible by working with what we will call “cellular diagrams” whose shape is a finite poset
(P,\leq)
endowed with a linear refinement of its order, that is, a linear order
\vartriangleleft
on
P
such that
a\vartriangleleft b
whenever
a\leq b
. We expect our construction to be obtainable by specializing the Reedy diagrams from
[
26
]
to the case where the domain inverse category is a poset and the codomain
cwa
is a contextual category,
8
8

8

See
[
26
]
, Remark 7.3 for instances and variants of their construction that had been previously considered in the literature.
but we don’t establish a formal correspondence.

As discussed in
[
37
]
, following Makkai’s use of simple categories in the study of first-order logic with dependent sorts (
[
33
]
), there is close connection between locally finite direct categories
9
9

9

A category
D
is
direct
if it has no infinite sequence
\cdots\rightarrow\bullet\rightarrow\bullet\rightarrow\bullet
of non-identity morphisms, and it is
locally finite
if the slice
D/x
is finite for every object
x
. A category is locally finite and direct precisely when its opposite category is simple in the sense of
[
33
]
, or inverse (which includes a finiteness assumption) in the sense of
[
26
]
.
and dependent type signatures. A lfdc
D
defines a contextual category
\mathcal{C}(D)
whose underlying category is, essentially, opposite to the category
\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
of finitely presentable presheaves on
D
.
10
10

10

Although the contextual structure on
\mathcal{C}(D)
, which involves operations on the
set
of objects, cannot be transferred to one on
\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
.
In turn,
\mathcal{C}(D)
can be presented by a
gat

\mathbb{D}
having one sort axiom for each object of
D
; conversely, every
gat
that only has sort axioms arises essentially uniquely, by this process, from a locally finite direct category. Restricting to the case where
D
is a finite poset, we obtain those theories that only have finitely many sort axioms, and such that the context of each axiom lists any sort symbol at most once.

The underlying idea, implicit in Proposition
4.24
, is to try to characterize contextual functors
\mathcal{C}(D)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
for a given contextual category
\mathcal{E}
in terms of their restrictions
D^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
along a functor
I:D^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(D)
such that the composite
D^{\text{op}}\overset{I}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{C}(D)\simeq\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)^{\text{op}}\hookrightarrow\text{PSh}(D)^{\text{op}}
is isomorphic to the Yoneda embedding. Since
\mathscr{Y}:D\rightarrow\text{PSh}(D)
is full-and-faithful and objects of
D
have no non-identity automorphisms, choosing
I
amounts to specifying for each
a\in D
an object of
\mathcal{C}(D)
isomorphic (such an isomorphism being unique) to
\mathscr{Y}(a)
in
\text{PSh}(D)
. Once
I
has been chosen, we would like to characterize when a functor
D\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is “cellular” in the sense that it is the restriction along
I
of a contextual functor
\mathcal{C}(D)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. Doing that when
D
is a finite poset turns out to involve simpler combinatorial data and to be sufficient for the intended applications in the present text, which is why we restrict our attention to this case.

More precisely, for a finite poset
P
, one way to choose
I
is to linearly order the elements of
P
, say as
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
, so that the “boundary”
\partial a_{i}=\{x\in P\mid x<a_{i}\}
is contained in
\{a_{1},...,a_{i-1}\}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Equivalently, we consider a linear refinement
\vartriangleleft
of the given partial order. Restricting
\vartriangleleft
defines a linear order on each
a_{i}^{\leq}=\{x\in P\mid x\leq a_{i}\}
, so the latter now corresponds to an object of
\mathcal{C}(D)
, say
c_{i}
. Sending
a_{i}
to
c_{i}
uniquely extends to a functor
I:P\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(P)
isomorphic to the Yoneda embedding. Note that this is not the only way of choosing
I
– at this point, there need not be any relation between the linear orders on the different
a_{i}^{\leq}
. The reason why we use this strategy is that it gives us methods for

•

determining when a functor
P\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is cellular with respect to
\vartriangleleft
once we know that its restriction
\{a_{1},...,a_{n-1}\}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is cellular with respect to the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
;

•

given linear refinements
\vartriangleleft
,
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
of
P
, modifying in a canonical way a functor
F:P\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
cellular with respect to
\vartriangleleft
to one, say
F^{\prime}
, cellular with respect to
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
and endowed with an isomorphism
F^{\prime}\cong F
.

In a contextual category
\mathcal{A}
, the display maps arrange the set of objects of length
\geq 1
into a tree
T(\mathcal{A})
, and
T(\mathcal{A})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
extends canonically to a contextual functor
\mathcal{C}(T(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
. Thus we expect to have, for contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, a cellular diagram

T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{k})\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}(T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{k}))\cong\mathcal{C}(T(\mathcal{A}_{1}))\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(T(\mathcal{A}_{k}))\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{k}

where the embedding
I
is to be chosen in accordance with the above discussion. Since
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{k})
may not be finite, we don’t choose a linear refinement of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{k})
: it suffices to do that locally, that is, to refine
a_{1}^{\leq}\times\cdots\times a_{k}^{\leq}
for each
a_{1}\in T(A_{1})
, …,
a_{k}\in T(A_{k})
.

This is why having a way of explicitly describing cellular diagrams out of posets will be useful in extending the concept of bimorphism to that of
k
-ary morphism for
k\geq 1
; for example, bimorphisms can be recovered by taking
k=2
and characterizing cellular maps when
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times T(\mathcal{A}_{2})
is (locally) equipped with the lexicographic order. Our toolkit on poset-shaped cellular diagrams will help in addressing (1) above due to the possibility of switching between different linear refinements of a poset (the “reverse” lexicographic order on
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times T(\mathcal{A}_{2})
can be used to express the symmetry of the tensor product), as well as (2) due to the associativity of the lexicographic product.

Precontextual categories

Many of our constructions use what we will call
precontextual categories
. They will be categories equipped with a length function on objects, a chosen arrow (display map) out of each object of length
\geq 1
, and a class of commutative squares (distinguished squares); but, unlike for contextual categories, we impose a minimal list of axioms for this extra structure. This will be a convenient framework for obtaining contextual categories that have a desired universal property, which is sometimes difficult using
gat
s.

In fact, (small) precontextual categories will be organized into a locally presentable category
Precont
having
Cont
as a reflective subcategory such that the inclusion functor is
\omega
-accessible, i.e. it preserves filtered colimits. For example, it will be straightforward to describe the precontextual category
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}^{\text{pre}}
freely generated by the following data: a length-
m
object
a
, a length-
n
object
b
, and and a morphism
f:a\rightarrow b
. Applying the reflection functor
L:\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
to it yields a contextual category
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
which is, again, free on the above data. In the syntax of
gat
s, specifying
f
would lead us to introduce
n
many term symbols whose sorts are defined recursively. This kind of procedure can become unwieldy if the generating data involve several morphisms and relations involving them. Note that while in
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}^{\text{pre}}
the arrow
f
is a primitive structure, after passing to the contextual category
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
we gain access to a canonical decomposition of
f
as a sequence of sections of display maps.

Organization of the text

The text is structured as follows:

•

In Section 2, we recall the definitions of a contextual category, of a category with attributes (
cwa
), and of the categories of those —
Cont
and
Att
. We also describe, following
[
25
]
, the coreflection functor
\text{Att}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
, which will play a major role in the text. We also discuss precontextual categories and the category of these,
Precont
. The exposition is set up so that contextual categories are defined as precontextual categories satisfying further conditions, and
Cont
is the corresponding full subcategory of
Precont
. We also check that
\text{Cont}\hookrightarrow\text{Precont}
is an
\omega
-accessible right adjoint functor.

•

In Section 3, we define exponentiation and bimorphisms of (pre)contextual categories, and discuss some instances of these constructions.

Let
\mathbf{Cont}
be the strict
2
-category obtained from
Cont
by taking the natural transformations between contextual functors as
2
-cells; we verify in §
3.2
that
\mathbf{Cont}
has
Cat
-powers and that these are examples of exponentials as previously introduced. We also provide an alternative description, based on a notion of family-valued model relative to a set-valued functor, of
\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}
where
\mathcal{A}\in\text{Cont}
and
Fam
is the contextual category of iterated families of sets from
[
12
]
.

We verify in §
3.5
that for
gat
s
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, the functor
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
is a bimorphism. This example is central to connecting the present text with
[
5
]
, and it can be useful for familiarizing oneself with the definition of a bimorphism, especially the aspects that involve the strictness of the data in a contextual category.

•

In Section 4, we define and study what we will call (poset-shaped) cellular diagrams. These will be functors
P^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where
P
is a suitably structured locally finite poset and
\mathcal{C}
is a contextual category, that can be used to assign display maps (resp. objects) in
\mathcal{C}
to elements (resp. sieves) of
P
with objects in a particular way.

•

In Section 5, we define multimorphisms
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
from a tuple of precontextual categories to a contextual category, and prove several results about those.

In §
5.3
we describe a process for modifying a multimorphism out of
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to one out of
(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n})
for a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, and introduce certain concepts related to this construction — shuffling diagrams, permutative morphisms — that will be useful later. In §
5.4
we check that multimorphisms
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
coincide with the previously-introduced bimorphisms. In §
5.5
we construct (see Theorem
5.31
) a (natural) isomorphism
\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
where
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
are precontextual categories and
\mathcal{C}
is a contextual category. The main results of §6 and §7 are proved by combining Theorem
5.31
with properties of the structures from §
5.3
. In §
5.6
, we prove that exponentials and sets of multimorphisms are invariant under replacing any precontextual category among the arguments by its image under the reflection functor
\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
.

•

In Section 6, multimorphisms whose source is a tuple of contextual categories are proved to be closed under (multi)composition, and we use this to obtain a multicategory
\mathscr{Cont}
of contextual categories and multimorphisms between them.

•

In Section 7, we realize
Cont
as a (closed) symmetric monoidal category. We start by proving the existence of binary tensor products, that is, of a universal bimorphism out of any given pair of contextual categories (Construction
7.3
). Our approach is based on the functor
L:\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
and on the representability of functors of poset-shaped cellular diagrams (Proposition
4.24
), and it does not rely on the tensor product of
gat
s defined in
[
5
]
. We then use this to prove the existence of
n
-ary tensor products for all
n\geq 1
(Proposition
7.5
). After that, we lift
\otimes:\text{Cont}\times\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
to a symmetric monoidal structure by constructing natural isomorphisms
\alpha
,
\lambda
,
\rho
,
\beta
and proving that they satisfy the required coherence identities.

•

In Section 8 we describe the pushout-tensor maps associated with pairs of contextual functors
(F,G)
of a special kind: each of
F
,
G
encodes in a universal way one of the kinds of axioms for generalized algebraic theories.

•

In Section 9, we use the description from §8 to prove that the functor
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
from
[
5
]
is a universal bimorphism, which allows us to conclude that the monoidal structure on
GAT
obtained by transferring the one on
Cont
along
\text{Cont}\simeq\text{GAT}
is compatible with the tensor product from
[
5
]
.

•

In Section 10, which can be seen as a continuation of §
3.2
, we prove that the
Cat
-enrichment and the symmetric monoidal structure on
Cont
are, in a certain sense, compatible.

I am extremely grateful to Simon Henry, my doctoral advisor, who introduced me to this topic, helped me navigate it through countless and insightful discussions, and provided valuable feedback on this text.

2
A brief overview of (pre)contextual categories and categories with attributes

In this section, we will recall the definitions of a contextual category and of a category with attributes, fix some notation and terminology, and, along the way, introduce what we will call precontextual categories.

2.1
(Pre)contextual categories

Contextual categories were introduced in J. Cartmell’s doctoral thesis
[
11
]
(see also
[
12
]
, §14) in order to capture the aspects of the syntax of generalized algebraic theories that are essential to formulate their semantics. For this reason, as remarked by Cartmell, contextual categories can be viewed as providing an
algebraic semantics
for generalized algebraic theories.

A generalized algebraic theory
\mathbb{A}
has a
syntactic category
,
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
, whose objects (resp. morphisms) are equivalence classes, with respect to derivable equality, of contexts (resp. of realizations between contexts
11
11

11

In
[
5
]
we referred to these also as
morphisms
; so in the terminology of that article, given contexts
X
,
Y
in
\mathbb{A}
, a morphism
[\textbf{X}]\rightarrow[\textbf{Y}]
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
is an equivalence class of morphisms
\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
.
) in
\mathbb{A}
. The category
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
inherits extra structure from
\mathbb{A}
: each object has a
length
, defined as the length of any context in the given equivalence class; each object of length
\geq 1
has a distinguished projection map out of it — a
display map
— that corresponds to removing from a non-empty context its last variable; display maps have specified and strictly functorial pullbacks along arbitrary morphisms, corresponding to substitution of sorts and terms along arbitrary realizations. A
contextual category
is then defined as a category equipped with the above kinds of additional structure.

Morphisms of contextual categories
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})
— which, by definition, are the functors that preserve all the additional structure strictly, including the specified length of objects — correspond bijectively to morphisms
\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
. Also, every contextual category is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
for some
gat

\mathbb{A}
. It follows that the syntactic category construction defines an equivalence
\text{GAT}\simeq\text{Cont}
between the category of
gat
s and that of contextual categories; this is one of the central results from
[
11
]
. We refer the reader to Cartmell’s work for the details of this correspondence.

As mentioned in the introduction, precontextual categories will be used to present contextual categories having certain universal properties of interest; informally, they can be thought of as a variant of contextual categories in which substitution is only partially (and possibly ambiguously) specified. As we will see, the category
Precont
of precontextual categories is an
\omega
-orthogonality class (see
[
1
]
) in
Cont
, from which will follow that the full-and-faithful inclusion
\text{Cont}\subset\text{Precont}
is an accessible right adjoint functor.

Convention 2.1
.

We work within ZFC set theory with, additionally, an uncountable Grothendieck universe
\mathscr{U}
. We say that a set
X
is
small
if
X\in\mathscr{U}
. Specifically when discussing the family-valued semantics of contextual categories, we will suppose given another universe,
\mathscr{U}^{+}
, such that
\mathscr{U}\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
.

For us, a category
C
will have, by definition, a set
\text{Ob}(C)
and a set
\text{Ar}(C)
of morphisms. We say that it is
small
if
\text{Ob}(C)
and
\text{Ar}(C)
are both small. When this causes no ambiguity, we abbreviate
a\in\text{Ob}(C)
as
a\in C
. We write
\mathbf{Ar}(C)
for the category of arrows of
C
.

We write
B^{A}
for the category of functors
A\rightarrow B
and natural transformations between them. The set of functors
\text{Ob}(B^{A})
will be denoted by
\text{Fun}(A,B)
.

For
n\geq 0
, we write
[n]
for the ordered set
\{0\leq 1\leq\cdots\leq n\}
, as well as for the corresponding category.

We denote by
Set
the category having as objects the small sets, and as morphisms the functions between those.

Definition 2.2
.

A
precontextual category
is a tuple
\mathcal{C}=(C,\ell,\partial,\textbf{p},\textbf{Q})
consisting of:

(i)

A category
C
, which we call the
underlying category
of
\mathcal{C}
.

(ii)

A function
\ell:\text{Ob}(C)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. We refer to
\ell(a)
as the
length
of
a
.

(iii)

A function
\partial:\text{Ob}(C)\rightarrow\text{Ob}(C)
such that
\ell(\partial a)=\ell(a)-1
if
\ell(a)\geq 1
, and
\partial(a)=a
if
\ell(a)=0
.

(iv)

For each
a\in C
of length
\geq 1
, a morphism
\textbf{p}_{a}:a\rightarrow\partial a
.

A finite composite of arrows of this form will be referred to as a
display map
. Note that for all
a\in C
and
n\leq\ell(a)
, there exists a unique display map of the form
a\rightarrow b
with
\ell(b)=n
.

We use the notation
a\twoheadrightarrow b
to indicate that a morphism is a display map. This will be convenient since, in this case, we can identify the arrow without naming it: by the above remark, for fixed
a
,
b
, there exists at most one display map from
a
to
b
.

The
length
of
p:a\twoheadrightarrow b
, for which we write
\ell(p)
by abuse of notation, is defined as
\ell(a)-\ell(b)
.

(v)

A set
Q
of commutative squares in
C
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

A
distinguished square
is a finite vertical composite of squares in
Q
. In particular, every distinguished square is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
a\twoheadrightarrow a^{\prime}
and
b\twoheadrightarrow b^{\prime}
are display maps of the same length
n\geq 0
. We refer to
n
as the
length
of (
*
). Note that the length-
1
distinguished squares are precisely the elements of
Q
.

We say that
\mathcal{C}
is a
contextual category
12
12

12

The concept was introduced in
[
11
]
. More recently, contextual categories also became known as
C-systems
following Voevodsky’s work on the subject; see
[
38
]
for an explanation for the suggested change in terminology.
if, in addition, it has the following properties:

(vi)

It has a unique object of length
0
, and it is a terminal object.

We call it the
distinguished terminal object
and denote it by
1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

(vii)

Every element of
Q
is a pullback square.

This implies, by the pasting law for pullbacks, that every distinguished square is a pullback square.

(viii)

Q
forms a category (whose objects are the length-
1
display maps) under horizontal composition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\textbf{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\textbf{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{a}}

is in
Q
for all
a
of length
\geq 1
, and if the left and right squares in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

are in
Q
, then so is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial c.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}

Note that, as a consequence, the set of all distinguished squares is closed under (vertical and) horizontal composition.

(ix)

For every diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

there exists a unique element of
Q
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

As long as this causes no ambiguity, when dealing with two or more (pre)contextual categories we will abuse notation and write
\ell
,
\partial
,
p
,
Q
for the corresponding structures in all of them. The underlying category of
\mathcal{C}
(i.e.
C
in the above definition) will be denoted by
|\mathcal{C}|
.

For precontextual categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, say with underlying categories
C
and
D
, respectively, we define a
morphism
or
contextual functor
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
as a functor
F:C\rightarrow D
that (strictly) preserves all the additional structure: it commutes with
\ell
,
\partial
and
p
, and the induced function on commutative squares sends elements of
Q
to elements of
Q
. We compose morphisms as usual for functors.

We say that a (pre)contextual category is
small
if its underlying category is small.

We denote by
Precont
the category whose objects are the small precontextual categories and whose morphisms are the ones defined above. We let
Cont
be the full subcategory of
Precont
whose objects are the small contextual categories. It is immediate that
Cont
is isomorphic to the category of contextual categories considered in
[
11
]
.

By taking natural transformations as
2
-cells, we have strict
2
-categories
\mathbf{Precont}
and
\mathbf{Cont}
.

13

13

See Remark
2.15
for a discussion of this definition.

Remark 2.3
.

It is possible to prove, in a standard but laborious way, that
Precont
is the category of
Set
-valued models of a finite-limit sketch, hence a locally finitely presentable category (see
[
1
]
). The idea is to use a sketch whose underlying graph has, as a first step, a vertex
o_{n}
for each
n\geq 0
, to be interpreted as the set of length-
n
objects, and a vertex
a_{m,n}
for each
m
,
n\geq 0
, to be interpreted as the set of arrows from a length-
m
object to a length-
n
one. One then encodes the remaining structure separately for each tuple of lengths. For example,
\partial
is encoded by an edge
o_{n}\rightarrow o_{n-1}
for each
n\geq 1
, co/domains by edges
a_{m,n}\rightarrow o_{m}
and
a_{m,n}\rightarrow o_{n}
, and composition by edges
a_{m,n}\times_{o_{n}}a_{n,p}\rightarrow a_{m,p}
, where
a_{m,n}\times_{o_{n}}a_{n,p}
is a new vertex to be interpreted as a pullback in the standard way.

Definition 2.4
.

Recall that in a category
A
, an object
a
is said to be
orthogonal
to a morphism
i:b\rightarrow b^{\prime}
if for every
g:b\rightarrow a
, there exists a unique
g^{\prime}:b^{\prime}\rightarrow a
such that
g^{\prime}\circ i=g
.

For a set
M
of morphisms in
A
, we write
M^{\perp}
for the full subcategory of
A
spanned by all objects that are orthogonal to every element of
M
.

An
orthogonality class
in
A
is a full subcategory of the form
M^{\perp}
for some set of morphisms
M
. We say that it is a
\kappa
-orthogonality class
for a regular cardinal
\kappa
if
M
can be taken as a set of morphisms whose domain and codomain are
\kappa
-presentable.

Proposition 2.5

(
[
1
]
, Theorem 1.39)
.

Let
A
be a locally
\kappa
-presentable category. If
B\subset A
is a
\kappa
-orthogonality class, then
B
is locally
\kappa
-presentable, and the inclusion functor
B\rightarrow A
is
\kappa
-accessible (that is, it preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits) and has a left adjoint.

Proposition 2.6
.

The full subcategory
\text{Cont}\subset\text{Precont}
is an
\omega
-orthogonality class.

We defer its proof to the end of this section.

Notation 2.7
.

We let
L:\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
be a left adjoint of the inclusion
\text{Cont}\hookrightarrow\text{Precont}
.

It is often very difficult to describe explicitly the effect of the reflection functor
L:\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Precont}
on a given precontextual category; so throughout the text, this process usually allows us to conclude that a functor
\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
is representable without having a full description of a representing object — that is, we won’t be able to characterize maps
to
a representing object. This applies, for example, to the construction of the tensor product of two contextual categories; the syntactic approach from
[
5
]
addresses this shortcoming by employing a different method and language, and so can be seen as complementary to the one from the present text.

Remark 2.8
.

We will prove in Corollary
5.33
that
L
lifts to a strict
2
-adjoint of
\mathbf{Cont}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Precont}
.
14
14

14

This uses in an essential way (which is not the case for the
1
-categorical adjunction) that a precontextual category
\mathcal{C}
has an underlying tree of display maps rather than just a length function
\text{Ob}(|\mathcal{C}|)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and a set arrows specified as display maps.

2.1.1
Colimits of precontextual categories, an example, and the proof of Proposition
2.6

Remark 2.9

(Colimits of precontextual categories)
.

Consider a diagram
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
in
Precont
where
I
is a small category. Let
(\varphi_{i}:|A_{i}|\rightarrow C)
be a colimit cocone of the underlying diagram in
Cat
. Since
\text{Ob}:\text{Cat}\rightarrow\text{Set}
preserves colimits, objects of
C
correspond bijectively to equivalence classes of the smallest equivalence relation on
\coprod_{i\in I}\text{Ob}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
with
(i,a)\sim(j,b)
whenever there exists
f:i\rightarrow j
such that the induced morphism
A_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{j}
sends
a
to
b
.
But note that

•

as contextual functors preserve length, if
(i,a)\sim(j,b)
, then
\ell_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(a)=\ell_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(b)
;

•

as contextual functors preserve display maps, if
(i,a)\sim(j,b)
with
\ell_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(a)\geq 1
, then
\varphi_{i}(\textbf{p}_{a})=\varphi_{j}(\textbf{p}_{b})
.

This implies that we have a precontextual category
\mathcal{C}
with underlying category
C
and such that

•

for
i\in I
and
a\in|\mathcal{A}_{i}|
, we have
\ell_{\mathcal{B}}(\varphi_{i}(a))=\ell_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(a)
; if
\ell_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(a)\geq 1
, then
\textbf{p}_{\varphi_{i}(a)}=\varphi_{i}(\textbf{p}_{a})
;

•

distinguished squares in
\mathcal{C}
are the images of distinguished squares under the functors
\varphi_{i}
.

Given a cone
(K_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{D})_{i\in I}
in
Precont
, there exists a unique functor
K^{\prime}:|C|\rightarrow|\mathcal{D}|
such that
K^{\prime}\circ\varphi_{i}=K_{i}
for all
i
; it is immediate from the definition of
\mathcal{C}
that
F
defines a morphism
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
. We conclude that
(\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C})
is a colimit cocone in
Precont
.

Example 2.10
.

For
n\geq 0
, we let
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
be the precontextual category whose underlying category is a linearly ordered set with
n+1
elements, which we write as

o_{n}\rightarrow o_{n-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow o_{1}\rightarrow o_{0},

and where:
\ell(o_{i})=i
;
\partial o_{i}=o_{i-1}
with
\textbf{p}_{o_{i}}
the unique arrow
o_{i}\rightarrow o_{i-1}
; and
Q
is empty. We denote
L(\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}})
by
\mathcal{O}_{n}
.

Note that
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
(resp.
\mathcal{O}_{n}
) represents the functor
\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
(resp.
\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
) that sends (pre)contextual category to its set of length-
n
objecs. The same functor is represented by the syntactic category of the
gat

\mathbb{O}_{n}
given by

\displaystyle\vdash O_{1}\;\mathsf{sort}

\displaystyle x_{1}:O_{1}

\displaystyle\vdash O_{2}(x_{1})\;\mathsf{sort}

\displaystyle x_{1}:O_{1},...,x_{n-1}:O_{n-1}(x_{1},...,x_{n-2})

\displaystyle\vdash O_{n}(x_{1},...,x_{n-1})\;\mathsf{sort},

so the Yoneda lemma yields an isomorphism
\mathbb{O}_{n}\cong\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{O}_{n})
.

In what follows, we consider certain precontextual categories obtained by adding further structure to some coproduct such as
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{p}^{\text{pre}}
. We then write
o_{i}
,
o^{\prime}_{i}
,
o^{\prime\prime}_{i}
for the objects of
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{p}^{\text{pre}}
, respectively, and analogously in similar cases.

2.2
Categories with attributes

Categories with attributes —
cwa
s for short — are a useful generalization of contextual categories. Similarly to the latter, a
cwa
has a set of types over each object, arranged contravariantly so as to encode substitution, and each type
U
over an object
X
has an associated “projection” or “display map”
X.U\twoheadrightarrow X
thought of as adjoining to
X
an element or free variable of type
U
. However, an object
X
of a
cwa
might fail to fit uniquely into a sequence of display maps
X=X_{n}\twoheadrightarrow X_{n-1}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow X_{0}=1
where
1
is a previously chosen terminal object. That is, such a decomposition may not exist, or there may be several of them, possibly of different lengths. Also, we can have
X.U=X.V
for two distinct types
U
,
V
over
X
.

Definition 2.11
.

A
category with attributes
15
15

15

The definition we use, which is common in the literature, differs slightly from the one given in
[
11
]
, where the presence of dependence sums is also required.
is a tuple
\mathcal{C}=(C,1,\text{Ty},R)
consisting of a category
C
, a terminal object
1\in C
, a presheaf (of
types
)
\text{Ty}:C^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}
16
16

16

It can be useful, for example when studying the semantics of
cwa
s or contextual categories, to consider a presheaf taking values in a larger universe of sets. For simplicity, we will use the same terminology when talking about such variants.
, and a functor
R:\int\text{Ty}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ar}(C)
such that:

•

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\text{Ty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Ar}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
cod

where
P
is the canonical projection from the category of elements of
R
and
cod
is the codomain projection, commutes strictly.

•

Every morphism in
\int\text{Ty}
is sent by
R
to a cartesian square in
C
.

A
morphism
from
(C,1,\text{Ty},R)
to
(C^{\prime},1^{\prime},\text{Ty}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is a pair
(F,\phi)
consisting of a functor
F:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
and a natural transformation
\phi:\text{Ty}\Rightarrow\text{Ty}^{\prime}\circ F
such that, letting
\overline{\phi}:\int\text{Ty}\rightarrow\int\text{Ty}^{\prime}
be the induced functor, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int\text{Ty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int\text{Ty}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ar}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ar}(C^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Ar}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}}

strictly commutes. Composition of morphisms is defined by
(G,\gamma)\circ(F,\phi)=(G\circ F,\;\gamma F\circ\phi)
.

We denote by
Att
the category of small
cwa
s (ones whose base category is small) and morphisms between them.

Following standard notation (see e.g.
[
25
]
, Definition 4.1), for
X\in C
and
U\in\text{Ty}(X)
we write
R(X,U)
as
p_{U}:X.U\rightarrow X
. This matches how
cwa
s are used to model dependent type theory: we think of
X.U
as being obtained from
X
by adjoining a free variable of type
U
, with
p_{U}
the corresponding projection/display map. Now, the image under
R
of an arrow
f:(Y,f^{*}U)\rightarrow(X,U)
in
\int\text{Ty}
takes the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y.f^{*}U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{f^{*}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Construction 2.12

(From contextual categories to
cwa
s and back)
.

A contextual category
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to a category with attributes
\text{att}(\mathcal{C})=(|\mathcal{C}|,1_{\mathcal{C}},\text{Ty},R)
where:

•

\text{Ty}:|\mathcal{C}|^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}
sends
a\in C
to
\{a^{\prime}\in C\mid\partial a^{\prime}=a\}
and, for an arrow
f:b\rightarrow a^{\prime}
, the map
R(f)
sends
a^{\prime}
to the unique
b^{\prime}
fitting into a distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Ty}(f)}

Hence
\int\text{Ty}
is canonically isomorphic to the category of length-
1
distinguished squares from Definition
2.2
(viii).

•

In terms of the above isomorphism,
R
sends each length-
1
distinguished square to itself viewed as an arrow in
\mathbf{Ar}(|\mathcal{C}|)
.

Conversely, a
cwa

\mathcal{C}=(C,1,\text{Ty},R)
gives rise to a contextual category
\text{cont}(\mathcal{C})
defined as follows:

•

For
n\geq 0
, its length-
n
objects are the sequences
((X_{0},U_{0}),...,(X_{n-1},U_{n-1}))
of objects of
\int\text{Ty}
such that
X_{0}=1
and
X_{i}=X_{i-1}.U_{i-1}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
. (Thus
X_{i}=1.U_{0}.\cdots.U_{i-1}
.)

Morphisms and their composition are induced from
\mathcal{C}
via

\text{Hom}_{\text{cont}(\mathcal{C})}((X_{i},U_{i})_{i<m},\;(Y_{i},V_{i})_{i<n}):=\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X_{m-1}.U_{m-1},\;Y_{n-1}.V_{n-1}).

Note that we then have a full-and-faithful functor
U_{\mathcal{C}}:\text{cont}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

•

For
n\geq 1
, we let
\partial((X_{i},U_{i})_{i<n})=(X_{i},U_{i})_{i<{n-1}}
with corresponding display map characterized by
U(\textbf{p}_{(X_{i},U_{i})_{i<n}})=p_{U_{n-1}}:X_{n-1}.U_{n-1}\rightarrow X_{n-1}
. Distinguished squares are defined similarly using the functor
R
.

Note that
U_{\mathcal{C}}
is a morphism
\text{att}(\text{cont}(\mathcal{C}))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, the above constructions extend into functors

\text{att}:\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Att},\qquad\quad\text{cont}:\text{Att}\rightarrow\text{Cont},

and
\text{cont}\circ\text{att}
is isomorphic to the identity functor. The following result can be obtained by a routine calculation:

Proposition 2.13

(
[
25
]
, Proposition 4.4)
.

The functor
att
is full-and-faithful, and we have an adjunction
\text{att}\dashv\text{cont}
with counit components
U_{\mathcal{C}}:\text{att}(\text{cont}(\mathcal{C}))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Explicitly, the map
\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{D},\text{cont}(\mathcal{C}))\rightarrow\text{Hom}_{\text{Att}}(\text{att}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{C})
given by
F\mapsto U_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\text{att}(F)
is a bijection natural in
\mathcal{D}\in\text{Cont}^{\text{op}}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Att}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Taking natural transformations as
2
-cells, we have a strict
2
-category
\mathbf{Att}
.

Since
\text{att}:\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Att}
commutes with taking underlying categories/functors, it extends into a
2
-full-and-faithful strict
2
-functor
\textbf{att}:\mathbf{Cont}\rightarrow\mathbf{Att}
. Similarly, we have a strict
2
-functor
\textbf{cont}:\mathbf{Att}\rightarrow\mathbf{Cont}
.

In the notation of Proposition
2.13
, the fact that
U_{\mathcal{C}}
is full-and-faithful implies that we actually have a
2
-natural isomorphism
\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{D},\text{cont}(\mathcal{C}))\cong\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Att}}(\text{att}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{C})
, hence a strict
2
-adjunction
\textbf{att}\dashv\textbf{cont}
.

Remark 2.15
.

[
3
]
describes in Def. 5 (based on
[
13
]
) a
2
-category
CompCat
of comprehension categories (in the sense of
[
23
]
), pseudomorphisms, and transformations. Viewing
cwa
s as comprehension categories whose underlying fibration is discrete, we obtain (essentially) a full sub-
2
-category of
CompCat
, which is denoted in that article by
\textbf{CwA}^{ps}
(see Def. 37 and Prop. 38). The latter differs from the
2
-category
\mathbf{Att}
considered in the present paper in two ways: morphisms in
\mathbf{Att}
preserve all available structure strictly, rather than up to coherent isomorphism; and
2
-cells in
\mathbf{Att}
are arbitrary natural transformations, whereas
\textbf{CwA}^{ps}
only keeps those that are suitably compatible with base change of types in the codomain.

As explained in
[
3
]
, Prop. 46, the full sub-
2
-category of
\textbf{CwA}^{ps}
spanned by the contextual
cwa
s (those in the essential image of
\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Att}
) is actually equivalent to the
1
-category
Cont
: between contextual categories, every pseudofunctor is isomorphic to a strict one, and there exists at most one
2
-cell between two given pseudofunctors. For
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
and
F
,
G\in\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, a natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow G
defines a morphism in
\textbf{CwA}^{ps}
if and only if for every
a\in\text{Ob}(\mathcal{A})
of length
\geq 1
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\partial a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a}}

is distinguished. But this implies, as can be checked by induction on the length of
a
, that
\eta
is an identity natural transformation.
17
17

17

This fact can be viewed in an alternative way using constructions and results discussed later in the paper: recalling that
[1]=\{0<1\}
, let
[1]^{\text{dist}}_{\text{pre}}
be the precontextual category obtained from
[1]_{\text{pre}}
(see Proposition
3.17
) by regarding

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

as a distinguished square. By Proposition
5.34
, using that
L([1]_{\text{pre}}^{\text{dist}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{1}\cong L(\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}})
, we have a bijection between bimorphisms
\mathcal{A}\times[1]^{\text{dist}}_{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
and bimorphisms
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
. The latter correspond to contextual functors
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
, and the former to pairs of contextual functors equipped with a natural transformation such that the naturality squares of all length-
1
display maps are distinguished.

3
Exponentiation and bimorphisms

3.1
Exponentiation of contextual categories

We will now define the exponential
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
of two contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{C}
. In fact, we will construct a certain category with attributes
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
and then let
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}=\text{cont}(D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category. Suppose given a commutative square
Q:[1]^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

i.e. such that
p
and
p^{\prime}
are display maps (of any length). Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be such that the bottom right square is distinguished and
g
is the unique arrow making the diagram commute. We call
g
the
gap map
of
Q
. We say that
Q
is a
relative length-
1
display map
if
g
is a length-
1
display map.

Remark 3.2
.

•

If
Q
as above is a relative length-
1
display map, then
\ell(p^{\prime})=\ell(p)+1
. Also, if
f
is an identity arrow, then
Q
is a relative length-
1
display map precisely when
f^{\prime}
is a length-
1
display map.

•

If
Q
,
Q^{\prime}:[1]^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
are relative length-
1
display maps, then a natural transformation
\alpha:Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
induces, by functoriality of limits, a morphism
\alpha_{*}:g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{Ar}(\mathcal{C})
between the respective gap maps.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a precontextual category and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category. Given functors
F
,
G:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{C}|
, we will say that a natural transformation
\pi:G\Rightarrow F
is an
indexed sort
if it has the following properties:
18
18

18

Indexed sorts play a role analogous to that of strict Reedy types in
[
26
]
.

IS(i)

For every length-
1
display map
p:a\rightarrow\partial a
in
\mathcal{A}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\partial a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}

is a relative length-
1
display map.

IS(ii)

Given a length-
1
distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{A}
, let
p:G(a)\twoheadrightarrow x
be the gap map of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\partial a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\partial a),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}

and
q:G(b)\twoheadrightarrow y
that of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\partial b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\partial b).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\textbf{p}_{b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\textbf{p}_{b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial b}}

Then the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where the bottom arrow is induced by
\pi
, is distinguished.

Remark 3.4
.

x\rightarrow y
is the unique dashed arrow making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\partial a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\textbf{p}_{b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\textbf{p}_{a})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}

Example 3.5
.

If
F:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{C}|
is the constant functor on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
, then
!:G\Rightarrow F
is an indexed sort if and only if
G
is a contextual functor from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Notation 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a precontextual category and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category with underlying categories
A
and
C
, respectively. We will denote by
D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
the full subcategory of the functor category
C^{A}
spanned by the functors that preserve display maps (we do not require that their lengths be preserved) and send every length-
0
object of
\mathcal{A}
to
1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

The following will be useful later when we discuss bimorphisms of contextual categories:

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a precontextual category and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category. A
flexible morphism
from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
that preserves display maps, distinguished squares, and sends every length-
0
object to
1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

3.1.1
Exponentials as categories with attributes

We will now describe, given a precontextual category
\mathcal{A}
and a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, a
cwa
structure
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
on the category
D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
as in Notation
3.6
. The exponential
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
will then be defined as
\text{cont}(D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))
. In §
3.4
, we will define bimorphisms in such a way that we have a natural bijection

\{\text{bimorphisms }(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\}\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Att}}(\text{att}(\mathcal{B}),D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}).

Let
A
,
C
be the underlying categories of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{C}
, respectively.

Construction 3.8

(Pullback of an indexed sort along a natural transformation)
.

For a functor

, with codomain

can be made into a functor

. Suppose that we have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}

Definition 3.9
.

A diagram of natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

obtained as above will be called an
indexed distinguished square
.

Remark 3.10
.

It can be verified inductively that indexed distinguished squares admit the following characterization: given an indexed sort
\pi:G\rightarrow F
and a natural transformation
\alpha:F^{\prime}\rightarrow F
where
F
,
G
,

, there exists a unique triple
(G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
– which is then constructed as above – as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

such that

IDS(i)

\pi^{\prime}
is an indexed sort.

IDS(ii)

Let
p:a\rightarrow\partial a
be a length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{A}
. Let
g:G(a)\rightarrow x
and

be the gap maps of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\partial a)}}
