{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(\partial a),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\partial a}}

respectively. Then the canonical square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is distinguished.

It follows from this description than for a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

in
D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
, if the left and right squares are indexed distinguished squares, then so is the composite square.

This allows us to extend, as desired, the assignment
F\mapsto E(F)
for
F\in D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
to a functor
E:D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}
. Its category of elements
\int E
is then canonically isomorphic to the category whose objects are the indexed sorts and whose morphisms are the indexed distinguished squares, with composition performed horizontally as indicated above.

We then have a category with attributes, which will be denoted by
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
, defined by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Ar}(D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
cod

where:
P:\int E\rightarrow D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
is the canonical projection from the category of elements; the functor
\int E\rightarrow\mathbf{Ar}(D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))
maps an object, resp. arrow, to its corresponding indexed sort, resp. indexed distinguished square; and the constant functor with value
1_{\mathcal{C}}
is taken as the distinguished terminal object.

3.1.2
Exponential contextual categories

Definition 3.11
.

The
exponential
between a precontextual category
\mathcal{A}
and a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, which will be denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
, is defined as the contextual category obtained by applying the coreflection functor
\text{cont}:\text{Att}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
to the category with attributes
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
constructed above.

By construction, length-
n
objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
correspond bijectively to diagrams

F_{n}\rightarrow F_{n-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow F_{1}\rightarrow F_{0}

in the functor category
C^{A}
such that
F_{0}
is constant on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
and each
F_{i}\rightarrow F_{i-1}
is an indexed sort. Under this translation, morphisms in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
from
(F_{i})_{i\leq n}
to
(G_{i})_{i\leq m}
correspond to natural transformations
F_{n}\rightarrow G_{m}
.

Notation 3.12
.

The above observation defines a “top level” functor
|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}|\rightarrow C^{A}
. We will denote its action on objects and morphisms by the subscript
\varheartsuit
:

(F_{i})_{i\leq n}\mapsto F_{\varheartsuit}:=F_{n},\qquad(\eta:(F_{i})_{i\leq n}\rightarrow(G_{i})_{i\leq m})\mapsto\eta_{\varheartsuit}:=(\eta:F_{n}\rightarrow G_{m}).

This functor is precisely the composite

\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}=\text{cont}(D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))\xrightarrow{U_{D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})}}D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow C^{A}

where
U_{D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})}
is as in Construction
2.12
.

Remark 3.13
.

It follows from Example
3.5
that the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
spanned by its length-
1
objects is isomorphic to the category of contextual functors
\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Precont}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
.

3.2
Cat
-powers of contextual categories

Recall that the category of (small) contextual categories,
Cont
, underlies a strict
2
-category
\mathbf{Cont}
in which the
2
-cells are the natural transformations between contextual functors. From now on, we drop the subscript and write just
\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
for the category of morphisms from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
.

We will now construct, for a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
and a category
A
, a contextual category
\mathcal{C}^{A}
with isomorphisms

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{A})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})^{A}

natural in
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}^{\text{op}}
. After giving a direct description of
\mathcal{C}^{A}
, we will remark how it can be constructed as an exponential, in the sense of Definition
3.11
, between
\mathcal{C}
and a precontextual category.

Writing
C
for the underlying category of
\mathcal{C}
, we will equip the functor category
C^{A}
with a structure of
cwa
. For each functor
F:A\rightarrow C
, let
E(F)
be the set of natural transformations
\pi:G\rightarrow F
such that
\pi_{a}
is a length-
1
display map for each
a\in A
. The assignment
F\mapsto E(F)
extends into a functor
E:(C^{A})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}
in the following way: given
\pi:G\rightarrow F
in
E(F)
and a natural transformation
\alpha:F^{\prime}\rightarrow F
, we let
E(\alpha)(\pi)
be the natural transformation
\pi^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\rightarrow F^{\prime}
as in the unique pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

whose diagram of
a
-components is a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}
for all
a\in A
. Since distinguished pullbacks are strictly functorial,
E
preserves composition and identities.

Definition 3.14
.

Denote by
\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
the category with attributes given by the above construction with distinguished terminal object the functor
A\rightarrow C
constant on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
. We define
\mathcal{C}^{A}
as the contextual category
\text{cont}(\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C}))
.

Remark 3.15
.

Using the definition of
\text{cont}:\text{Att}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
, we can explicitly describe
\mathcal{C}^{A}
as follows:

(i)

For
n\geq 0
, its length-
n
objects are sequences
(F_{0},...,F_{n})
of functors
A\rightarrow C
such that

•

F_{0}
is the constant functor with value
1_{\mathcal{C}}
, and

•

for
1\leq i\leq n
, we have that
\partial(F_{i}(a))=F_{i-1}(a)
and the family of display maps
(F_{i}(a)\twoheadrightarrow F_{i-1}(a))_{a\in A}
is a natural transformation
F_{n}\rightarrow F_{n-1}
.

Alternatively, length-
n
objects of
\text{cont}(\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C}))
correspond to morphisms
A\rightarrow\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}},\mathcal{C})
.

(ii)

Morphisms from
(F_{0},...,F_{m})
to
(G_{0},...,G_{n})
are the natural transformations from
F_{m}
to
G_{n}
.

(iii)

For
n\geq 1
, the predecessor of an object
(F_{0},...,F_{n})
is
(F_{0},...,F_{n-1})
, and the corresponding length-
1
display map is the natural transformation
F_{n}\rightarrow F_{n-1}
whose components are length-
1
display maps in
\mathcal{C}
.

Given a display map
\pi:(F_{0},...,F_{m})\rightarrow(F_{0},...,F_{m-1})
and a morphism
\alpha:(G_{0},...,G_{n})\rightarrow(F_{0},...,F_{m-1})
, the corresponding distinguished square is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G_{0},...,G_{n},G^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F_{0},...,F_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G_{0},...,G_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F_{0},...,F_{m-1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

where
G^{\prime}
,
\pi^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are as in the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{m-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

given by the structure of
\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
, i.e. the latter diagram is componentwise a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Given
A\in\text{Cat}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
, there exist isomorphisms of categories

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{A})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})^{A}

natural in
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
.

Proof.

The strict
2
-adjunction between
cont
and
att
yields an isomorphism

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{A})=\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\text{cont}(\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})))\cong\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Att}}(\text{att}(\mathcal{B}),\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C}))

natural in
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
. Let
B
,
C
be the underlying categories of
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
. Morphisms from
\text{att}(\mathcal{B})
to
\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
are functors
F:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow C^{A}
such that (i) for each length-
1
display map
p
in
\mathcal{B}
,
F(p):A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is componentwise a length-
1
display map; and (ii) each distinguished square in
\mathcal{B}
is sent to a commutative square in
C^{A}
whose
a
-component is a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}
for all
a\in A
. We conclude that the canonical isomorphism
(C^{A})^{B}\cong(C^{B})^{A}
restricts to an isomorphism between
\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Att}}(\text{att}(\mathcal{B}),\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C}))
and the full subcategory of
(C^{B})^{A}
spanned by those functors
H
such that for each
a\in A
,
H(a):\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a contextual functor. In other words, we have obtained an isomorphism
\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Att}}(\text{att}(\mathcal{B}),\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C}))\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})^{A}
.
∎

Now, we note that
\mathcal{C}^{A}
can be obtained as a certain exponential between a precontextual category and a contextual category.

Proposition 3.17
.

For a category
A
, let
A_{\text{pre}}
be the following precontextual category:

•

Its underlying category is the category
A_{+}
obtained by freely adjoining a terminal object
z
to
A
.

•

The length function is given by
l(z)=0
and
l(a)=1
for
a\in A
. The display maps are the terminal arrows
a\rightarrow z
for
a\in A
.

•

There are no distinguished squares.

There exists an isomorphism of contextual categories
\mathcal{C}^{A_{\text{pre}}}\cong\mathcal{C}^{A}
.

Proof.

It suffices to check that there exists an isomorphism of categories with attributes
D_{\text{att}}(A_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
. Let
C
be the underlying category of
\mathcal{C}
. A functor
F:A_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
belongs to
D(A_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{C})
if and only if
F(z)=1_{\mathcal{C}}
(as this implies that display maps are preserved). Since
z
was freely adjoined, restricting to
A
defines an isomorphism of categories
D(A_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{C})\cong C^{A}
.

Now, note that a natural transformation
\pi:G\rightarrow F
in
D(A_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{C})
is an indexed sort if and only if for every
a\in A
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(z)=1_{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(z)=1_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{z}=\;id}

is a relative length-
1
display map, which in turn is equivalent to
\pi_{a}
being a length-
1
display map. This means precisely that
\pi|_{A}:F|_{A}\rightarrow G|_{A}
is a display map with respect to
\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
. Finally, consider a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

where
\pi
,
\pi^{\prime}
are indexed sorts. By Remark
3.10
, it is an indexed distinguished square if and only if for each
a\in A
the square of
a
-components is a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}
. This is equivalent to the diagram of functors
A\rightarrow C
obtained by restriction of (
*
) being distinguished with respect to
\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
. We conclude that restriction along
A\hookrightarrow A_{+}
defines an isomorphism
D_{\text{att}}(A_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Fun}_{\text{att}}(A,\mathcal{C})
.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The contextual category associated with
A_{\text{pre}}
is isomorphic to the one presented by the generalized algebraic theory (in fact, multisorted algebraic theory) given by the following data:

•

for each object
a\in A
, a sort symbol
\underline{a}
introduced by
\vdash\underline{a}\;\mathsf{sort}
;

•

for each arrow
f:a\rightarrow b
in
A
, a term symbol
\underline{f}
introduced by
x:\underline{a}\vdash\underline{f}(x):\underline{b}
;

•

for each object
a
, an axiom
x:\underline{a}\vdash\underline{id_{a}}(x)\equiv x:\underline{a}
, and for each diagram
a\overset{f}{\rightarrow}b\overset{g}{\rightarrow}c
, an axiom

x:\underline{a}\vdash\underline{g\circ f}(x)\equiv\underline{g}(\underline{f}(x)):\underline{c}.

As an application of
\mathbf{Cont}
being
Cat
-powered, we obtain:

Remark 3.19
.

It is not difficult, at this point, to construct an isomorphism
\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cont}}(L(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{C})\cong\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Precont}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
for
\mathcal{A}\in\text{Precont}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
. However, we will derive it in §
5.6
as a corollary of
\mathcal{C}^{L(\mathcal{A})}\cong\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

3.3
The contextual category of family-valued models

A standard notion of model of a contextual category
\mathcal{A}
is a morphism

\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\text{Fam}

where
Fam
is the contextual category, defined in
[
11
]
, of iterated families of (small) sets: a length-
1
object is a small set
X
, a length-
2
object over
X
is a family of small sets
(Y_{x})_{x\in X}
, a length-
3
object is a family
(Z_{y})_{y\in\coprod_{x\in X}Y_{x}}
, and so on. Precisely, it can be described as
\text{cont}(\text{Fam}_{\text{att}})
for the (large) category with attributes
\text{Fam}_{\text{att}}=(\text{Set},\{*\},\text{fam},\Sigma)
where:

•

\text{fam}(X)
is the (large) set of all of families of small sets
(Y_{x})_{x\in X}
. In other words,
\text{fam}(X)=\mathscr{U}^{X}
where
\mathscr{U}
is the universe from Convention
2.1
. On morphisms,
fam
acts by precomposition.

•

\Sigma
sends a family
(Y_{x})_{x\in X}
to the projection
\pi_{Y}:\coprod_{x\in X}Y_{x}\rightarrow X
.

Note that the contextual category
\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}=\text{cont}(D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam}))
has the family-valued models
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{Fam}
as its length-
1
objects. However, the default description of higher dependencies in
\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}
, given by the display maps in
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})
, is convoluted as it involves functors
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{Fam}
rather than
|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\text{Set}
; see Definition
3.11
. In order to clarify the structure of
\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}
, we will now show how to present it, up to isomorphism, from a
cwa
whose base category is
\text{Set}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
. The idea is that for a functor
M:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{Fam}
in
D(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})
— that is, it preserves display maps (but not necessarily their length) and the distinguished terminal object —, the set
E(M)
of relative length-
1
display maps over
M:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{Fam}
only depends, up to isomorphism, on the composite
\mathcal{A}\overset{M}{\rightarrow}\text{Fam}\overset{U}{\rightarrow}\text{Set}
.

More precisely, we will consider a notion of family-valued model relative to a given “base” functor
B:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\text{Set}
; the usual models
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{Fam}
are recovered by taking
B
as the terminal functor. For
M
as above,
E(M)
will be isomorphic to the set of models relative to
U\circ M
.

3.3.1
Relative models

Notation 3.20
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be the category whose objects are the families of small sets
Y:X\rightarrow\mathscr{U}
(which, as usual, we also write as
(Y_{x})_{x\in X}
), and whose morphisms
(Y_{x})_{x\in X}\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in X^{\prime}}
are the functions
\amalg Y\rightarrow\amalg Y^{\prime}
. By construction, we have a full-and-faithful (and essentially surjective) functor
\amalg:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\text{Set}
. As above, we let
\pi_{Y}:\amalg Y\rightarrow X
be the projection map.

Definition 3.21
.

Consider a functor
B:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\text{Set}
. A
(family-valued) model of
\mathcal{A}
relative to
B
is a functor
F:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
equipped with a natural transformation
\varphi:\amalg\circ F\Rightarrow B
such that:

RM(i)

F(1_{\mathcal{A}})
is the constant family
\{*\}:B(1_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathscr{U}
, and
\amalg_{x\in B(1_{\mathcal{A}})}\{*\}=\amalg F(1_{\mathcal{A}})\overset{\varphi_{1_{\mathcal{A}}}}{\rightarrow}B(1_{\mathcal{A}})
is the standard projection.

RM(ii)

If
a\in\mathcal{A}
has length
\geq 1
, then
F(a)
is a family of the form
19
19

19

In this definition, which keeps track of set-theoretic data, we fix a pullback functor
\mathbf{Ar}(\text{Set})\times_{\text{Set}}\mathbf{Ar}(\text{Set})\rightarrow\text{Set}
. For definiteness, we can use the standard one, whose action on objects is
(f:Y\rightarrow X,\;g:Y\rightarrow Z)\mapsto\{(y,z)\in Y\times Z\mid f(y)=g(z)\}
.

\big(\amalg F(\partial a)\big)\times_{B(\partial a)}B(a)\rightarrow\mathscr{U}
, and
F(\textbf{p}_{a})
and
\varphi_{a}
are the composites indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\amalg F(\partial a)\big)\times_{B(\partial a)}B(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(\partial a).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\partial a}}

RM(iii)

For a distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{A}
, the diagram of sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\amalg F(\partial a)\big)\times_{B(\partial a)}B(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\amalg F(\partial b)\big)\times_{B(\partial b)}B(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\times_{B(f)}B(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b)}

commutes.

The latter condition implies that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg F(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg F(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\amalg F(\partial a)\big)\times_{B(\partial a)}B(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\amalg F(\partial b)\big)\times_{B(\partial b)}B(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\times_{B(f)}B(f^{\prime})}

is cartesian, but it also encodes the set-theoretic property of
F(a)
being a
strict
pullback, as a family, of
F(b)
along
F(f)\times_{B(f)}B(f^{\prime})
.

Write
\text{Mod}_{B}(\mathcal{A})
for the (large) set of models of
\mathcal{A}
relative to
B
. We will now show how to turn the assignment
B\mapsto\text{Mod}_{B}(\mathcal{A})
into a functor
(\text{Set}^{|\mathcal{A}|})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}
.

Construction 3.22
.

Consider functors
B^{1}
,
B^{2}:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\text{Set}
, a natural transformation
\beta:B^{2}\rightarrow B^{1}
, and a relative model

(F^{1}:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\mathscr{F},\;\varphi^{1}:\amalg\circ F^{1}\Rightarrow B^{1}).

Let us describe a functor
F^{2}:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
and natural transformations
\gamma
,
\varphi^{2}
fitting into a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod\circ F^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod\circ F^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{1}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

We start by constructing, recursively on the tree of display maps of
\mathcal{A}
, a function
F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}:\text{Ob}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\text{Ob}(\mathscr{F})
and a pullback square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod F^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{1}(a).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{2}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{1}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{a}}

•

We let
F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
be the family
(\{*\})_{x\in B^{2}(1_{\mathcal{A}})}
, with
\varphi^{2}_{1_{\mathcal{A}}}:\amalg_{x\in B^{2}(1_{\mathcal{A}})}\{*\}\rightarrow B^{2}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
the standard projection and
\gamma_{1_{\mathcal{A}}}:\amalg_{x\in B^{2}(1_{\mathcal{A}})}\{*\}\rightarrow\amalg F^{1}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=\amalg_{x\in B^{1}(1_{\mathcal{A}})}\{*\}
the map induced by
B^{2}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow B^{1}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
.

•

For
a\in\mathcal{A}
of length
\geq 1
, suppose that
F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(\partial a)
,
\varphi^{2}_{\partial a}
and
\gamma^{2}_{\partial a}
have been constructed. Then we let
F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)
be the composite

(\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(\partial a))\times_{B^{2}(\partial a)}B^{2}(a)\overset{\gamma_{\partial a}\times_{\beta_{\partial a}}\beta_{a}}{\longrightarrow}(\amalg F^{1}(\partial a))\times_{B^{1}(\partial a)}B^{1}(a)\overset{F^{1}(a)}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{U},

and
\gamma_{a}
the top arrow in the canonical cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(\partial a))\times_{B^{2}(\partial a)}B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\amalg F^{1}(\partial a))\times_{B^{1}(\partial a)}B^{1}(a).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{1}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\partial a}\times_{\beta_{\partial a}}\beta_{a}}

Now, note that in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(\partial a))\times_{B^{2}(\partial a)}B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\amalg F^{1}(\partial a))\times_{B^{1}(\partial a)}B^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F_{1}(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{2}(\partial a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{1}(\partial a),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\partial a}\times_{\beta_{\partial a}}\beta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{1}(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\partial a}}
(I)
(III)
(II)
(IV)

the squares (II), (III) (by definition) and (IV) (by the induction hypothesis) are cartesian, hence so is (I) by the pasting lemma for pullbacks. It follows that the outer composite square in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\amalg F^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(\partial a))\times_{B^{2}(\partial a)}B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\amalg F^{1}(\partial a))\times_{B^{1}(\partial a)}B^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{1}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{1}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\partial a}\times_{\beta_{\partial a}}\beta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{a}}

is cartesian, and we let
\varphi^{2}_{a}:\amalg F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}(a)\rightarrow B^{2}(a)
be the left vertical composite.

•

Having constructed
F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}
,
\gamma
and
\varphi^{2}
, observe that
F^{2}_{\text{Ob}}
extends uniquely, by functoriality of pullbacks, into a functor
F^{2}:|\mathcal{A}|\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
such that the families
\gamma
and
\varphi^{2}
define natural transformations
\amalg\circ F^{2}\Rightarrow\amalg\circ F^{1}
and
\amalg\circ F^{2}\Rightarrow B^{2}
, respectively.

Proposition 3.23
.

The pair
(F^{2},\varphi^{2})
constructed above is a model of
\mathcal{A}
relative to
B^{2}
.

Proof.

Conditions RM(i) and RM(ii) from Definition
3.21
are satisfied by construction. For RM(iii), note that for a distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\amalg F^{2}(\partial a)\big)\times_{B^{2}(\partial a)}B^{2}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\amalg F^{2}(\partial b)\big)\times_{B^{2}(\partial b)}B^{2}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\amalg F^{1}(\partial a)\big)\times_{B^{1}(\partial a)}B^{1}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\amalg F^{1}(\partial b)\big)\times_{B^{1}(\partial b)}B^{1}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{2}(f)\times_{B^{2}(f)}B^{2}(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\partial a}\times_{\beta_{\partial a}}\beta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\partial b}\times_{\beta_{\partial b}}\beta_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{1}(f)\times_{B^{1}(f)}B^{1}(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{1}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{1}(b)}

commutes, and the composites
\big(\amalg F^{2}(\partial a)\big)\times_{B^{2}(\partial a)}B^{2}(a)\rightarrow\mathscr{U}
and
\big(\amalg F^{2}(\partial b)\big)\times_{B^{2}(\partial b)}B^{2}(b)\rightarrow\mathscr{U}
are, by definition,
F^{2}(a)
and
F^{2}(b)
, respectively.
∎

Definition 3.24
.

We will say that the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod\circ F^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod\circ F^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

realizes the relative model
(F^{2},\varphi^{2})
as the
distinguished base change
of
(F^{1},\varphi^{1})
along
\beta
.

We leave it as an exercise to verify from Construction
3.22
that the distinguished base change operation is strictly functorial. This yields a (large) category with attributes
\text{Mod}_{rel}(\mathcal{A})
with base category
\text{Set}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, presheaf of types
\text{Mod}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}):(\text{Set}^{|\mathcal{A}|})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}^{+}
, where
\text{Set}^{+}
is the category of sets
X\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
(as in Convention
2.1
) and functions between them.

3.3.2
Characterizing
\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}
via relative models

It can be proved that if a functor
M:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{Fam}
belongs to
D(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})
, then we have a canonical bijection between
\text{Mod}_{U\circ M}(\mathcal{A})
and the set
E(M)
of relative length-
1
display maps with codomain
M
. Let us sketch how to associate with a relative length-
1
display map
\varphi:F\Rightarrow M
a functor
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
, a natural transformation
\varphi^{\prime}:\amalg\circ F^{\prime}\Rightarrow U\circ M
, and a natural isomorphism
\omega:\amalg\circ F^{\prime}\Rightarrow U\circ F
recursively on the tree of display maps of
\mathcal{A}
:

•

Noting that
UF(1_{\mathcal{A}})=UM(1_{\mathcal{A}})=U(1_{\text{Fam}})=\{*\}
, we (must) let
F^{\prime}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=(\{*\})_{x\in\{*\}}
, with
\varphi^{\prime}_{1_{\mathcal{A}}}
,
\omega_{1_{\mathcal{A}}}:\amalg_{x\in\{*\}}\{*\}\rightarrow\{*\}
the unique map.

•

For
a\in\mathcal{A}
of length
\geq 1
, suppose that
F^{\prime}(\partial a)
,
\varphi^{\prime}_{\partial a}
and
\omega_{\partial a}
have been constructed. Then from the relative length-
1
display map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\partial a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\partial a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\textbf{p}_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\partial a}}

in
Fam
we obtain a length-
1
display map
F(a)\twoheadrightarrow F(\partial a)\times_{M(\partial a)}M(a)
, which in turn corresponds to a family
X:U\big(F(\partial a)\times_{M(\partial a)}M(a)\big)\rightarrow\mathscr{U}
. We let
F^{\prime}(a)
be the composite

\amalg F^{\prime}(\partial a)\times_{UM(\partial a)}UM(a)\overset{\omega_{\partial a}\times id}{\cong}UF(\partial a)\times_{UM(\partial a)}UM(a)\cong U\big(F(\partial a)\times_{M(\partial a)}M(a)\big)\overset{X}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{U}.

This yields, in particular, a canonical isomorphism
\coprod F^{\prime}(a)\cong UF(a)
, which we take as
\omega_{a}
. Also, we let
\varphi^{\prime}_{a}:\coprod F^{\prime}(a)\rightarrow UM(a)
be
U(\varphi_{a})\circ\omega_{a}
.

•

For
a
,
b\in\mathcal{A}
, we let
F^{\prime}
act on morphisms
a\rightarrow b
by the composite

\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(a,b)\overset{UF}{\rightarrow}\text{Hom}_{\text{Set}}(UF(a),UF(b))\overset{\blacklozenge}{\cong}\text{Hom}_{\text{Set}}(\amalg F^{\prime}(a),\amalg F^{\prime}(b))=\text{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(F^{\prime}(a),F^{\prime}(b))

where
\blacklozenge
is given by
f\mapsto\omega_{b^{-1}}\circ f\circ\omega_{a}
.

It is not difficult to verify functoriality of
F^{\prime}
, naturality of
\omega
and
\varphi^{\prime}
, and that
F^{\prime}
satisfies the last condition from Definition
3.21
.

A similar construction, also using an auxiliary natural isomorphism
\omega
, allows us to obtain a relative length-
1
display map with codomain
M
from a model relative to
U\circ M
; this construction is set up so that it is inverse to the previous one. This yields a morphism of
cwa
s

K:D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})\longrightarrow\text{Mod}_{rel}(\mathcal{A})

whose functor between base categories is
U\circ-:D(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})\rightarrow\text{Set}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
and whose natural transformation
E\Rightarrow\text{Mod}_{U\circ-}(\mathcal{A})
has as components the bijections constructed above. We then get a contextual functor

\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}=\text{cont}(D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam}))\overset{\text{cont}(K)}{\longrightarrow}\text{cont}(\text{Mod}_{rel}(\mathcal{A})),

which is full-and-faithful since
U\circ-
is full-and-faithful, and bijective on objects since
E\Rightarrow\text{Mod}_{U\circ-}(\mathcal{A})
is an isomorphism; hence
\text{cont}(K)
is an isomorphism.

3.4
Bimorphisms of contextual categories

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
be contextual categories with underlying categories
A
,
B
and
C
, respectively. A contextual functor

F:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}=\text{cont}(D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}))

corresponds, via the adjunction
\text{att}\dashv\text{cont}
, to a morphism
\text{att}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
. By taking the base categories of these
cwa
s, the latter morphism yields a functor
B\rightarrow C^{A}
, hence a functor
A\times B\rightarrow C
. Our next goal will be to give a more explicit characterization of the functors
A\times B\rightarrow C
that arise in this way. Such functors will be the
bimorphisms
from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{C}
. In fact, our characterization will make sense when
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
are precontextual categories and
\mathcal{C}
is a contextual category, so we introduce bimorphisms in this more general setting. This will be useful later when doing explicit calculations.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
be precontextual categories and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category with underlying categories
A
,
B
,
C
, respectively. A functor
H:A\times B\rightarrow C
is said to be a
bimorphism
from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{C}
if it satisfies the following conditions:

Bim(i)

For each
b\in B
,
H(-,b):A\rightarrow C
belongs to
D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
, and it is the constant functor on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
if
b
has length
0
.

Bim(ii)

Suppose given length-
1
display maps
p:a\rightarrow\partial a
in
\mathcal{A}
and
q:b\rightarrow\partial b
in
\mathcal{B}
. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a,\partial b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\partial a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\partial a,\partial b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

is a relative length-
1
display map.

In what follows, we denote by
\text{gap}_{H}(a,b)
the gap map of (
*
) (recall Definition
3.1
).

Bim(iii)

Consider a length-
1
display map
q:b\rightarrow\partial b
in
\mathcal{B}
and a length-
1
distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{A}
. The commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a_{1},b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a_{2},b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{gap}_{H}(a_{1},b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{gap}_{H}(a_{2},b)}

where the bottom arrow is induced by
H
and functoriality of pullbacks, is a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}
.

Bim(iv)

Consider a length-
1
display map
p:a\rightarrow\partial a
in
\mathcal{A}
and a length-
1
distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{B}
. The commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a,b_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a,b_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{gap}_{H}(a,b_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{gap}_{H}(a,b_{2})}

where the bottom arrow is induced by
H
and functoriality of pullbacks, is a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 3.26
.

In the notation of Definition
3.25
, a functor
H:A\times B\rightarrow C
is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{C}
if and only if there exists a contextual functor
F:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
such that
H
equals the image under
(C^{A})^{B}\cong C^{A\times B}
of the composite (recall Notation
3.12
)

B\overset{F}{\longrightarrow}|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}|\overset{(-)_{\varheartsuit}}{\longrightarrow}C^{A}.

This establishes a bijection, natural in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Precont}^{\text{op}}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
, between contextual functors
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
and bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Firstly, note that for a morphism
F:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
, the functor
B\overset{F}{\rightarrow}|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}|\overset{(-)_{\varheartsuit}}{\rightarrow}C^{A}
factors through
D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})\subset C^{A}
; also, the image of a length-
0
object
b\in\mathcal{B}
under
B\rightarrow D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
is
F(b)_{\varheartsuit}=(1_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}})_{\varheartsuit}
, which in turn is the functor
\text{ct}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}
constant on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
. So Bim(i) holds.

From the adjunction
\text{att}\dashv\text{cont}
and the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
, morphisms
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
correspond, via the above construction, to morphisms of categories with attributes
\text{att}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
. Thus we must prove that for a functor
F:B\rightarrow D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
such that
F(b)=\text{ct}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}
whenever
\ell(b)=0
, the following are equivalent:

•

it is a morphism
\text{att}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
;

•

its adjunct
H:A\times B\rightarrow C
satisfies Bim(ii)-(iv).

Now, observe that Bim(ii) holds for
H
if and only if condition IS(i) from Definition
3.3
holds for
F(q):F(b)\rightarrow F(\partial b)
for every length-
1
display map
q:b\rightarrow\partial b
in
\mathcal{B}
. Similarly, Bim(iii) holds for
H
if and only if IS(ii) holds for
F(q)
for every length-
1
display map
q
in
\mathcal{B}
.

Also, applying
F
to a length-
1
distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,b_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,b_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,\partial b_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,\partial b_{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,q_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,q_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(-,f)}

By Remark
3.10
, the latter is an indexed distinguished square (in other words, a square that is distinguished in
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
) if and only if
H
satisfies Bim(iv) for the square (
*
) and every length-
1
display map
p:a\rightarrow\partial a
in
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

Proposition 3.27
.

In the notation of Definition
3.25
, a functor
H:A\times B\rightarrow C
is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{C}
if and only if it satisfies

Bim(i’)

for each
b\in B
,
H(-,b):A\rightarrow C
is a flexible morphism from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{C}
, and it is constant on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
if
b
has length
0
,

and conditions Bim(ii) and Bim(iv) from the statement of Proposition
3.26
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that if
H
satisfies Bim(i), Bim(ii) and Bim(iv) from Proposition
3.26
, then it satisfies Bim(iii) if and only if for each
b\in B
,
H(-,b)
preserves distinguished squares.

Firstly, suppose that
H
satisfies Bim(iii). Let us check by induction on
n\geq 0
that for every
b\in\mathcal{B}
of length
n
,
H(-,b)
preserves distinguished squares. For
n=0
the claim holds as if
\ell(b)=0
, then
H(-,b)=\text{ct}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}
. Given
n\geq 1
, assume that it holds for
0
, …,
n-1
. Suppose that
q:b\rightarrow\partial b
is a length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{B}
with
\ell(b)=n
, and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a length-
1
distinguished square in
\mathcal{A}
. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{2},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{2},\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{1},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{1},\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{2},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{2},\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{1},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{1},\partial b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{2},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{2},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{1},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{1},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,id)}

By Bim(ii), faces (1) and (2) are relative length-
1
display maps. Then from the arrows
\text{gap}(a_{1},b):H(a_{1},b)\rightarrow x_{1}
and
\text{gap}(a_{2},b):H(a_{2},b)\rightarrow x_{2}
we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{2},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{1},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{2},\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a_{1},\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{2},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{2},\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{1},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(\partial a_{1},\partial b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{gap}(a_{2},b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{2},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{gap}(a_{1},b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{1},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{2},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{1},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

where (1’) and (2’) are distinguished. By the induction hypothesis, the face with vertices
H(a_{1},\partial b)
,
H(a_{2},\partial b)
,
H(\partial a_{1},\partial b)
,
H(\partial a_{2},\partial b)
is a distinguished square, hence by the pasting lemma for distinguished pullbacks, the square containing
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
H(\partial a_{1},b)
,
H(\partial a_{2},b)
is also distinguished. By Bim(iii), square (3) is distinguished, so again by the pasting lemma, so is the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a_{1},b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a_{2},b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\partial a_{1},b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\partial a_{1},b).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{1},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{2},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,id)}

This concludes the induction step.

Conversely, suppose that
H(-,b)
preserves distinguished squares for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
. To verify condition Bim(iii), suppose given a length-
1
display map
q:b\rightarrow\partial b
in
\mathcal{B}
and a length-
1
distinguished square in
\mathcal{A}
of the form (
*
). Then Bim(ii) allows us to construct diagrams of the same form as the ones above. An analogous application of the pasting lemma – now using that
H(-,b)
preserves distinguished pullbacks – shows that the square with vertices
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
H(\partial a_{1},b)
,
H(\partial a_{2},b)
is distinguished. On the other hand, as
H(-,b^{\prime})
preserves distinguished pullbacks, the square of the form (
**
) above is distinguished. It now follows from the pasting lemma that so is (3). Thus Bim(iii) holds, as required.
∎

3.5
A syntactic example

We will now present one of the key results towards comparing the monoidal structure from the present text with the tensor product from
[
5
]
.

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be generalized algebraic theories. The functor

\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})

from
[
5
]
is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))
to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
.

Proof.

Following Proposition
3.27
, let us verify that the functor
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
, which will be denoted simply by
\otimes
, satisfies conditions Bim(i’), Bim(ii) and Bim(iv).

For Bim(i’), consider a context
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
in
\mathbb{B}
. Let us prove that
-\otimes[\textbf{Y}]:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
is a flexible morphism. Firstly, note that it preserves the distinguished terminal object:

1_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})}\otimes[\textbf{Y}]=[\varnothing]\otimes[\textbf{Y}]=[\varnothing\otimes\textbf{Y}]=[\varnothing]=1_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})}.

Now, suppose given a length-
1
distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
, say corresponding to a substitution diagram of context morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}^{\prime}}}
X
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}^{\prime}}
f
p
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{f}^{\prime}}

where

\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m+1}:X_{m+1}),\qquad\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{1}:X^{\prime}_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{n}:X^{\prime}_{n},x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}:X_{m+1}[\textbf{f}]),

\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{m}),\quad\textbf{f}^{\prime}=(f_{1},...,f_{m},x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}),\quad\textbf{p}=(x_{1},...,x_{m}),\quad\textbf{p}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}).

It is mapped by
-\otimes[\textbf{Y}]
to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{p}\otimes\textbf{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}}

which is given explicitly by

\displaystyle\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(x_{i}y_{j}:X_{i}\otimes Y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{i}y_{j}:X^{\prime}_{i}\otimes Y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m^{\prime}\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x_{m+1}y_{1}:X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{1},...,x_{m+1}y_{n}:X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{n})

\displaystyle\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}y_{1}:X_{m+1}[\textbf{f}]\otimes Y_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}y_{n}:X_{m+1}[\textbf{f}]\otimes Y_{n})

\displaystyle\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(f_{i}\otimes y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}y_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}y_{n})

\displaystyle\textbf{p}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\textbf{p}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m^{\prime}\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}

where, by abuse of notation, a family indexed by a set of the form
\{1,...,a\}\times\{1,...,b\}
indicates the one indexed by
\{1,...,ab\}
induced by the lexicographic order on the former set, and
*
denotes concatenation of finite sequences. It is immediate from this description that
\textbf{p}\otimes\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{p}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
are (length-
n
) display maps. Moreover, the two-sided substitution property discussed in
[
5
]
implies that for
j=1
, …,
n
, the sort equality

X_{m+1}[\textbf{f}]\otimes Y_{j}\equiv(X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{j})[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}]

is derivable in context
(\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}y_{1}:X_{m+1}[\textbf{f}]\otimes Y_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}y_{j-1}:X_{m+1}[\textbf{f}]\otimes Y_{j-1})
. It follows that
\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
is provably equal to

(\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x^{\prime}_{m+1}y_{1}:(X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{1})[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}],...,x^{\prime}_{m+1}y_{n}:(X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{n})[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}]).

From this and the above description of
\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
we conclude that the square in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
associated with (
*
) is distinguished. This also shows, in particular, that
-\otimes[\textbf{Y}]
preserves display maps. Therefore
-\otimes[\textbf{Y}]
is a flexible morphism.

If
Y
is the empty context, it is clear that
-\otimes[\textbf{Y}]
is constant with value
1_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})}
. This concludes the proof of Bim(i’).

For Bim(ii), we must prove that for contexts
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m+1}:X_{m+1})
in
\mathbb{A}
and
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n+1}:Y_{n+1})
in
\mathbb{B}
, the diagram of contexts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n+1\end{subarray}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m+1\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}}

corresponds to a relative length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
. Observe that substitution of
\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}
along
\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}
yields

Z
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n+1\end{subarray}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq m+1\\
j\leq n\end{subarray}}}

where
\textbf{Z}=(\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x_{m+1}y_{1}:Y_{m+1}\otimes Y_{1},...,x_{m+1}y_{n}:Y_{m+1}\otimes Y_{n})
. Hence the gap map of (
**
) in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
is

[(x_{i}y_{j})_{(i,j)\in\{1,...,m+1\}\times\{1,...,n+1\}\smallsetminus(m+1,n+1)}]:[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\longrightarrow[\textbf{Z}],

which is clearly a length-
1
display map.

Finally, let us check Bim(iv). Consider a length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
and a length-
1
distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})
, say corresponding, respectively, to

\textbf{p}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\partial\textbf{X}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hbox to92.04pt{\vbox to52.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 46.01726pt\lower-27.04419pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where

\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m+1}:X_{m+1}),\qquad\textbf{p}=(x_{1},...,x_{m}),

\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n}),\quad\textbf{f}^{\prime}=(f_{1},...,f_{n},y^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}),\quad\textbf{q}=(y_{1},...,y_{n}),\quad\textbf{q}^{\prime}=(y^{\prime}_{1},...,y^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}).

and

we obtain, as in the above proof of Bim(ii), the gap map

(x_{i}y_{j})_{(i,j)\in\{1,...,m+1\}\times\{1,...,n+1\}\smallsetminus(m+1,n+1)}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow(\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})*(x_{m+1}y_{j}:X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{j})_{j\leq n}=\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}),

and from
p
and
\textbf{q}^{\prime}
we obtain the gap map

(x_{i}y^{\prime}_{j})_{(i,j)\in\{1,...,m+1\}\times\{1,...,n^{\prime}+1\}\smallsetminus(m+1,n^{\prime}+1)}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\longrightarrow(\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})*(x_{m+1}y_{j}:X_{m+1}\otimes Y^{\prime}_{j})_{j\leq n^{\prime}}=\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}).

Then, noting that
(\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime})*(x_{m+1}\otimes f_{1},...,x_{m+1}\otimes f_{n})=\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime})
, the diagram from the statement of Bim(iv) is the equivalence class of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y^{\prime}_{j})_{(i,j)\neq(m+1,n^{\prime}+1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{i}y_{j})_{(i,j)\neq(m+1,n+1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime}}

Now, by the two-sided substitution property from
[
5
]
we can derive the sort equality

X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{n+1}[\textbf{f}]\equiv(X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{n+1})[\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime})]

in context
\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})
, which implies that
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
is provably equal to
\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})*(x_{m+1}y^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}:(X_{m+1}\otimes Y_{n+1})[\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime})])
. It follows from this (also observing that the last entry of
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{f}^{\prime}
is
x_{m+1}y^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}
) that the above diagram is a distinguished square in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{C})
.

Having verified Bim(i’), Bim(ii) and Bim(iv), we conclude that
\otimes
is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))
to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
.
∎

4
Poset-shaped cellular diagrams

We will now study what we will call
cellular diagrams
from a locally finite poset to a contextual category. As mentioned in the introduction, we expect these to correspond, essentially, to the Reedy diagrams from
[
26
]
in the special case where the domain category is a poset and the codomain
cwa
is a contextual category.

Poset-shaped cellular diagrams
(P,\leq)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
will come in two variants, each one requiring a kind of additional structure on
P
. Firstly, for finite
P
, we will introduce (Definition
4.5
) cellular diagrams
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
where
\vartriangleleft
is a linear refinement of
\leq
. Then, for locally finite
P
, we will define cellular diagrams
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
where
\vartriangleleft
is a
local linearization
(Definition
4.19
): we only compare elements that have a common upper bound with respect to
\leq
.
20
20

20

When
P
is finite, the two concepts do not coincide but can be used interchangeably. See Remark
4.22
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(P,\leq)
be a finite poset. We define a
linearization
of
(P,\leq)
as a linear order
\vartriangleleft
on
P
that refines
\leq
: for all
a
,
b\in P
, if
a\leq b
, then
a\vartriangleleft b
.

A
linearized finite poset
is a triple
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
where
(P,\leq)
is a finite poset and
\vartriangleleft
is a linearization of
(P,\leq)
.

Definition 4.2
.

A
sieve
in a poset
(P,\leq)
is a downwards closed subset, i.e.
S\subset P
is a sieve if for any
a\in P
,
b\in S
, if
a\leq b
, then
a\in S
. The set of sieves in
(P,\leq)
will be denoted by
\text{Sieve}(P,\leq)
or simply by
\text{Sieve}(P)
.

A poset morphism
F:(P,\leq)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
is said to be a
sieve embedding
if
F(P)
is a sieve in
P^{\prime}
and
F
defines a poset isomorphism
P\cong F(P)
. The latter condition means that (i)
F
is injective, and (ii) if
F(a)\leq^{\prime}F(b)
, then
a\leq b
.

A sieve in a linearly ordered set will be referred to as an
initial segment
, and a sieve embedding between linearly ordered sets is referred to as an
initial segment embedding
.

For a linearized finite poset
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
, the notation
\text{Sieve}(P)
will be used for
\text{Sieve}(P,\leq)
. On the other hand, by an initial segment of
P
we mean an initial segment of
(P,\vartriangleleft)
; in a similar way we talk about initial segment embeddings between linearized finite posets.

When this causes no confusion, we denote
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
simply by
P
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
(P,\leq)
be a poset that has a top element
\top
. Its
boundary
, denoted by
\partial(P,\leq)
or simply by
\partial P
, is the sieve
P\smallsetminus\{\top\}
.

Given a linearization
\vartriangleleft
of
(P,\leq)
, we will also write
\partial P
for the linearized poset given by endowing
P\smallsetminus\{\top\}
with the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
. In this case, note that the inclusion
\partial P\rightarrow P
is both a sieve embedding (with respect to
\leq
) and an initial segment embedding (with respect to
\vartriangleleft
). When no further description is made,
\partial P\rightarrow P
will denote the inclusion map.

Notation 4.4
.

Given a poset
(P,\leq)
and
x\in P
, we let

x^{\leq}\coloneqq\{y\in P\mid y\leq x\},\qquad\partial x\coloneqq\partial(x^{\leq})=\{y\in P\mid y<x\}.

4.1
Defining cellular diagrams

In what follows we let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category.

Definition 4.5
.

Given a linearized finite poset
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
, we define a
cellular diagram
of shape
P
in
\mathcal{C}
as a functor

F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

such that one can choose
21
21

21

Although we don’t take such a choice as part of the structure, we will see in Proposition
4.6
that it is uniquely determined.
for each sieve
X\subset P
a limit cone of the form

\varphi^{X}:a^{X}\Longrightarrow F|_{X}

(where
a^{X}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
, and we use the same notation for the corresponding constant diagram) in a way that the following hold:

(i)

a^{\varnothing}=1_{\mathcal{C}}
(with
\varphi^{\varnothing}
the empty cone).

(ii)

For each
x\in P
,
a^{x^{\leq}}=F(x)
and
\varphi^{x^{\leq}}
is the cone whose
y
-component is the image of the unique arrow
y\rightarrow x
under
F
.

(iii)

Suppose given sieves
X\subset Y
such that
Y\smallsetminus X
is a singleton
\{y\}
with
x\vartriangleleft y
for all
x\in X
. Then the induced map between limits

a^{Y}\longrightarrow a^{X}

is a length-
1
display map.

(iv)

Consider a diagram of sieve inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}

such that
Y\smallsetminus X=\{y\}=Y^{\prime}\smallsetminus X^{\prime}
with
x\vartriangleleft y
for all
x\in X^{\prime}
. Then the induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{X}}

is a distinguished square.

We will say that a family of cones as above
realizes
F
as a cellular diagram
(of shape
P
).

Proposition 4.6
.

Consider a linearized finite poset
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
, a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, and a functor
F:{(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Then

(a)

If
F
is a cellular diagram of shape
P
, then for every sieve
Y\subset P
we have that
F|_{Y}:(Y,\leq|_{Y})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow C
is a cellular diagram of shape
Y
(endowed with the restrictions of
\leq
and
\vartriangleleft
). In fact, for a family
(a^{X},\varphi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P)}
realizing
F
as a cellular diagram,
(a^{X},\varphi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(Y)}
realizes
F|_{Y}
as a cellular diagram.

(b)

There exists at most one family of cones
(a^{X},\varphi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P)}
realizing
F
as a cellular diagram.

(c)

Suppose that
P\neq\varnothing
. Denoting by
\top
the top element of
(P,\vartriangleleft)
and recalling that
\partial P=P\smallsetminus\{\top\}
, assume that
F|_{\partial P}:\partial P^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a cellular diagram, say whose corresponding family of cones is
(a^{X},\varphi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P\smallsetminus\{\top\})}
. Then
F
is cellular if and only if the induced map
F(\top)\rightarrow a^{\partial\top}
is a length-
1
display map.

(Hence extending a cellular diagram of shape
P\smallsetminus\{\top\}
to one of shape
P
amounts to choosing a length-
1
display map with codomain
a^{\partial\top}
.)

Proof.

(a)

Conditions (i)-(iv) from Definition
4.5
are immediate.

(b)

We prove by induction on
n\geq 0
that the claim holds whenever
P
has cardinality
n
. For
n=0
, it holds by condition (i) in Definition
4.5
. Let
n\geq 1
and suppose that the claim holds for
0
, …,
n-1
. Let
F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow C
be a cellular diagram with
\sharp P=n
and realized by families

(a^{X},\varphi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P)},

(b^{X},\psi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P)}.

By (a) and the induction hypothesis, we have
a^{X}=b^{X}
and
\varphi^{X}=\psi^{X}
whenever
X
does not contain the top element
\top
of
(P,\vartriangleleft)
. On the other hand, if
\top\in X
, consider the diagram of sieve inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\top}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial X=X\smallsetminus\{\top\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\top^{\leq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X.}}

By condition (iv) from Definition
4.5
, we have distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\top^{\leq}}=F(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\partial X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\partial\top},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\top^{\leq}}=F(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\partial X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\partial\top}.}

As
a
,
b
and
\varphi
,
\psi
coincide for sieves in
\partial P
, the bottom arrows are equal. By the same remark and condition (ii) from Definition
4.5
, the right vertical arrows are equal. It follows that the left vertical arrows are also equal. Now, the components of
\varphi^{X}
are the top arrow
a^{X}\longrightarrow F(\top)
and the composites

a^{X}\twoheadrightarrow a^{\partial X}\longrightarrow F(x)

for
x\in\partial X
. By comparing this with the analogous description of
\psi^{X}
we conclude that
\varphi^{X}=\psi^{X}
.

(c)

We define a family
(b^{X},\psi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P)}
following the same strategy used in (b). For
X
not containing
\top
, we let
b^{X}=a^{X}
and
\psi^{X}=\varphi^{X}
. For
X
containing
\top
, we let
b^{X}
be as in the distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\partial X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\partial\top}}

In this case, we let
\psi^{X}
be the cone whose components are
b^{X}\rightarrow F(\top)
and the composites

b^{X}\twoheadrightarrow a^{\partial X}\longrightarrow F(x)

for
x\in\partial X
(the fact that these arrows form a cone follows from commutativity of (
*
)). A routine calculation shows that
\psi^{X}
is a limit cone from
b^{X}
to
F
. It remains to verify conditions (ii)-(iv) from Definition
4.5
.

For
x\neq\top
, (ii) follows from the corresponding condition for
F|_{\partial P}
. For
\top
, it follows from the construction of
b^{\top^{\leq}}
and
\psi^{\top^{\leq}}
, which is given by pulling back along
a^{\partial\top}\overset{id}{\rightarrow}a^{\partial\top}
.

For (iii), consider sieves
X\subset Y
such that
Y\smallsetminus X=\{y\}
with
x\vartriangleleft y
for all
x\in X
. Note that
X\subset\partial P
. If
y\neq\top
, the condition follows from the corresponding property of
F|_{\partial P}
. For
y=\top
, note that the comparison map
b^{Y}\rightarrow b^{X}=a^{X}
is the left arrow in (
*
) with
X
replaced by
Y
, hence a length-
1
display map.

For (iv), suppose given sieve inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}

such that
Y\smallsetminus X=\{y\}=Y^{\prime}\smallsetminus X^{\prime}
with
x\vartriangleleft y
for all
x\in X^{\prime}
. If
y\neq\top
, the assertion follows from the corresponding one for
F|_{\partial P}
. On the other hand, for
y=\top
we consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{Y^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\top)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{X^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\partial\top}.}}

Since the right square and the composite one are distinguished, so is the left one, as required.

∎

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category,
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
a linearized finite poset, and
F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a cellular diagram. For
(a^{X},\varphi^{X})_{X\in\text{Sieve}(P)}
the family as in Definition
4.5
and Proposition
4.6
,
a^{P}
will be called the
distinguished limit
of
F
. We will denote it by
F[\textbf{P}]
, or by
F[P]
when no ambiguity is caused.

For a sieve
X\subset P
, which we view as a linearized poset via the restrictions of
\leq
and
\vartriangleleft
, we abbreviate
F|_{X}[X]
as
F[X]
. We write
F[X]_{\vartriangleleft}
when dealing with more than one linearization of
(P,\leq)
.

Although we do not fix a notation for the natural transformation
\varphi^{X}
, we usually denote it by
\pi
or variants (such as
\pi^{F,(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)}
, or
\pi^{\prime}
when different cellular diagrams are being considered).

Example 4.8
.

The terminal contextual category
\mathcal{Z}
is the co-discrete category whose objects are the natural numbers (that is, there is a unique arrow
m\rightarrow n
for each
m
,
n\geq 0
), with length function the identity
\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, and display maps and distinguished squares given in the unique possible way (for instance, an arrow
m\rightarrow n
is a display map if and only if
m\geq n
).

Let
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
be a linearized finite poset. By Proposition
4.6
(c), there exists a unique cellular diagram
F
of shape
P
in
\mathcal{Z}
. Induction on the cardinality of
P
shows that for each
x\in P
,
F(x)
is the cardinality of
x^{\leq}
; more generally, for a sieve
X\subset P
,
F[X]
is the cardinality of
X
.

Definition 4.9
.

Given linearized finite posets
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
,
(P^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\vartriangleleft^{\prime})
, and a sieve embedding
F:(P,\leq)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
, we will say that
F
is a
strong sieve embedding
from
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
to
(P^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\vartriangleleft^{\prime})
if
\vartriangleleft
is the restriction of
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
along
F
. Equivalently,
F
is monotone with respect to
\vartriangleleft
and
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
.

We will denote by
LFPos
the category whose objects are the linearized finite posets and whose morphisms are the strong sieve embeddings.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category. For
\textbf{P}\in\text{LFPos}
we will write
\text{cd}(\textbf{P},\mathcal{C})
for the set of cellular diagrams of shape
P
in
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4.5
).

Since restricting a cellular diagram along a strong sieve embedding defines a cellular diagram, we have a functor

\text{cd}(-,\mathcal{C}):\text{LFPos}^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\text{Set}.

4.2
Some operations on cellular diagrams

Let
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
be a linearized finite poset,
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category, and

F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

a cellular diagram of shape
P
.

We will now describe a procedure that, given another linearization
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
of
(P,\leq)
, yields a cellular diagram
F^{\prime}:{(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of shape
\textbf{P}^{\prime}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft^{\prime})
endowed with an isomorphism
F^{\prime}\cong F
.

Proposition 4.10
.

In the above setting, there exists a unique pair
(F^{\prime},\varphi)
consisting of a cellular diagram
F^{\prime}:{(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of shape
\textbf{P}^{\prime}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft^{\prime})
and a natural transformation
\varphi:F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F
with the following property: for every
x\in P
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(x)=F^{\prime}[x^{\leq}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x)=F[x^{\leq}]_{\vartriangleleft}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}[\partial x]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial x]_{\vartriangleleft},}

where the horizontal arrows are induced by
\varphi
, is distinguished. Also,
\varphi
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Write
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
for the elements of
P
ordered by
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
, let
P_{i}=\{x_{1},...,x_{i}\}
for
0\leq i\leq n
(which is a sieve), and let
F_{i}
be the cellular diagram obtained by restricting
F
to
(P_{i},\leq|_{P_{i}},\vartriangleleft|_{P_{i}})
. We will prove by induction that for
0\leq i\leq n
, the claim in the statement holds for
F_{i}
and
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}|_{P_{i}}
.

The claim holds for
i=0
as there is a unique cellular diagram indexed by
\varnothing
. Now, let
1\leq i\leq n
and suppose that it holds for
i-1
. Let
(G,\gamma)
be the unique pair consisting of a cellular diagram
G
of shape
(P_{i-1},\leq|_{P_{i-1}},\vartriangleleft^{\prime}|_{P_{i-1}})
and a natural transformation
\gamma:G\Rightarrow F_{i-1}
such that the square analogous to (
*
) is distinguished for all
x\in P_{i-1}
. Consider the distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x_{i})=F[x_{i}^{\leq}]_{\vartriangleleft}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\partial x_{i}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial x_{i}]_{\vartriangleleft}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

As
p
is a length-
1
display map (Definition
4.5
(iii)), so is
p^{\prime}
. By Proposition
4.6
(c), we can extend
G
to a cellular diagram
G^{\prime}
of shape
(P_{i},\leq|_{P_{i}},\vartriangleleft^{\prime}|_{P_{i}})
by setting
G^{\prime}(x_{i})=a
and, for each
y\leq x_{i}
, mapping the unique arrow
y\rightarrow x_{i}
to the composite

a\overset{p^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}G[\partial x_{i}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}\longrightarrow G(y).

It then follows that
\gamma
extends to a natural transformation
\gamma^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\Rightarrow F_{i}
by taking
\gamma^{\prime}_{x_{i}}
as the top arrow in (
**
). It follows, in particular, that
(G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
is the unique pair satisfying the condition in the statement for
F_{i}
and
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}|_{P_{i}}
.

Hence the statement holds for
F_{n}=F
and
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}|_{P_{n}}=\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
, as required.

Let
(F^{\prime},\varphi)
be the pair so obtained. An induction using that (
*
) is a distinguished square shows that
\varphi_{x_{i}}
is an isomorphism for
1\leq i\leq n
. The base case
i=1
follows from the canonical map
F^{\prime}[\varnothing]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}\rightarrow F[\varnothing]_{\vartriangleleft}
being
id_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}
. The induction step follows from
F^{\prime}[\partial x_{i}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}\rightarrow F[\partial x_{i}]_{\vartriangleleft}
being induced by the isomorphisms
\varphi_{y}
for
y<x_{i}
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

In the setting of Proposition
4.10
, given sieves
X\subset Y
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}[Y]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[Y]_{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}[X]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[X]_{\vartriangleleft}}}

commutes, where the arrows are given by functoriality of limits, i.e. the vertical ones are obtained by restricting the indexing category and the horizontal ones are induced by
\varphi
.

As a consequence we obtain the following variant of condition (iv) from Definition
4.5
:

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category and
F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a cellular diagram of shape
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
. Suppose given a diagram of sieve inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}

with
Y\smallsetminus X=Y^{\prime}\smallsetminus X^{\prime}
. Then the induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[Y^{\prime}]_{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[Y]_{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[X^{\prime}]_{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[X]_{\vartriangleleft}}}

is a pullback square.

Proof.

First, we consider the case where
Y\smallsetminus X=Y^{\prime}\smallsetminus X^{\prime}
has a single element, say
y
. Let
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
be a linearization of
(P,\leq)
such that
x\vartriangleleft^{\prime}y
for all
x\in X^{\prime}
. By Remark
4.11
, diagram (
*
) is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[Y^{\prime}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[Y]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[X^{\prime}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[X]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}

which is a pullback square by Definition
4.5
(iv).

The general case follows inductively using the above case and the pasting law for pullbacks.
∎

This can be further generalized as follows:

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category and
F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a cellular diagram of shape
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a subset of
\text{Sieve}(P)
closed under intersections. Then the family of projection maps

(F[\cup\mathcal{S}]\longrightarrow F[X])_{X\in\mathcal{S}}

is a limit cone over the diagram indexed by the poset
(\mathcal{S},\subset)^{\text{op}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the cardinality of
\mathcal{S}
. The claim is trivial for
\mathcal{S}=\varnothing
. For
n\geq 1
, assume that the claim holds for
0
, …,
n-1
and suppose given
\mathcal{S}
of cardinality
n
. Let
Z
be a maximal element of
\mathcal{S}
. Then
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}\smallsetminus\{Z\}
is closed under intersections and, by Lemma
4.12
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\cup\mathcal{S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[Z]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[(\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cap Z]}}

is a pullback square. Suppose given
a\in C
and a cone

(a\overset{\theta_{X}}{\longrightarrow}F[X])_{X\in\mathcal{S}}

over
F[-]:(\mathcal{S},\subset)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow C
. By the induction hypothesis, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[Z]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[(\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cap Z].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\theta_{X})_{X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Z}}

Hence there exists a unique arrow
f:a\rightarrow F[\cup\mathcal{S}]
such that
\theta_{Z}
equals

a\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}F[\cup\mathcal{S}]\longrightarrow F[Z]

and
(\theta_{X})_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
equals

a\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}F[\cup\mathcal{S}]\longrightarrow F[\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}].

This means that
f
is the unique arrow
a\rightarrow F[\cup\mathcal{S}]
defining a factorization of
(\theta_{X})_{X\in\mathcal{S}}
through
(F[\cup\mathcal{S}]\rightarrow F[X])_{X\in\mathcal{S}}
.
∎

4.2.1
Pasting cellular diagrams

We will now describe how to construct cellular diagrams of a given shape
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
by gluing, in a suitable sense, cellular diagrams whose shapes are sieves in
(P,\leq)
. The main tool will be the following lemma, which combines Proposition
4.6
(c) with the transport procedure described in Proposition
4.10
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)\in\text{LFPos}
and suppose that
x\in P
is maximal with respect to
\leq
. Then the diagram of strong sieve embeddings (see Definition
4.9
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\leq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\smallsetminus\{x\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}}

is mapped under
\text{cd}(-,\mathcal{C})
to a pullback square of sets.

Proof.

Using that
x
is maximal for
\leq
, choose a linearization
\blacktriangleleft
of
(P,\leq)
with respect to which
x
is the top element. Then we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((\partial x,\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((x^{\leq},\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((\partial x,\leq,\vartriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((x^{\leq},\leq,\vartriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((P\smallsetminus\{x\},\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((P,\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((P\smallsetminus\{x\},\leq,\vartriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((P,\leq,\vartriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the horizontal arrows connecting the front and back faces are the bijections from Proposition
4.10
, and the arrows in the front and back faces are obtained by applying the functor
\text{cd}(-,\mathcal{C})
. Now, the front face is a cartesian square if and only if the back one is cartesian. To conclude, we note that by the remark in Proposition
4.6
(c),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((\partial x,\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((x^{\leq},\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((P\smallsetminus\{x\},\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}
