{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}((P,\leq,\blacktriangleleft),\mathcal{C})}}

is a pullback square.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)\in\text{LFPos}
and let
A
,
B\subset P
be sieves such that
A\cup B=P
. Then the diagram of strong sieve embeddings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\cap B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}}

is mapped under
\text{cd}(-,\mathcal{C})
to a pullback square of sets.

Proof.

For the general case, we proceed by induction on the cardinality, say
n
, of
P\smallsetminus A
. If
A=P
, the claim holds trivially. For
n\geq 1
, suppose that the claim holds for
0
, …,
n-1
. If
A\subset B
, we have
B=P
, in which case the claim holds. Otherwise, there exists an element
z\in A\smallsetminus B
which is maximal in
(P,\leq)
. Then we have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\cap B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\smallsetminus\{z\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\smallsetminus\{z\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

of strong sieve embeddings. By the induction hypothesis, the left square is mapped to a pullback of sets under
\text{cd}(-,\mathcal{C})
. To see that the same holds for the right square, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\smallsetminus\{z\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\smallsetminus\{z\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\leq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P.}

By Lemma
4.14
, both the left and the outer composite squares are mapped under
\text{cd}(-,\mathcal{C})
to pullbacks of sets, so the same holds for the right one, which corresponds to the right square in (
*
). It follows that
\text{cd}(-,P)
maps the outer square in (
*
) to a pullback of sets, so the claim holds for
n
.
∎

4.2.2
Cellular diagrams indexed by products

Suppose given
n\geq 1
and finite posets
(P_{1},\leq_{1})
, …,
(P_{n},\leq_{n})
. For
x_{1}\in P_{1}
, …,
x_{n}\in P_{n}
and a subset
I\subset\{1,...,n\}
, we let

\partial_{I}(x_{1},...,x_{n})=d_{I}(x_{1})\times\cdots\times d_{I}(x_{n})

where
d_{I}(x_{i})
equals
\partial x_{i}
if
i\in I
, and
x_{i}^{\leq_{i}}
otherwise.

Denote by
\mathscr{P}^{*}(A)
the poset, via the inclusion order, of nonempty subsets of a set
A
.

Note that the subset

\mathcal{S}(x_{1},...,x_{n})\coloneqq\{\partial_{I}(x_{1},...,x_{n})\mid I\in\mathscr{P}^{*}(\{1,...,n\})\}

of
\text{Sieve}(P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n})
is closed under intersections and satisfies

\bigcup\mathcal{S}(x_{1},...,x_{n})=\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n}).

Now, consider a linearization
\vartriangleleft
of
P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n}
, a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, and, denoting by
\leq
the product of the orders
\leq_{1}
, …,
\leq_{n}
, a cellular diagram
F:(P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n},\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of shape
(P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n},\leq,\vartriangleleft)
. This induces a length-
1
display map

F(x_{1},...,x_{n})=F[(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}]\longrightarrow F[\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})]

(as in Definition
4.5
(iii)) and, by Proposition
4.13
, a limit cone

(F[\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})]\longrightarrow F[\partial_{I}(x_{1},...,x_{n})])_{I\in\mathscr{P}^{*}(\{1,...,n\})}.

Construction 4.16
.

In the above setting, suppose we are also given
y_{1}\in P_{1}
, …,
y_{n}\in P_{n}
and a natural transformation

\psi:F[\partial_{\bullet}(x_{1},...,x_{n})]\Longrightarrow F[\partial_{\bullet}(y_{1},...,y_{n})]

between functors
\mathscr{P}(\{1,...,n\})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. This induces a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x_{1},...,x_{n})=F[(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(y_{1},...,y_{n})=F[(y_{1},...,y_{n})^{\leq}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(y_{1},...,y_{n})].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\varnothing}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{I\neq\varnothing}\psi_{I}}

When we discuss multimorphisms of contextual categories, diagrams of this form will play a key role as they will allow us to relate distinguished squares in the domain contextual categories to those in the codomain one. The property of such a diagram that will be most useful to us is that, while it may not be a distinguished square, being distinguished is independent, in a sense explained below, of the choice of linearization of
P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n}
.

Suppose given another linearization
\vartriangleleft^{\prime}
of
P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n}
. Let
F^{\prime}:(P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n},\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and
\varphi:F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F
be as in Proposition
4.10
. Let
\psi^{\prime}
be the composite natural transformation

F^{\prime}[\partial_{\bullet}(x_{1},...,x_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}\overset{\varphi}{\Longrightarrow}F[\partial_{\bullet}(x_{1},...,x_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft}\overset{\psi}{\Longrightarrow}F[\partial_{\bullet}(y_{1},...,y_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft}\overset{\varphi^{-1}}{\Longrightarrow}F[\partial_{\bullet}(x_{1},...,x_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}

between functors
\mathscr{P}(\{1,...,n\})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Then (
*
) is a distinguished square if and only if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(x_{1},...,x_{n})=F^{\prime}[(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(y_{1},...,y_{n})=F^{\prime}[(y_{1},...,y_{n})^{\leq}]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}[\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}[\partial(y_{1},...,y_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}_{\varnothing}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{I\neq\varnothing}\psi^{\prime}_{I}}

is distinguished.

Indeed, if (
*
) is distinguished, then (
**
) being distinguished follows from commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(x_{1},...,x_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(y_{1},...,y_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(x_{1},...,x_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(y_{1},...,y_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\partial(y_{1},...,y_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}[\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}[\partial(y_{1},...,y_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft^{\prime}},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{(y_{1},...,y_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}_{\varnothing}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{I\neq\varnothing}\psi^{\prime}_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{I\neq\varnothing}\psi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{s\in\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})}\varphi_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{s\in\partial(y_{1},...,y_{n})}\varphi_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\varnothing}}

whose left and right faces are distinguished squares, and the pasting law for distinguished pullbacks. If (
**
) is distinguished, we obtain that so is (
*
) by using a similar argument with the cube where each arrow from the front face to the back face is replaced by its inverse (for example,
\varphi_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})}
is replaced by
\varphi^{-1}_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})}
).

The following will be crucial when relating exponentials to multimorphisms of contextual categories.

For
n\geq 0
, we let
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
be the precontextual category with underlying category the poset
\{0<\cdots<n\}^{\text{op}}
, length function
i\mapsto i
, all arrows as display maps, and no distinguished squares.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a contextual category. Given
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)\in\text{LFPos}
and
n\geq 0
, consider the function

\alpha_{n,P}:\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})\longrightarrow\text{Fun}((\{1<\cdots<n\}\times P)^{\text{op}},C)

given by sending
F:P^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}
to the functor adjunct to the composite

P^{\text{op}}\overset{F}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}\overset{(-)_{\varheartsuit}}{\longrightarrow}\text{Fun}(\{0<\cdots<n\}^{\text{op}},C)\overset{\text{restriction}}{\longrightarrow}\text{Fun}(\{1<\cdots<n\}^{\text{op}},\mathcal{C}).

Then, viewing the poset product
\{1<\cdots<n\}\times P
as a linearized poset via the lexicographic product between
\{1<\cdots<n\}
and
(P,\vartriangleleft)
, the map
\alpha_{n,P}
defines a bijection between
\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})
and
\text{cd}(\{1<\cdots<n\}\times P,\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

The idea is to work inductively on both
P
and
n
using that, denoting by
\top
the top element of
(P,\vartriangleleft)
, we can express cellular diagrams of shape
\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P
by combining:

(i)

The explicit procedure for extending cellular diagrams along the sieve embedding

\partial(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P)=(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P)\smallsetminus\{(n,\top)\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P

given by Proposition
4.6
(c).

(ii)

The expression of
\partial(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P)
as a union of sieves as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\partial P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\partial P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P)}

of the form required in Proposition
4.15
.

More precisely, we will prove by induction on
N
that the claim holds for all
n\geq 0
and
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)\in\text{LFPos}
such that
n\cdot\sharp P=N
.

For
N=0
, either
n=0
or
P=\varnothing
. If
P=\varnothing
, the claim holds trivially. If
n=0
, then the claim holds as
\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P=\varnothing
and, on the other hand,
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}
is the terminal contextual category
\mathcal{Z}
(see Example
4.8
), so
\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})
is a singleton.

Given
N\geq 1
, suppose that the claim holds for
0
, …,
N-1
. Consider
n
and
P
such that
n\cdot\sharp P=N
.

Since
\alpha_{\bullet,\bullet}
is a natural transformation between functors
\mathbb{N}^{\text{op}}\times\text{LFPos}^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{Set}
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times P,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\partial P,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\partial P,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\partial P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\partial P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{op}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the isomorphisms between the front and back faces are given by the induction hypothesis. We will describe a bijection
\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\textbf{n+1}^{\text{op}}})\rightarrow\text{cd}(\{1,...,n\}\times P,\mathcal{C})
given by restriction of
\alpha_{n,P}
and completing (
*
) into a commutative cube.

Let
Q=\partial(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P)=(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P)\smallsetminus\{(n,\top)\}
. By Proposition
4.15
, we have a bijection

\text{cd}(Q,\mathcal{C})\cong\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\partial P,\mathcal{C})\times_{\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\partial P,\mathcal{C})}\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times P,\mathcal{C})

compatible with the respective canonical maps from
\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P,\mathcal{C})
.

Hence by taking pullbacks in the front and back faces of (
*
) we obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\{1,\ldots,n\}\times P,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(\partial P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})\times_{\text{cd}(\partial P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}})}\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{cd}(Q,\mathcal{C}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Denoting by
\alpha
the inverse of the horizontal arrow in (
**
), we will construct for each
F\in\text{cd}(Q,\mathcal{C})
a bijection between
\psi^{-1}(F)
and
\varphi^{-1}(\alpha(F))
. We will do this by describing the fibers of
\varphi
and
\psi
:

•

Let
F\in\text{cd}(Q,\mathcal{C})
. An element of
\psi^{-1}(F)
corresponds, by Proposition
4.6
(c), to a length-
1
display map with codomain
F[\partial(n,\top)]
. On the other hand, the latter fits into a distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(n,\top)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\partial\top]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\top^{\leq}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\partial\top].}

We then obtain (see Definition
3.1
) a bijection between
\psi^{-1}(F)
and the set of relative length-
1
display maps of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\partial\top]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\top^{\leq}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\times\partial\top].}

•

Let
(G,H)\in\text{cd}(\partial P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}})\times_{\text{cd}(\partial P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}})}\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}})
. Explicitly, we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial P^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

where
G
,
H
are cellular diagrams and the right vertical arrow is the morphism of contextual categories induced by the inclusion
\iota:\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
of precontextual categories. An element of
\varphi^{-1}(G,H)
is then a cellular diagram
K:P^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}
filling (
***
) in the sense that the top and bottom triangles commute.

By Proposition
4.6
(c), such a
K
is characterized by a choice of length-
1
display map

\pi:S\twoheadrightarrow G[\partial\top]

whose top level,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\varheartsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\partial\top]_{\varheartsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

has the following property: the restriction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\varheartsuit}\circ\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\partial\top]_{\varheartsuit}\circ\iota=H[\partial\top]_{\varheartsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\iota}

is such that
S_{\varheartsuit}\circ\iota=H(\top)_{\varheartsuit}
and
\pi\iota
equals the length-
1
display map
H(\top)\rightarrow H[\partial\top]
. From this and the construction of display maps in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}
it can be checked that the data of such a
K
corresponds to a relative length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\partial\top]_{\varheartsuit}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\top)_{\varheartsuit}(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[\partial\top]_{\varheartsuit}(n-1)=H[\partial\top]_{\varheartsuit}(n-1).}

When
(G,H)=\alpha(F)
, diagram (B) becomes diagram (A), so we obtain a bijection
\beta:\text{cd}(P,\mathcal{C}^{\textbf{n+1}^{\text{op}}})\rightarrow\text{cd}(\{1,...,n\}\times P,\mathcal{C})
completing (
**
) into a commutative square. It follows from the construction that
\beta
is given by restriction of
\alpha_{n,P}

-
details were omitted. This concludes the induction step.
∎

4.2.3
A lemma on cellular diagrams in a contextual category associated with a category with attributes

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with attributes and
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
a linearized finite poset. We let
U:\text{cont}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be the forgetful functor.

Lemma 4.18
.

Suppose that
F
,
G:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\text{cont}(\mathcal{C})
are
P
-shaped cellular diagrams in
\mathcal{C}
such that
UF=UG
. Then
F=G
.

Proof.

We will prove by induction on
N\geq 0
that if
X
is a sieve in
(P,\leq)
of cardinality at most
N
, then
F[X]=G[X]
and, for any inclusion
X\subset Y
of such sieves, the induced maps
F[Y]\rightarrow F[X]
and
G[Y]\rightarrow G[X]
are equal.

For
N=0
the claim holds as
F[\varnothing]
and
G[\varnothing]
are both the distinguished terminal object of
\text{cont}(\mathcal{C})
. Let
N\geq 1
and suppose that the claim holds for
0
, …,
N-1
. Suppose that
X\subset P
is a sieve with
N
elements, and let
\top
be the top element of
X
with respect to
\vartriangleleft
. Consider the distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\top)=F[\top^{\leq}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(\top)=G[\top^{\leq}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[X\smallsetminus\{\top\}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F[\partial\top],}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[X\smallsetminus\{\top\}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G[\partial\top].}}

By assumption, the cones

(UF(\top)\longrightarrow UF(x))_{x\in\partial\top},

(UF(\top)\longrightarrow UF(x))_{x\in\partial\top}

in
C
are equal. Also, by the induction hypothesis the cones

(U(F[\partial\top])\longrightarrow U(F[x^{\leq}])=UF(x))_{x\in\partial\top},

(U(G[\partial\top])\longrightarrow U(G[x^{\leq}])=UG(x))_{x\in\partial\top}

are equal. It follows that the images under
U
of the arrows
F(\top)\rightarrow F[\partial\top]
and
G(\top)\rightarrow G[\partial\top]
in the above diagrams are equal. But
F(\top)
is obtained by adjoining
UF(\top)\rightarrow U(F[\partial\top])
to
F[\partial\top]
to the chain of morphisms
F[\partial\top]
; similarly,
G(\top)
is obtained by adjoining
UG(\top)\rightarrow U(G[\partial\top])
to
G[\partial\top]
. Hence
F(\top)=G(\top)
. Since, by the induction hypothesis, the lower horizontal arrows in the above squares are also equal, we conclude that the two squares are equal.

Moreover, suppose given a proper sieve
Y\subset X
. Let us prove that the arrows
F[X]\rightarrow F[Y]
and
G[X]\rightarrow G[Y]
are equal.

If
X\neq\top^{\leq}
, we note that it suffices to prove that the pairs of arrows

(F[X]\rightarrow F[Y\cap\partial X],\;\;F[X]\rightarrow F[Y\cap\top^{\leq}]),

(G[X]\rightarrow G[Y\cap\partial X],\;\;G[X]\rightarrow G[Y\cap\top^{\leq}])

are equal. But, using the induction hypothesis and what we proved above, the composites
F[X]\rightarrow F[\partial X]\rightarrow F[Y\cap\partial X]
and
G[X]\rightarrow G[\partial X]\rightarrow G[Y\cap\partial X]
are equal. We conclude similarly (now using that
X\neq\top^{\leq}
) that the composites
F[X]\rightarrow F[\top^{\leq}]\rightarrow F[Y\cap\top^{\leq}]
and
G[X]\rightarrow G[\top^{\leq}]\rightarrow G[Y\cap\top^{\leq}]
are equal.

Now, suppose that
X=\top^{\leq}
. Then
Y\subset\partial\top
, and from what was proved above and the induction hypothesis we obtain that the composites
F[\top^{\leq}]\rightarrow F[\partial\top]\rightarrow F[Y]
and
G[\top^{\leq}]\rightarrow G[\partial\top]\rightarrow G[Y]
are equal.

This concludes the induction step.

As for each
x\rightarrow y
in
P
the arrow
F(y)\rightarrow F(x)
is equal to the morphism
F[y^{\leq}]\rightarrow F[x^{\leq}]
induced by the inclusion of sieves
x^{\leq}\subset y^{\leq}
, and similarly for
G(y)\rightarrow G(x)
, we conclude that
F=G
.
∎

4.3
Generalized cellular diagrams

Definition 4.19
.

A poset
(P,\leq)
is said to be
locally finite
if
x^{\leq}=\{y\in P\mid y\leq x\}
is finite for all
x\in P
.

For a locally finite poset
P
, we write
\text{cub}(P)
for the set of all pairs
(x,y)\in P\times P
such that
x
,
y
have a common upper bound, that is, there exists
z\in P
with
x
,
y\leq z
.

We define a
local linearization
of
(P,\leq)
as a binary relation
\vartriangleleft\;\subset\text{cub}(P)\subset P\times P
such that for each
x\in P
, the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
to
x^{\leq}
is a linearization of the latter (Definition
4.1
).

A triple
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
where
(P,\leq)
is a locally finite poset and
\vartriangleleft
is a local linearization of
(P,\leq)
will be referred to as a
locally linearized poset
.

Remark 4.20
.

(i)

Note that a local linearization is completely determined, as it is contained
\text{cub}(P)
, by its restrictions to the sub-posets
x^{\leq}
. Hence a local linearization can be equivalently described as a family
(\vartriangleleft_{x})_{x}
consisting of a linearization of
x^{\leq}
for each
x\in P
such that if
a
,
b
are bounded above by both
x
and
y
, then
a\vartriangleleft_{x}b
if and only if
a\vartriangleleft_{y}b
.

(ii)

If
(P,\leq)
is a finite poset, then a local linearization of it is a linearization precisely when it is a linear order. On the other hand, a linearization of it will only be a local linearization when it is contained in
\text{cub}(P)
, which happens precisely when there is a top element with respect to
\leq
.

It can be proved that any local linearization of
(P,\leq)
extends to a linearization, and that if
\vartriangleleft
is a linearization, then
\vartriangleleft\cap\;\text{cub}(P)
is a local linearization.

Definition 4.21
.

Let
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
be a locally linearized poset. For a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, a
cellular diagram
of shape
P
in
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that for all
x\in P
, the restriction
F|_{x^{\leq}}:x^{\leq,op}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a cellular diagram with respect to the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
to
x^{\leq}
.

For a (finite) sieve
X\subset(P,\leq)
such that
X\subset x^{\leq}
for some
x\in P
, the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
to
X
is a linearization of the latter. We write
F[X]_{\vartriangleleft}
, or just
F[X]
when
\vartriangleleft
is implicit, for
F[X]_{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}
where
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
is the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
to
X
(equivalently, we can let
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
be the restriction of
\vartriangleleft
to
x^{\leq}
for any
x
such that
X\subset x^{\leq}
).

Remark 4.22
.

It can be proved by induction on the cardinality of
P
, using Lemma
4.14
, that if
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
is a linearized finite poset, then
F:(P,\leq)^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is cellular in the sense of Definition
4.5
precisely when it is cellular, as in Definition
4.21
, with respect to the locally linearized poset
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft\cap\;\text{cub}(P))
.

Notation 4.23
.

For a locally linearized poset
P
and a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\text{cd}(\textbf{P},\mathcal{C})
for the set of cellular diagrams of shape
P
in
\mathcal{C}
. Note that we have a functor
\text{cd}(\textbf{P},-):\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
.

In principle, this clashes with Definition
4.9
, but Remark
4.22
, if
(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
shows that this abuse of notation is harmless.

Proposition 4.24
.

For any locally linearized poset
P
, the functor
\text{cd}(\textbf{P},-):\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
is representable.

Sketch of proof.

An element
x
of a locally finite poset
X
has a
height
, say
h(x)
, defined as the largest
k\geq 0
such that there exists a chain
x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{k}=x
. It can then be checked that
h(x)=\text{max}\{h(y)\mid y<x\}+1
. In particular, if two distinct elements have the same height, they are not comparable.

Write
\textbf{P}=(P,\leq,\vartriangleleft)
and, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P_{n}\subset P
consist of all elements of height at most
n
. We also regard
P_{n}
as a locally linearized poset via the restrictions
\leq|_{P_{n}}
and
\vartriangleleft\cap\text{cub}(P_{n})
.

Let us prove by induction that
\text{cd}(P_{n},-):\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
is representable for all
n\geq 0
.

Firstly, as
P_{0}
is empty,
\text{cd}(P_{0},-)
is represented by the initial contextual category,
\mathcal{O}_{0}
, whose only object is the distinguished terminal one. Given
1\leq n<\omega
, assume that the claim holds for
0
, …,
n-1
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a contextual category that represents
\text{cd}(P_{n-1},-)
, and fix a universal cellular diagram
I:P_{n-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
.

Note that if
x\in P
has height
n
, then
\partial x\subset P_{n-1}
is linearly ordered with respect to
\vartriangleleft
. By Remark
4.22
, the restriction of a cellular diagram
F:P_{n-1}^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
to
\partial x
is cellular in the sense of Definition
4.5
. Hence this restriction has a distinguished limit
F[\partial x]
. Now, extending
F
to a cellular diagram
P_{n}^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
amounts to choosing for each height-
n
element
x
an object
c_{x}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\partial(c_{x})=F[\partial x]
. On the other hand, in terms of the bijection
\text{cd}(P_{n-1},\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
, the morphism
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
corresponding to
F
sends
I[\partial x]
to
F[\partial x]
.

Define
\mathcal{B}
as the precontextual category obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by freely adjoining, for each height-
n
element
x\in P
, an object
a_{x}
such that
\partial(a_{x})=I[\partial x]
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=L(\mathcal{B})
(recall that
L
is a left adjoint of
\text{Cont}\hookrightarrow\text{Precont}
), and let
b_{x}
be the image of
a_{x}
under the canonical morphism
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Then we have
\text{cd}(P_{n},-)\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},-)
with the corresponding universal cellular diagram
I^{\prime}:P_{n}^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
given on
P_{n-1}
by
I
and on height-
n
elements by
I^{\prime}(x)=a_{x}
.

This concludes the induction step.

Finally, let us construct a representing object for
\text{cd}(\textbf{P},-)
. Consider a sequence
\mathcal{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}\rightarrow\cdots
of contextual categories and contextual functors where
\mathcal{A}_{n}
represents
\text{cd}(P_{n},-)
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
is induced, via the universal property of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, by the composite
P_{n}^{\text{op}}\hookrightarrow P_{n+1}^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
.

Letting
\mathcal{A}_{\omega}
be a colimit of
(\mathcal{A}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}_{\omega},-)\cong\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{n<\omega}\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}_{n},-)\cong\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{n<\omega}\text{cd}(P_{n},-).

Now, note that
\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{n<\omega}\text{cd}(P_{n},\mathcal{C})
is in bijection, naturally in
\mathcal{C}
, with the set of all functors
P^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
whose restriction to
P_{n}^{\text{op}}
is cellular for all
n
. But the latter are precisely the cellular diagrams of shape
P
in
\mathcal{C}
; indeed,
P^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
being cellular only depends, by definition, on the restrictions
x^{\leq,op}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for
x\in P
.
∎

Remark 4.25
.

A similar construction can be performed for any locally finite direct category
D
; see
[
37
]
. The corresponding contextual category,
\mathcal{C}(D)
, is such that
|\mathcal{C}(D)|^{\text{op}}
is equivalent to the category
\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
of finitely presentable presheaves on
D
. In particular,
\mathcal{A}
from the proof of Proposition
4.24
is such that
|\mathcal{A}|^{\text{op}}\simeq\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
.

5
Multimorphisms

In this section, we define and study multimorphisms of contextual categories. We start by introducing, as a preliminary step, the concept of a
pre-multimorphism
(Definition
5.6
). These will be, for contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
22
22

22

More generally,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
can be any precontextual categories
functors
\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
that are cellular with respect to the product of trees of display maps
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
. Passing from pre-multimorphisms to multimorphisms (Definition
5.8
) amounts to imposing a form of compatibility with distinguished squares in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
.

After that, we discuss the effect on (pre-)multimorphisms of changing the choice of local linearization of the poset
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
; the main example to keep in mind is moving across the lexicographic local linearizations corresponding to different permutations of
\{1,...,n\}
. This will lead to the concept of a
permutative morphism
, a kind of transformation between multimorphisms that will be one of the key ingredients for studying the symmetry of multimorphisms (and, later, of the symmetry and associativity of the tensor product of contextual categories).

In §
5.4
, we will see how specializing to the case
n=2
recovers the bimorphisms from §
3
. In §
5.5
, which is comparatively quite technical, we construct a natural isomorphism between categories of multimorphisms

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{1};\mathcal{C})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}).

Finally, in §
5.6
, we check that
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}\cong\mathcal{C}^{L\mathcal{A}}
and
\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}(L\mathcal{A}_{1},...,L\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
where
L:\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
is the reflection functor.

5.1
Cellular diagrams out of products of trees

Definition 5.1
.

By a
tree
we will mean a poset
(T,\leq)
such that for all
x\in T
,
x^{\leq}
is finite and linearly ordered by the restriction of
\leq
.

Note that
\leq
is itself a local linearization of
(T,\leq)
.

Our reason for considering trees is that they arise from contextual categories in the following way:

Definition 5.2
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a contextual category, its
associated tree
, denoted by
T(\mathcal{A})
, is the poset whose elements are the objects of
\mathcal{A}
of length
\geq 1
, and where
x\leq y
if and only if there exists a display map
y\rightarrow x
.

When defining multimorphisms of contextual categories, the key ingredient will be the following definition and proposition, which allow us to consider cellular diagrams out of a product of trees of the form
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
. The latter, equipped with the local linearization which we describe next, will encode an important part of the structure of multimorphisms
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Consider trees
T_{1}
, …,
T_{n}
. We define their
lexicographic product
as the binary relation
\blacktriangleleft
on
T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{n}
given by
(x_{1},...,x_{n})\blacktriangleleft(y_{1},...,y_{n})
if and only if either

•

(x_{1},...,x_{n})=(y_{1},...,y_{n})
, or

•

there exists
i\in\{1,...,n+1\}
such that (i)
x_{j}=y_{j}
for
j<i
, (ii)
x_{i}<y_{i}
, and (iii) for
j>i
,
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
are comparable, i.e.
x_{j}\leq y_{j}
or
y_{j}\leq x_{j}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

In the notation of Definition
5.3
,
\blacktriangleleft
is a local linearization of
T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{n}
.

Proof.

Firstly, note that if
(x_{1},...,x_{n})\blacktriangleleft(y_{1},...,y_{n})
, then
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are comparable for
1\leq i\leq n
, so
(\max\{x_{i},y_{i}\})_{i}
is a common upper bound of
(x_{i})_{i}
and
(y_{i})_{i}
. Hence
\blacktriangleleft\;\subset\text{cub}(T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{n})
.

Also, given
x_{i}\in T_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, as
x_{i}^{\leq}
is linearly ordered, the restriction of
\blacktriangleleft
to
(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}
is the usual lexicographic (linear) order on
x_{1}^{\leq}\times\cdots\times x_{n}^{\leq}
.

Finally, if
(x_{i})_{i}\leq(y_{i})_{i}
, then
(x_{i})_{i}\blacktriangleleft(y_{i})_{i}
: in the notation of Definition
5.3
, either
(x_{i})_{i}=(y_{i})_{i}
, or we can take
j
as the smallest
i
such that
x_{i}<y_{i}
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Any permutation of
\{1,...,n\}
defines a local linearization of
T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{n}
: for
\sigma\in S_{n}
, we can consider the local linearization
\blacktriangleleft
of
T_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times T_{\sigma(n)}
given by Proposition
5.4
, and then pull it back along the poset isomorphism

T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{n}\cong T_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times T_{\sigma(n)}.

5.2
Multimorphisms

In what follows, we let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
be precontextual categories and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\vartriangleleft
be a local linearization of the (locally finite) poset
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
. A
\vartriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism
from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
F:|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\times\cdots\times|\mathcal{A}_{n}|\rightarrow|\mathcal{C}|
such that the restriction

F|_{(T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n}))^{\text{op}}}:(T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n}))^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

is a cellular diagram with respect to
\vartriangleleft
.

The set of all such pre-multimorphisms will be denoted by
\text{Hom}^{?}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
, and the corresponding full subcategory of
|\mathcal{C}|^{|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\times\cdots\times|\mathcal{|}A_{n}|}
by
\textbf{Hom}^{?}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
.
23
23

23

The question mark is meant to indicate that a pre-multimorphism may or may not be a multimorphism in the sense of Definition
5.8
.

Recalling that
\blacktriangleleft
denotes the lexicographic local linearization of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
from Definition
5.3
, we refer to a
\blacktriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism simply as a
pre-multimorphism
.

For a subset
I\subset\{1,...,n\}
we let
\chi_{I}:\{1,...,n\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be its characteristic function, i.e.
\chi_{I}(x)
equals
1
if
x\in I
, and
0
otherwise. Given
x_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
x_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, we write
\partial(x_{1},...,x_{n})
and
\partial_{I}(x_{1},...,x_{n})
for the respective constructions, as defined in §
4.2.2
, in the case of the finite poset
x_{1}^{\leq}\times\cdots\times x_{n}^{\leq}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}\subset T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.

Construction 5.7
.

In the above setting, suppose given for each
i=1
, …,
n
a commutative square
Q_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{0}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{0}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{1}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{1}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{0}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{1}_{i}}

where
p_{i}
,
q_{i}
are length-
1
display maps.

Since
\partial x_{i}^{0}=x_{i}^{1\leq}
and
\partial y_{i}^{0}=y_{i}^{1\leq}
for each
I\subset\{1,...,n\}
, in the notation of
4.2.2
we have

F[\partial_{I}(x_{1}^{0},...,x_{n}^{0})]=F(x^{\chi_{I}(1)}_{1},...,x^{\chi_{I}(n)}_{n}),

F[\partial_{I}(y_{1}^{0},...,y_{n}^{0})]=F(y^{\chi_{I}(1)}_{1},...,y^{\chi_{I}(n)}_{n}).

Now, the arrows
f^{0}_{i}
,
f^{1}_{i}
define a natural transformation

\psi:F[\partial_{\bullet}(x^{0}_{1},...,x^{0}_{n})]\Longrightarrow F[\partial_{\bullet}(y^{0}_{1},...,y^{0}_{n})]

between functors
\mathscr{P}(\{1,...,n\})^{\text{op}}\rightarrow C
, namely, whose
I
-component is
F(f^{\chi_{I}(1)}_{1},...,f^{\chi_{I}(n)}_{n})
.

We then obtain, as in Construction
4.16
, a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x^{0}_{1},...,x^{0}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(y^{0}_{1},...,y^{0}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(x^{0}_{1},...,x^{0}_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(y^{0}_{1},...,y^{0}_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\varnothing}=F(f^{0}_{1},...,f^{0}_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underleftarrow{\text{lim }}_{I\neq\varnothing}\psi_{I}}

We will denote (
*
) by
F\langle Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\vartriangleleft}
.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\vartriangleleft
be a local linearization of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
. We define a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped multimorphism
from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
as a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism
F:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
that satisfies the following: given for each
i=1
, …,
n
a length-
1
distinguished square
Q_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, the commutative square
F_{\langle}Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\vartriangleleft}
(see Construction
5.7
) is distinguished.

The set of
\vartriangleleft
-shaped multimorphisms from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
will be denoted by
\text{Hom}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
, and the corresponding full subcategory of
|\mathcal{C}|^{|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\times\cdots\times|\mathcal{A}_{n}|}
by
\textbf{Hom}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
.

Recalling that
\blacktriangleleft
denotes the lexicographic local linearization of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
from Definition
5.3
, we refer to a
\blacktriangleleft
-shaped multimorphism simply as a
multimorphism
. If we want to emphasize
n
, we refer to a (
\vartriangleleft
-shaped) multimorphism as a (
\vartriangleleft
-shaped
)
n
-ary morphism
.

The following criterion will be useful for checking whether a given pre-multimorphism is a multimorphism:

Proposition 5.9
.

In the notation of definitions
5.6
and
5.8
, suppose that
F
is a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
. For
F
to be a multimorphism, it suffices that
F\langle Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\vartriangleleft}
be distinguished for all sequences
(Q_{i})_{i}
of length-
1
distinguished squares such that for some
j\in\{1,...,n\}
,
Q_{i}
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{b}}

for all
i\neq j
.

Proof.

This follows from functoriality of
F\langle-\rangle_{\vartriangleleft}:\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{C})
and the fact that
\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n})
is spanned by those arrows corresponding to a sequence of squares
(Q_{1},...,Q_{n})
as in the statement.
∎

5.3
Transporting (pre-)multimorphisms

We now describe a construction, derived from Proposition
4.10
, that will play a central role in our study of (pre-)multimorphisms. It will be used not only to express, in a straightforward way, symmetry of the tensor product of contextual categories, but also as a technical component of our proof of associativity.

Generally, if
F:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a (pre-)multimorphism with respect to a given local linearization
\vartriangleleft
of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
, it will not remain a (pre-)multimorphism if we replace
\vartriangleleft
by another local linearization, say
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
. Still, we can canonically modify, in a certain sense,
F
to a
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
-shaped (pre-)multimorphism
\overline{F}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
; more precisely, we will have an isomorphism
F\cong\overline{F}
characterized essentially as in Proposition
4.10
. In Proposition
5.11
we prove that
\overline{F}
is a multimorphism precisely when
F
is a multimorphism.

Construction 5.10
.

Let
F:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be, as in Definition
5.6
, a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism. Let
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
be another local linearization of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.

For each
x_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
x_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
we can associate to the
\vartriangleleft
-shaped
24
24

24

We also write
\vartriangleleft
for the restriction
\vartriangleleft|_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}
.
cellular diagram

F|_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}:(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq,op}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C},

via Proposition
4.10
, a natural isomorphism of the form

\eta^{x_{1},...,x_{n}}:G^{x_{1},...,x_{n}}\Longrightarrow F|_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}

where, in particular,
G^{x_{1},...,x_{n}}
is cellular with respect to
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
.

It can be verified that for
y_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
y_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
such that
y_{i}\leq x_{i}
for each
i
, we have

\eta^{x_{1},...,x_{n}}_{y_{1},...,y_{n}}=\eta^{y_{1},...,y_{n}}_{y_{1},...,y_{n}}.

Thus for fixed
y_{1}
, …,
y_{n}
the arrow
\eta^{x_{1},...,x_{n}}_{y_{1},...,y_{n}}
does not depend on
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
and, denoting
\eta^{y_{1},...,y_{n}}_{y_{1},...,y_{n}}
by
\eta_{y_{1},...,y_{n}}
, the family

(\eta_{y_{1},...,y_{n}})_{(y_{1},...,y_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}

is a natural transformation from a uniquely determined
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
-shaped pre-multimorphism
\overline{F}
from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
. Explicitly,
\overline{F}
maps an object
(x_{1},...,x_{n})
to the domain of
\eta_{x_{1},...,x_{n}}
, and an arrow
(f_{1},...,f_{n}):(x_{1},...,x_{n})\rightarrow(y_{1},...,y_{n})
in
\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}
to the unique dashed arrow making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(x_{1},...,x_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x_{1},...,x_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(y_{1},...,y_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(y_{1},...,y_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x_{1},...,x_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f_{1},...,f_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{y_{1},...,y_{n}}}

commute.

Proposition 5.11
.

In the notation of Construction
5.10
, if
F
is a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped multimorphism, then
\overline{F}
is a
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
-shaped multimorphism.

Proof.

Suppose given for each
i=1
, …,
n
a length-
1
distinguished square
Q_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. As remarked in Construction
4.16
,
\overline{F}\langle Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}
is distinguished if and only if
F\langle Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\vartriangleleft}
is distinguished. By assumption, the latter is distinguished, hence so is the former, as required.
∎

The following result is an immediate application of the characterization from Proposition
4.10
to the setting of
(pre-)
multimorphisms:

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
\vartriangleleft
and
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
be local linearizations of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
. For a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped (pre-)multimorphism
F:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, there exists a unique pair
(\overline{F},\eta)
consisting of a
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
-shaped (pre-)multimorphism
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
-shaped multimorphism
\overline{F}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and a natural isomorphism
\eta:\overline{F}\Rightarrow F
such that for all
(a_{1},...,a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}
, the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(a_{1},...,a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a_{1},...,a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}[\partial(a_{1},...,a_{n})]_{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(a_{1},...,a_{n})]_{\vartriangleleft},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a_{1},...,a_{n}}}

where the bottom square is induced by
\eta
and functoriality of limits, is distinguished. Explicitly,
\overline{F}
and
\eta
are given by Construction
5.10
. ∎

Notation 5.13
.

We will write
\mathfrak{T}_{\vartriangleleft,\overline{\vartriangleleft}}
for both functions
F\mapsto\overline{F}
from Proposition
5.12
, that is,
\text{Hom}^{?}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Hom}^{?}_{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
and its co/restriction
\text{Hom}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Hom}_{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
.

Corollary 5.14
.

The following hold for any local linearizations
\vartriangleleft
,
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
,
\overline{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}
of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
:

•

\mathfrak{T}_{\vartriangleleft,\vartriangleleft}
is the identity map;

•

\mathfrak{T}_{\overline{\vartriangleleft},\overline{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}}\circ\mathfrak{T}_{\vartriangleleft,\overline{\vartriangleleft}}=\mathfrak{T}_{\vartriangleleft,\overline{\overline{\vartriangleleft}}}
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{T}_{\vartriangleleft,\overline{\vartriangleleft}}
is bijective for all
\vartriangleleft
,
\overline{\vartriangleleft}
.

5.3.1
Symmetry of multimorphisms

Consider precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, and a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
.

As usual,
\blacktriangleleft
denotes the lexicographic local linearization of the cartesian product of trees
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
(see Definition
5.3
).

For a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, we write
\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}
for the lexicographic local linearization of
T(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n})
. Given a local linearization
\vartriangleleft
of
T(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n})
we let
\sigma^{*}(\vartriangleleft)
be the local linearization of
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
induced from
\vartriangleleft
via the cartesian symmetry isomorphism
\underline{\sigma}:T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow T(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n})
.

Firstly, note that a functor
F:\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a
\vartriangleleft
-shaped (pre-)multimorphism if and only if
F\circ\underline{\sigma}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a
\sigma^{*}(\vartriangleleft)
-multimorphism. Hence we have bijections

\text{Hom}^{?}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{C})\overset{-\circ\underline{\sigma}}{\cong}\text{Hom}^{?}_{\sigma^{*}(\vartriangleleft)}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}),\qquad\text{Hom}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{C})\overset{-\circ\underline{\sigma}}{\cong}\text{Hom}_{\sigma^{*}(\vartriangleleft)}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}).

Now, specializing to the case where
\vartriangleleft
is
\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}
and composing with
\mathfrak{T}_{\sigma^{*}(\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}),\blacktriangleleft}
(see Corollary
5.14
), we obtain a bijection

\mathfrak{T}_{\sigma^{*}(\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}),\blacktriangleleft}(-\circ\underline{\sigma}):\text{Hom}^{?}_{\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\text{Hom}^{?}_{\blacktriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}),

which in turn co/restricts to a bijection
\text{Hom}_{\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}_{\blacktriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
. However, note that these maps do not encode an important related structure, namely, the natural isomorphism
\eta
from Proposition
5.12
. Precisely, that proposition yields a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

where
\overline{F}=\mathfrak{T}_{\sigma^{*}(\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}),\blacktriangleleft}(F\circ\underline{\sigma})
, and we have:

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
F:\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a (pre-)multimorphism (that is, with respect to the lexicographic local linearization
\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}
). Then there exists a unique pair
(F^{\prime},\phi)
consisting of a (pre-)multimorphism
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and a natural isomorphism
\phi
as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

such that for all
(a_{1},...,a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}
, the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(a_{1},...,a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a_{\sigma 1},...,a_{\sigma n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}[\partial(a_{1},...,a_{n})]_{\blacktriangleleft}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(a_{\sigma 1},...,a_{\sigma n})]_{\blacktriangleleft_{\sigma}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a_{1},...,a_{n}}}

where the bottom square is induced by
\phi
and functoriality of limits, is distinguished. In the notation of the above discussion, such
F^{\prime}
,
\phi
are
\overline{F}
,
\eta
, respectively. ∎

Definition 5.16
.

A triple
(F,F^{\prime},\phi)
as in Proposition
5.15
— which we depict as in (
*
) — will be referred to as a
shuffling diagram
.

Remark 5.17
.

The fact that length-
1
distinguished squares in
\mathcal{C}
are closed under horizontal composition implies, due to the defining condition on a shuffling diagram encoded by (
**
) in Proposition
5.15
, that shuffling diagrams are closed under horizontal composition: suppose given
\sigma
,
\tau\in S_{n}
and a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\tau 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\tau n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma^{-1}\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where the left and right triangles are shuffling diagrams; then the composite outer triangle, i.e.
(F,F^{\prime\prime},\;\phi\underline{\sigma}\circ\phi^{\prime})
, is a shuffling diagram.

It also follows from Proposition
5.15
that shuffling diagrams are stable under pre- and post-composition with contextual functors in the following sense: if a triangle of the form (
\texttt{*})
is a shuffling diagram, then for any contextual functors

U_{1}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1},\quad...,\quad U_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n},\quad V:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime},

the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{\sigma n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\circ F^{\prime}\circ(U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\circ F\circ(U_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times U_{\sigma n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\phi(U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n})}

is a shuffling diagram.

We now have:

Proposition 5.18
.

Consider
n\geq 1
, precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, and
\sigma\in S_{n}
. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};-)\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};-)

between functors
\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
whose
\mathcal{C}
-component sends a multimorphism
F:\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
to the unique multimorphism
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
that fits into a shuffling diagram
(F,F^{\prime},\phi)
(see (
*
) above).

Moreover, this isomorphism is natural in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
: for precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
and morphisms
U_{1}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
U_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n};\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\;\circ\;(U_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times U_{\sigma n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\;\circ\;(U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes. ∎

Remark 5.19
.

In the notation of Proposition
5.15
, if
a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
have length
1
, then the objects in the bottom row of (
**
) are both
1_{\mathcal{C}}
, which implies that
\phi_{a_{1},...,a_{n}}
is an identity morphism.

In particular, if
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
only have objects of length at most
1
, then
\phi
is the identity natural transformation. We will come back to this observation in §
10.1
when studying the
Cat
-enriched structure of
Cont
.

5.3.2
Permutative morphisms

We will now discuss an alternative way of encoding the content of shuffling diagrams (Definition
5.16
). The idea is that while shuffling diagrams compare multimorphisms with a common codomain, the approach introduced below allows us to compare multimorphisms whose codomains are connected by suitable maps, the
permutative morphisms
.

Definition 5.20
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
be contextual categories. We define
\text{Perm}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
as the following category:

•

an object is a triple
(\mathcal{B},\sigma,F)
consisting of a contextual category
\mathcal{B}
, a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, and a multimorphism
F\in\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};\mathcal{B})
.

•

a morphism from
(\mathcal{B},\sigma,F)
to
(\mathcal{C},\tau,G)
, which we call a
permutative morphism
, is a contextual functor
P:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that there exists a (necessarily unique) shuffling diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\tau 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\tau n};}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma^{-1}\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

•

permutative morphisms are composed in the usual way for contextual functors — the validity of this definition, that is, a composite of permutative morphisms being permutative, follows from Remark
5.17
.

It is useful to picture a permutative morphism as a square such as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\tau 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\tau n}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma^{-1}\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Composition is then expressed by horizontally pasting the given natural transformations.

The reason why we consider permutative morphisms is the following result:

Proposition 5.21
.

Suppose given permutative morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Then
P\circ F=Q\circ F
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.15
, the shuffling diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

are equal.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

The above proposition is particularly useful if — as will occur when we discuss
n
-ary tensor products of contextual categories —
F
is known to be a universal multimorphism out of
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
, as this will imply
P=Q
.

5.4
Comparison with bimorphisms (as introduced previously)

We will now verify that
2
-ary morphisms of contextual categories, in the sense of Definition
5.8
, are precisely the bimorphisms from Definition
3.25
.

Proposition 5.23
.

Given contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, a functor
H:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathcal{C}
in the sense of Definition
3.25
if and only if it is a multimorphism (or
2
-ary morphism) in the sense of Definition
5.8
.

Proof.

(
\implies
)

Assume that
H
is a bimorphism. Firstly, let us show that
H
is a
\blacktriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism (where
\blacktriangleleft
is the lexicographic local linearization on
T(\mathcal{A})\times T(\mathcal{B})
). For
a\in A
,
b\in B
, let

a=a_{m}\twoheadrightarrow a_{m-1}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow a_{1}\twoheadrightarrow a_{0}=1_{\mathcal{A}},

b=b_{n}\twoheadrightarrow b_{n-1}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow b_{1}\twoheadrightarrow b_{0}=1_{\mathcal{B}}

be the respective towers of length-
1
display maps. Let

\underline{a}:\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}

\underline{b}:\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}

be the morphisms of precontextual categories that classify
a
and
b
, respectively. Restricting
H
along
\underline{a}\times\underline{b}
yields a bimorphism
H^{\prime}:(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}},\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and by Proposition
3.26
we get a morphism
F:\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}}
. As the latter restricts to a cellular diagram
\{1<\cdots<n\}^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}}
, it follows from Proposition
4.17
that
H^{\prime}
restricts to a cellular diagram

\{1,...,m\}^{\text{op}}\times\{1,...,n\}^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

where the left-hand side is endowed with the lexicographic local linearization. It follows that
H
is a
\blacktriangleleft
-shaped pre-multimorphism. To check that it is a multimorphism, we use the criterion from Proposition
5.9
.

On the one hand, suppose that we have length-
1
distinguished squares
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, resp., of the forms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{{}^{\prime}0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{{}^{\prime}1},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{1}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

Then
H\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
equals the comparison square between the gap map of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

and that of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}0},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}0},b^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}1},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}1},b^{1}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

Since
H
corresponds to a morphism
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
(Proposition
3.26
), it follows from the construction of display maps in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
(given by indexed sorts, Definition
3.3
) that
H\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
is a distinguished square.

On the other hand, consider length-
1
distinguished squares
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, resp., of the forms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{{}^{\prime}0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{1},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{{}^{\prime}1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Then
H_{\vartriangleleft}(Q_{1},Q_{2})
is the comparison square between the gap map of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

and that of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{{}^{\prime}0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{{}^{\prime}1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{{}^{\prime}0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{{}^{\prime}1}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q^{\prime})}

By condition (iii) from Proposition
3.26
,
H\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
is distinguished. This concludes the proof that
H
is a
2
-ary morphism.

(
\impliedby
)
Conversely, assume that
H
is a
2
-ary morphism. Writing
A
,
C
for the underlying categories of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{C}
, let
F:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow C^{A}
be the adjunct of
H
. By the correspondence between bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and morphisms
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
(Proposition
3.26
), it suffices to prove that
F
factors through
D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})\subset C^{A}
and defines a morphism of categories with attributes
\text{att}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
. For the first part, note that given a display map
a^{\prime}\rightarrow a
in
\mathcal{A}
and
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the arrow

H(a^{\prime},b)\overset{H(p,id)}{\longrightarrow}H(a,b)

is a display map since
(a,b)\blacktriangleleft(a^{\prime},b)
and
H|_{(a^{\prime},b)^{\leq}}
is cellular with respect to
\blacktriangleleft
. Now, let us prove that
F:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow D(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
defines a morphism of categories with attributes.

•

F
preserves the distinguished terminal object.

F(1_{\mathcal{B}})=H(-,1_{\mathcal{B}})
is the constant functor on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
as
H
is
2
-ary.

•

F
sends length-
1
display maps to indexed sorts.
Let
q:b^{0}\rightarrow b^{1}
be a length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{B}
. If
p:a^{0}\rightarrow a^{1}
is a length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{A}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(b^{0})(a^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(b^{1})(a^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(b^{0})(a^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(b^{1})(a^{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b^{0})(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)_{a^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b^{1})(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)_{a^{0}}}

equals

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{1}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

which is a relative length-
1
display map. Moreover, if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{{}^{\prime}0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{{}^{\prime}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is a length-
1
distinguished square in
\mathcal{A}
, then the induced comparison square between the gap map of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{0},b^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{1},b^{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

and that of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}0},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}0},b^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}1},b^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(a^{{}^{\prime}1},b^{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p^{\prime},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,q)}

equals
H\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
where
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
are, respectively,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{{}^{\prime}0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{{}^{\prime}1},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{1}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

As
H
is
2
-ary,
H\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
is distinguished.

•

F
sends length-
1
distinguished squares to indexed distinguished squares.
Suppose given a length-
1
distinguished square
Q_{1}
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{{}^{\prime}0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{{}^{\prime}1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{B}
. By the previous item,
F(q)
and
F(q^{\prime})
are indexed sorts, so
F(Q_{1})
satisfies condition IDS(i) from Remark
3.10
. On the other hand, IDS(ii) follows from the fact that, letting
p:a^{0}\rightarrow a_{1}
be a length-
1
display map in
\mathcal{A}
and
Q_{2}
be the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

we have that
H\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
is distinguished.

This concludes the proof that
F
defines a morphism
\text{att}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})
.
∎

5.5
Relating multimorphisms and exponentials

In what follows, we let
n\geq 2
and consider precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
. To avoid ambiguity, we will denote their respective underlying categories by
A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}
,
C
.

Notation 5.24
.

We will write
\text{Hom}(\cdots)
for a set of multimorphisms
\text{Hom}_{\blacktriangleleft}(\cdots)
, that is, of
\blacktriangleleft
-shaped multimorphisms where
\blacktriangleleft
is the lexicographic local linearization. Similarly, we write
\textbf{Hom}(\cdots)
for the category
\textbf{Hom}_{\blacktriangleleft}(\cdots)
.

Our goal is to construct an isomorphism of categories

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}).

We start by defining

\widehat{(-)}:|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|^{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}\longrightarrow C^{A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}

as the composite functor

|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|^{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}\longrightarrow(C^{A_{1}})^{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}C^{A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}

where the left arrow is given by composition with the forgetful functor

|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|\overset{(-)_{\varheartsuit}}{\longrightarrow}D(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow C^{A_{1}}.

Note that
\widehat{(-)}
is full-and-faithful. We will prove that its action on objects restricts to a bijection

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}),

thus inducing the desired isomorphism of categories. Firstly, we verify (Lemma
5.26
) that
\widehat{(-)}
restricts to a map

\eta:\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\longrightarrow\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}).

Then we prove in Lemma
5.27
that for
F\in\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
, the pre-multimorphism
\eta(F)
is
n
-ary precisely when
F
is
(n-1)
-ary. In particular,
\eta
restricts to a map

\eta^{\prime}:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}).

In Proposition
5.29
, we construct a map

\phi:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})

such that
\eta\phi(H)=H
for all
H
. Using the other direction of Lemma
5.27
, we have that
\phi
restricts to a map

\phi^{\prime}:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}),

which is then a section of
\eta^{\prime}
. In Theorem
5.31
we conclude the proof that
\eta^{\prime}
is bijective by verifying that it is injective.

Notation 5.25
.

For emphasis, we will now use distinct notations for the lexicographic local linearizations of distinct products of trees: we write
\blacktriangleleft
for the one on
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
, and
\overline{\blacktriangleleft}
for the one on
T(\mathcal{A}_{2})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.

Lemma 5.26
.

For each
F\in\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
we have
\widehat{F}\in\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Let
F\in\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
. For
x_{1}\in A_{1}
, …,
x_{n}\in A_{n}
, by assumption,

\widehat{F}|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}:(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq,op}\longrightarrow|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|

is a cellular diagram in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
with respect to
\overline{\blacktriangleleft}
. Now, the inclusion of precontextual categories
x_{1}^{\leq}\cup\{1_{\mathcal{A}}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}
induces a morphism
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{x_{1}^{\leq,op}}
. Composing the latter with (
*
) we obtain a cellular diagram

(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq,op}\longrightarrow|\mathcal{C}^{x_{1}^{\leq,op}}|.

in
\mathcal{C}^{x_{1}^{\leq,op}}
. By Proposition
4.17
, the functor

x_{1}^{\leq,op}\times(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq,op}\longrightarrow C

corresponding to (
**
) is a cellular diagram in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to the lexicographic product between
x_{1}^{\leq}
and
((x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq},\overline{\blacktriangleleft})
. But this product is canonically isomorphic to the tree
((x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq},\blacktriangleleft)
, and it can be checked that the corresponding functor
(x_{1},...,x_{n})^{\leq,op}\longrightarrow|\mathcal{C}|
coincides with the restriction of
\widehat{F}
. We conclude that
\widehat{F}
is a pre-multimorphism.
∎

We let
\eta:\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\rightarrow\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
be the restriction of
\widehat{(-)}
provided by Lemma
5.26
.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
F
be an (
\overline{\blacktriangleleft}
-shaped) pre-multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
. Then
F
is a multimorphism if and only if
\widehat{F}
is a (
\blacktriangleleft
-shaped) multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Write
H
for
\widehat{F}
. By Lemma
5.26
,
H
is a pre-multimorphism. To study when it is a multimorphism, suppose given for each
i=1
, …,
n
a length-
1
distinguished square
Q_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{0}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{0}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{1}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{1}_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{0}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{1}_{i}}

We will characterize when
H\langle Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
is a distinguished square. By functoriality of
H\langle Q_{1},...,Q_{n}\rangle_{\blacktriangleleft}
in
Q_{1}
, …,
Q_{n}
, it will be sufficient to do this in the following two cases:

(Case 1)

For each
i=2
, …,
n
, the square
Q_{i}
is of the form

, …,

∎

Notation 5.28
.

We let

Proposition 5.29
.

Let

. For

, …,

.

(ii)

, …,

, is an indexed sort. Let

be a length-

. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[a^{\leq}\times S_{i+1}(x_{2},...,x_{n})]}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[\partial a\times S_{i+1}(x_{2},...,x_{n})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[\partial a\times S_{i}(x_{2},...,x_{n})]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}

is such that the vertical arrows are display maps (as

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow a^{\leq}\times S_{i}(x_{2},...,x_{n}),

\displaystyle\partial a\times S_{i+1}(x_{2},...,x_{n})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow a^{\leq}\times S_{i+1}(x_{2},...,x_{n})

are initial segment embeddings with respect to
\blacktriangleleft
). Moreover, writing

for the unique element of

It follows that the gap map of (
*
) is the arrow

induced by the inclusion

. For IS(ii), consider a length-

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in

. Then the comparison square between the gap maps associated with

) and, for

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial x_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

is a distinguished square. Thus IS(ii) holds and

is an indexed sort.

Hence from the formula in condition (ii) we obtain a function
F_{0}:\text{Ob}(A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n})\rightarrow\text{Ob}(|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|)
. Note that

\displaystyle F_{0}(x_{2},...,x_{n})_{\ell(x_{2})\cdots\ell(x_{n})}

\displaystyle=H[(-)^{\leq}\times S_{\ell(x_{2})\cdots\ell(x_{n})}(x_{2},...,x_{n})]

\displaystyle=H[(-)^{\leq}\times(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}]

\displaystyle=H[(-,x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}]

\displaystyle=H(-,x_{2},...,x_{n})

as
H
is a pre-multimorphism.

We extend
F_{0}
to a functor
F:A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\rightarrow|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|
by sending each
(f_{2},...,f_{n}):(x_{2},...,x_{n})\rightarrow(x^{\prime}_{2},...,x^{\prime}_{n})
to the morphism

F_{0}(x_{2},...,x_{n})\longrightarrow F_{0}(x^{\prime}_{2},...,x^{\prime}_{n})

given by the natural transformation

F_{0}(x_{2},...,x_{n})_{\ell(x_{2})\cdots\ell(x_{n})}=H(-,x_{2},...,x_{n})\Longrightarrow H(-,x^{\prime}_{2},...,x^{\prime}_{n})=F_{0}(x^{\prime}_{2},...,x^{\prime}_{n})_{\ell(x^{\prime}_{2})\cdots\ell(x^{\prime}_{n})}

whose
a
-component is
H(id_{a},f_{2},...,f_{n})
for each
