.

To check that
F
is a pre-multimorphism, let us prove that by induction on
N\geq 0
that for every “sub-principal” sieve
V\subset T_{2}\times\cdots\times T_{n}
— by which we mean a sieve that is contained in some
(a_{2},...,a_{n})^{\leq}
— of cardinality
N
, say whose elements are

(x_{2}^{1},...,x_{n}^{1})\;\overline{\blacktriangleleft}\;(x_{2}^{2},...,x_{n}^{2})\;\overline{\blacktriangleleft}\;\cdots\;\overline{\blacktriangleleft}\;(x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N}),

the diagram

F|_{V}:V^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}

is cellular (with respect to the restriction of
\overline{\blacktriangleleft}
to
V
) with the following distinguished limit:
F|_{V}[V]
is given by the chain of natural transformations

H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{k}]\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{1}]\longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{0}]

where
V_{i}=\{(x_{2}^{1},...,x_{n}^{1}),...,(x_{2}^{i},...,x_{n}^{i})\}
; for
1\leq i\leq N
, the projection map

F_{V}[V]\longrightarrow F(x_{2}^{i},...,x_{n}^{i})

is the natural transformation

H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{k}]\Longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times(x_{2}^{i},...,x_{n}^{i})^{\leq}]

whose
a
-component is induced by the inclusion
(a,x_{2}^{i},...,x_{n}^{i})^{\leq}\subset a^{\leq}\times V_{n}
.

For
N=0
the claim holds as the empty diagram
F|_{\varnothing}
is cellular with distinguished limit the chain whose single entry is
\text{ct}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}=H[\varnothing]
. For
N\geq 1
, suppose that the claim holds for
0
, …,
N-1
. Consider a sub-principal sieve
V\subset T_{2}\times\cdots\times T_{n}
with
N
elements, and let
x_{j}^{i}
be as above. Denote by
\top
the top element
(x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})
of
V
with respect to
\overline{\blacktriangleleft}
. By assumption,
F|_{V\smallsetminus\{\top\}}:(V\smallsetminus\{\top\})^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|
is cellular and
F|_{V\smallsetminus\{\top\}}[V\smallsetminus\{\top\}]
is given by the chain described above.

From the definition of
F
and the induction hypothesis applied to the sieve
\partial\top
, the morphism

F(\top)\longrightarrow F|_{V\smallsetminus\{\top\}}[\partial\top]

is a length-
1
display map. Hence by Proposition
4.6
(c),
F|_{V}
is cellular. Moreover,
F|_{V}[V]
is given by the distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[V]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[V\smallsetminus\{\top\}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial\top].}

It follows that:

•

By the induction hypothesis,
\partial(F[V])=F[V\smallsetminus\{\top\}]
corresponds to the chain

H[(-)^{\leq}\times(V\smallsetminus\{\top\})]=H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{N-1}]\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{1}]\longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{0}].

•

F[V]
is obtained by adjoining to the above chain the left vertical natural transformation in the indexed distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(-,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[(-)^{\leq}\times(V\smallsetminus\{\top\})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[(-)^{\leq}\times\partial\top].}}

We claim that this diagram equals

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[(-)^{\leq}\times V]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[(-)^{\leq}\times(x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})^{\leq}]=H(-,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[(-)^{\leq}\times(V\smallsetminus\{\top\})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H[(-)^{\leq}\times\partial\top]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

where the four arrows are given by functoriality of distinguished limits of restrictions of
H
. For that, it suffices to check that (
*
) is an indexed distinguished square. We have already verified that
\pi
is an indexed sort, so condition IDS(i) from Remark
3.10
holds. For IDS(ii), suppose given a length-
1

p:a\rightarrow\partial a
in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. The gap map of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[a^{\leq}\times V]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[a^{\leq}\times(V\smallsetminus\{\top\})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial a\times V]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial a\times(V\smallsetminus\{\top\})]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\partial a}}

is the length-
1
display map
H[a^{\leq}\times V]\twoheadrightarrow H[a^{\leq}\times V\smallsetminus\{(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})\}]
, and that of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[a^{\leq}\times\partial\top]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\partial a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[(\partial a)^{\leq}\times\partial\top]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}_{\partial a}}

is
H(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})\twoheadrightarrow H[(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})^{\leq}\smallsetminus\{(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})\}]
. As

is a pre-multimorphism, condition (iv) from Definition
4.5
implies that the induced comparison square,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[a^{\leq}\times V]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[a^{\leq}\times V\smallsetminus\{(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})\}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})^{\leq}\smallsetminus\{(a,x_{2}^{N},...,x_{n}^{N})\}],}

is distinguished. Hence IDS(ii) holds for (
*
).

•

From the above items, we have that
F[V]
is given by the chain

H[(-)^{\leq}\times V]=H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{N}]\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{1}]\longrightarrow H[(-)^{\leq}\times V_{0}],

as required. Also, it follows from the induction hypothesis (more precisely, the description of the cone of the distinguished limit of
F|_{V\smallsetminus\{\top\}}
) and the construction of diagram (
*
) that the associated limit cone from

to

has as its
(x_{2}^{i},...,x_{n}^{i})
-component the natural transformation

induced by the inclusion

.

This concludes the induction step, and it follows that
F
is a pre-multimorphism.
∎

Notation 5.30
.

Let
\phi:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
be the function sending
H
to the pre-multimorphism
F
obtained in Proposition
5.29
. By Lemma
5.27
,
\phi
restricts to a map

\phi^{\prime}:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}).

Theorem 5.31
.

The function
\eta^{\prime}:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
is bijective with inverse
\phi^{\prime}
. We thus obtain an isomorphism of categories
\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

By construction we have that
\eta^{\prime}\circ\phi^{\prime}
is the identity map. Hence it suffices to prove that
\eta^{\prime}
is injective.

Suppose given
F
,
G\in\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
such that
\eta(F)=\eta(G)
. Writing
U
for the forgetful functor
(-)_{\varheartsuit}:|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|\rightarrow D(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{C})
, by definition of
\eta
we have
UF=UG
. Then the desired equality
F=G
follows if we prove that
F
and
G
have the same action on objects. For that, note that for each
x_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
, …,
x_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, the restrictions
F|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}
,
G|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}:(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq,op}\rightarrow|\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}|
are cellular diagrams and
UF|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}=UG|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}
. Thus by Lemma
4.18
applied to the category with attributes
D_{\text{att}}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{C})
we have
F|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}=G|_{(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}}
. As
x_{2}
, …,
x_{n}
were arbitrary we conclude that
F_{\text{Ob}}=G_{\text{Ob}}
, as required.
∎

5.6
Using precontextual categories to describe multimorphisms and exponentials

Proposition 5.32
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a precontextual category and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category. Then the contextual functor
\mathcal{C}^{\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}:\mathcal{C}^{L(\mathcal{A})}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}}
induced by the reflection morphism
\iota_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow L(\mathcal{A})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
, consider the diagram of functions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}^{L(\mathcal{A})})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(L(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B},L(\mathcal{A});\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A};\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(L(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{B}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{B}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}\;\circ\;-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\;\circ\;(\iota_{\mathcal{A}}\times\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\;\circ\;(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}\times\iota_{\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\;\circ\;\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}

where the indicated isomorphisms are as follows:

-

the top left, top right, bottom left and bottom right ones are as in Theorem
5.31
;

-

the middle left and middle right ones are as in Proposition
5.18
.

Naturality of the isomorphism from Theorem
5.31
implies that the top and bottom squares commute, and by Proposition
5.18
so does the middle one. Hence the outer composite square commutes. But by the universal property of
\iota_{\mathcal{A}}
, the bottom arrow is an isomorphism, from which we conclude that so is the top one, as required.

As the isomorphism
\mathcal{C}^{\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}\circ-:\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}^{L(\mathcal{A})})\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}})
is natural in
\mathcal{B}
, it follows from the Yoneda lemma that
\mathcal{C}^{\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}
is an isomorphism, as desired.
∎

Corollary 5.33
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a precontextual category and
\mathcal{C}
a contextual category. Then the functor

\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Precont}}(L(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{-\circ\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}\textbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})

is an isomorphism of categories.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
5.32
and Remark
3.13
.
∎

Proposition 5.34
.

For precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, precomposition with
\iota_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\times\cdots\times\iota_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}
defines an isomorphism of categories

\textbf{Hom}(L(\mathcal{A}_{1}),...,L(\mathcal{A}_{n});\mathcal{C})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})

natural in
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
.

Proof.

Let us prove by induction on
n\geq 1
that the claim holds for all
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{C}
. The case
n=1
corresponds to Corollary
5.33
. Given
n=2
, suppose that the claim holds for
1
, …,
n-1
. Given
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}\in\text{Precont}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
, we have a chain of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\textbf{Hom}(L(\mathcal{A}_{1}),L(\mathcal{A}_{2}),...,L(\mathcal{A}_{n});\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(L(\mathcal{A}_{2}),...,L(\mathcal{A}_{n});\mathcal{C}^{L(\mathcal{A}_{1})})

(Theorem
5.31
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{L(\mathcal{A}_{1})})

(induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})

(Proposition
5.32
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}).

(Theorem
5.31
)

We leave it as an exercise to verify, using the definition of each of these maps, that the composite is indeed given by precomposition with
\iota_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\times\cdots\times\iota_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}
.
∎

6
The multicategory structure

We will now verify that the sets of multimorphisms
\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{B}):=\text{Hom}_{\blacktriangleleft}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{B})
introduced above can be used to define a multicategory structure that extends the category of contextual categories and contextual functors. We refer the reader to
[
30
]
, Ch. 2 and 3, or
[
22
]
for an introduction to the theory of multicategories. Familiarity with their relationship to monoidal categories via the concept of a representable multicategory is recommended but not strictly required (see Remark
7.1
).

Consider the multicategory
\mathscr{Cat}
where
25
25

25

Succinctly,
\mathscr{Cat}
is the multicategory represented by the cartesian monoidal structure on
Cat
.
: objects are the small categories; the hom-set from
(A_{1},...,A_{n})
to
B
is the set of functors
\text{Fun}(A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{n},B)
(for
n=0
, we have the set of functors from the terminal category
\textbf{1}=\{*\}
to
B
); and, given a category
C
, integers
k_{1}
, …,
k_{n}\geq 0
and, for
1\leq i\leq n
, small categories
B_{i}
,
A_{i}^{1}
, …,
A_{i}^{k_{i}}
, the composition function

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Fun}(B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{n},C)\times\text{Fun}(A_{1}^{1}\times\cdots\times A_{1}^{k_{1}},B_{1})\times\cdots\times\text{Fun}(A_{n}^{1},...,A_{n}^{k_{n}},B_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Fun}(A_{1}^{1}\times...\times A_{1}^{k_{1}}\times...\times A_{n}^{1}\times...\times A_{n}^{k_{n}},C)}

maps
(G,F_{1},...,F_{n})
to the functor
G\circ(F_{1},...,F_{n})
defined in the usual way from the cartesian structure on
Cat
, i.e. it is given on objects and arrows by
26
26

26

Here we make an abuse of notation: if
k_{i}=0
, then there are no variables of the form
x^{j}_{i}
on the left-hand side, but on the right-hand side we evaluate
F_{i}
at the unique object or arrow of the terminal category. If
k_{i}=0
for all
i
, then the functor maps
*
(resp.
id_{*}
) to
G(F_{1}(*),...,F_{n}(*))
(resp.
G(F_{1}(id_{*}),...,F_{n}(id_{*}))
).

(x_{1}^{1},...,x_{1}^{k_{1}},...,x_{n}^{1},...,x_{n}^{k_{n}})\longmapsto G(F_{1}(x_{1}^{1},...,x_{1}^{k_{1}}),...,F_{n}(x_{n}^{1},...,x_{n}^{k_{n}})).

Consider the map
|-|:\text{Ob}(\text{Cont})\rightarrow\text{Ob}(\text{Cat})
that sends a contextual category to its underlying category. We have a multicategory structure
\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}
on
\text{Ob}(\text{Cont})
where the hom-sets are

\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{B})=\text{Fun}(|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\times\cdots\times|\mathcal{A}_{n}|,|\mathcal{B}|)

and composition is computed as in
\mathscr{Cat}
. In particular, for each
\mathcal{B}
we have
\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}(\;;\mathcal{B})=\text{Fun}(\textbf{1},|\mathcal{B}|)
.

We will prove that restricting to the subsets

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{B})\subset\text{Fun}(|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\times\cdots\times|\mathcal{A}_{n}|,|\mathcal{B}|)

gives a sub-multicategory of
\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}
. However, that also requires a suitable definition of
0
-ary morphism to a contextual category.

Definition 6.1
.

For a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, we let
\text{Hom}(\;;\mathcal{C})
be the set of all functors
\textbf{1}\rightarrow|\mathcal{C}|
that send the single object to a length-
1
object in
\mathcal{C}
.

Writing
\text{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{C})
for the set of length-
1
objects, we have
\text{Hom}(\;;\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{C})
.

To obtain the desired sub-multicategory, it now suffices to prove that multimorphisms are closed under multicomposition in
\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}
.

Notation 6.2
.

In what follows, when talking about (pre)contextual categories such as
\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
\mathcal{B}
, etc., we will write
A_{i}
,
B
, etc. for their underlying categories.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose given
n
,
k\geq 1
, precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{B}_{k}
,
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{C}
are contextual categories, and multimorphisms

F:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C},\qquad G:(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n}.

Then

F(-,...,-,G(-,...,-)):A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{n-1}\times B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\longrightarrow C

is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n-1},\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Recursively applying Theorem
5.31
,
F
defines a morphism
\overline{F}:\mathcal{A}_{n}\longrightarrow(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}}
. Thus

\overline{F}\circ G:B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\longrightarrow|(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}}|

is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})
to
(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}}
. Another recursive application of Theorem
5.31
gives a multimorphism

H:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n-1},\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

It can be checked from the construction of the isomorphism in Theorem
5.31
that
H=F(-,...,-,G(-,...,-))
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Our goal is to generalize Lemma
6.3
by allowing
G
to be, more generally, a multimorphism
(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{i}
for any
i\in\{1,...,n\}
. However, the asymmetric form of Theorem
5.31
prevents us from directly arguing as in the above proof. Informally, the strategy will be to use the symmetry construction from §
5.3
to move
i
to the last position, apply Lemma
6.3
to obtain a multimorphism by precomposition with
G
, and use the symmetry construction again to move
i
back to the original position. The main technical work lies in checking that the two applications of the symmetry construction are, in a certain sense, inverse to each other.

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose given
n
,
k\geq 1
, precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{B}_{k}
,
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}
are contextual categories, and multimorphisms

F:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C},\qquad G:(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}.

Then the functor

F(G(-,...,-),-,...,-):B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\longrightarrow C

is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\times A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\times B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}\times G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\text{Id}_{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where the horizontal arrows are symmetry isomorphisms (so the lower square commutes strictly) and the upper triangle is a shuffling diagram (Definition
5.16
). Then the following pasted triangle is also a shuffling diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\times B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(-,...,-,G(-,...,-))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G(-,...,-),-,...,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(\text{Id}_{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}\times G)}

Proof.

Write
H
for
F(G(-,...,-),-,...,-)
. We will prove by induction that the following holds for all
N\geq 0
: suppose given sieves
X\subset T(\mathcal{A}_{2})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
and
Y\subset T(\mathcal{B}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{B}_{k})
that are linearly ordered by the respective lexicographic local linearizations (Definition
5.3
) and satisfy
\sharp(Y\times X)=N
; then

H|_{Y\times X}:(Y\times X)^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow C

is cellular
27
27

27

With respect to the lexicographic product of the given linear orders on
Y
and
X
. It can be checked to coincide with the restriction to
Y\times X
of the lexicographic local linearization of
T(\mathcal{B}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{B}_{k})\times T(\mathcal{A}_{1})\times\cdots\times T(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.
and its distinguished limit is given by

H[Y\times X]=F[G[Y]^{\leq}\times X]

with associated cone having as components the canonical arrows

F[G[Y]^{\leq}\times X]\longrightarrow F[G[(b_{1},...,b_{k})^{\leq}]^{\leq}\times(a_{2},...,a_{n})^{\leq}]=F(G(b_{1},...,b_{k}),a_{2},...,a_{n})

for
(b_{1},...,b_{k},a_{2},...,a_{n})\in Y\times X
.

For
N=0
, the claim holds as
F[G[\varnothing]^{\leq}\times\varnothing]=F[\varnothing]=1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Given
N\geq 1
, assume that the claim holds for
0
, …,
N-1
, and consider
X
,
Y
of the required form with
\sharp(Y\times X)=N
. Let
(x_{2},...,x_{n})
and
(y_{1},...,y_{k})
be the top elements of
X
and
Y
with respect to the corresponding linear orders. By the induction hypothesis, recalling that
\partial X:=X\smallsetminus\{(x_{2},...,x_{n})\}
and
\partial Y:=Y\smallsetminus\{(y_{1},...,y_{k})\}
, we have that
H
is cellular on
\partial Y\times X
,
Y\times\partial X
and
\partial Y\times\partial X
; hence it is cellular on
\partial(Y\times X)=(\partial Y\times X)\cup(Y\times\partial X)
and the following square is distinguished:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial(Y\times X)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[Y\times\partial X]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial Y\times X]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial Y\times\partial X].}

By the induction hypothesis, the right and bottom arrows are equal, respectively, to those in the distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(G[Y]^{\leq}\times X)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[G(Y)^{\leq}\times\partial X]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[G[\partial Y]^{\leq}\times X]=F[\partial(G[Y])\times X]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[G[\partial Y]^{\leq}\times\partial X]=F[\partial(G[Y])\times\partial X].}

It follows that
H[\partial(Y\times X)]=F[\partial(G[Y]^{\leq}\times X)]
and the associated limit cones are equal. To verify that
H
is cellular on
Y\times X
and describe the corresponding distinguished limit cone, we consider two cases:

(1)

X=(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}
and
Y=(y_{1},...,y_{k})^{\leq}
.

In this case, it suffices to prove that the canonical arrow
H(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})\rightarrow H[\partial(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})]
is a length-
1
display map. But note that it equals

F(G(y_{1},...,y_{k}),x_{2},...,x_{n})\longrightarrow F[\partial(G(y_{1},...,y_{k}),x_{2},...,x_{n})],

which is a length-
1
display map as
F
is a multimorphism.

(2)

X\neq(x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}
or
Y\neq(y_{1},...,y_{k})^{\leq}
.

This is equivalent to
(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}
being a proper subset of
Y\times X
. By the induction hypothesis,
H
is cellular on
(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}
, hence on
\partial(X\times Y)\cup(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})^{\leq}=X\times Y
, and we have a distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[Y\times X]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial(Y\times X)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial(y_{1},...,y_{k},x_{2},...,x_{n})].}

By comparing the right and bottom arrows, it also follows from the induction hypothesis that this diagram equals the distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[G[Y]^{\leq}\times X]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G(y_{1},...,y_{k}),x_{2},...,x_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(G[Y]^{\leq}\times X)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(G(y_{1},...,y_{k}),x_{2},...,x_{n})].}

This also yields the desired description of the associated cone of
H[Y\times X]
.

This concludes the induction step. In particular, we have proved that
H\in\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})
. Let us check that it is a multimorphism.

Using the construction of shuffling diagrams from §
5.3.1
, we obtain from
F
a multimorphism
F^{\prime}:(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and a natural isomorphism
\phi:F^{\prime}\rightarrow F\circ\sigma
where
\sigma:A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\times A_{1}\rightarrow A_{1}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}
is the symmetry isomorphism, with the following property: for each
a_{1}\in A_{1}
, …,
a_{n}\in A_{n}
the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(a_{2},...,a_{n},a_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}[\partial(a_{2},...,a_{n},a_{1})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a_{2},...,a_{n},a_{1}}}

is distinguished of length
1
, where the bottom arrow is induced by
\phi
and functoriality of limits.

Now, let
H^{\prime}=F^{\prime}(-,...,-,G(-,...,-))
. Pasting
\phi
with
\text{Id}_{A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}}\times G
induces an isomorphism
\phi^{\prime}:H^{\prime}\rightarrow H\circ\sigma^{\prime}
where

\sigma^{\prime}:A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\times B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\longrightarrow B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}

is the symmetry isomorphism. By Lemma
6.3
,
H^{\prime}
is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})
to
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, for
a_{2}\in A_{2}
, …,
a_{n}\in A_{n}
,
b_{1}\in B_{1}
, …,
b_{k}\in B_{k}
of length
\geq 1
, the length-
1
distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(a_{2},...,a_{n},G(b_{1},...,b_{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G(b_{1},...,b_{k}),a_{2},...,a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}[\partial(a_{2},...,a_{n},G(b_{1},...,b_{k}))]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\partial(G(b_{1},...,b_{k}),a_{2},...,a_{n})]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a_{2},...,a_{n},G(b_{1},...,b_{k})}}

equals

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}(a_{2},...,a_{n},b_{1},...,b_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(b_{1},...,b_{k},a_{2},...,a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}[\partial(a_{2},...,a_{n},b_{1},...,b_{k})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H[\partial(b_{1},...,b_{k},a_{2},...,a_{n})],}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}_{a_{2},...,a_{n},b_{1},...,b_{k}}}

where the bottom arrow is induced by
\phi^{\prime}
and functoriality of limits.

We conclude from §
5.3.1
that
H
is also a multimorphism.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose given
n
,
k\geq 1
,
\;i\in\{1,...,n\}
, precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{B}_{k}
,
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}
are contextual categories, and multimorphisms

F:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C},\qquad G:(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{i}.

Then the functor

F(-,...,G(-,...,-),...,-):A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{i-1}\times B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\times A_{i+1}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\longrightarrow C

is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{i-1},\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
5.31
we obtain a multimorphism

\overline{F}:(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}}.

By Proposition
6.5
,

\overline{F}(G(-,...,-),-,...,-):B_{1}\times\cdots\times B_{k}\times A_{i+1}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\longrightarrow|(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}}|

is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}}
. Again using Theorem
5.31
, we obtain a multimorphism

H:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{i-1},\mathcal{B}_{1},...,\mathcal{B}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

It follows from the construction of the isomorphism in Theorem
5.31
that
H=F(-,...,G(-,...,-),...,-)
.
∎

We now verify the analogue of Proposition
6.6
for
k=0
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Suppose given
n\geq i\geq 1
, contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{C}
, and a multimorphism

F:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

Then for each length-
1
object
x
of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, the functor

F(-,...,x,...,-):A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{i-1}\times A_{i+1}\times\cdots\times A_{n}\longrightarrow C

is a multimorphism from
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{i-1},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n})
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

In what follows, we consider the precontextual category
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}
whose underlying category is the poset
o_{1}\rightarrow o_{0}
with
o_{0}
,
o_{1}
of length
0
,
1
, resp. Let
\underline{x}:\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{i}
be the unique morphism sending
o_{1}
to
x
.

From Theorem
5.31
we obtain a multimorphism

\overline{F}:(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}},

and Proposition
6.5
now yields a multimorphism

\overline{F}(\underline{x}(-),-,...,-):(\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}}.

By Theorem
5.31
we have a multimorphism

K:(\mathcal{A}_{i+1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow((\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}})^{\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}}.

But the inclusion functor
\{1\}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}
induces, by Proposition
3.17
, an isomorphism

((\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}})^{\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}}\cong((\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}})^{\{1\}}\cong(\cdots(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdots)^{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}}.

By composing
K
with this isomorphism and once again using Theorem
5.31
, we get a multimorphism

H:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{i-1},\mathcal{A}_{i+1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C},

which equals
F(-,...,x,...,-)
.
∎

Finally, Proposition
6.6
and Lemma
6.7
imply:

Theorem 6.8
.

The subsets

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{B})\subset\text{Fun}(|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\times\cdots\times|\mathcal{A}_{n}|,\;|\mathcal{B}|)=\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{B}),

with
n\geq 0
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}
ranging over all small contextual categories, are closed under multi-composition in
\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}
. In particular, they define a sub-multicategory of
\mathscr{Cont}_{fun}
.

Definition 6.9
.

We define the
multicategory of (small) contextual categories
as the multicategory obtained in Theorem
6.8
. It will be denoted by
\mathscr{Cont}
.

Remark 6.10
.

It is not difficult to prove from the results presented so far (particularly §
5.3
and the second part of Proposition
6.5
) that the isomorphisms from Proposition
5.18
realize
\mathscr{Cont}
as a symmetric multicategory (see
[
30
]
, Def. 2.2.21). However, this will follow (ultimately relying on the same preliminaries) from the symmetry of the monoidal structure from §
7
.

7
The monoidal structure

In this section, we will prove that for contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, the functor
\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};-):\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
is representable by a contextual category
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, the assignment
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mapsto\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
extends into a symmetric monoidal structure on
Cont
, and for
n\geq 1
and small contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, the functor

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};-):\text{Cont}\longrightarrow\text{Set}

is representable by
\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes(\cdots(\mathcal{A}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\cdots))
(or by the object obtained by associating the same factors in any other way). This will allow us to conclude indirectly that the multicategory
\mathscr{Cont}
from §
6
is representable (see
[
22
]
, Def. 8.1 and Th. 9.8) and symmetric (see
[
30
]
).

Remark 7.1
.

Conversely, we could start by proving, using similar techniques, that
\mathscr{Cont}
is a representable (closed) symmetric multicategory and use that to obtain a (closed) symmetric monoidal structure that
\mathscr{Cont}
; see the discussions in
[
39
, §3]
and
[
20
, Def. 3.3.1 and Prop. 3.3.2]
. This path is conceptually natural and is within reach from the results of §
6
(see Remark
6.10
), and carrying it out is an interesting exercise. In fact, our self-contained approach to finishing the construction of the monoidal structure can be seen as providing a supply of pre-universal multimorphisms (as in
[
30
]
, Def. 3.3.2) and coherences (compare Lemma
7.13
with condition (2:7) from
[
30
]
, Def. 2.2.21) that is sufficient to uniquely determine a representable symmetric multicategory structure. The reader familiar with representability of symmetric multicategories will observe that the coherences from Lemmas
7.10
,
7.11
and Propositions
7.14
,
7.15
are analogous to those in
[
22
, Def. 9.6]
extended to the symmetric case.

Definition 7.2
.

Given
n\geq 1
, a
tensor product
of a finite sequence of small contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is a contextual category
\mathcal{T}
endowed with an isomorphism

\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{T},-)\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};-)

of functors
\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Set}
. By the Yoneda lemma, this datum is equivalent to that of a multimorphism

F:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}

which is universal in the sense that for any multimorphism
G:(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, there exists a unique contextual functor
{G^{\prime}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}}
such that
G=G^{\prime}\circ F
. In particular,
\mathcal{T}
is unique up to unique isomorphism compatible with
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
.

We start by showing that binary tensor products exist.

Construction 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be contextual categories. We will obtain their tensor product in few steps using the existence of contextual categories that represent cellular diagrams (Proposition
4.24
) and the reflection functor
L:\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
.

Following Proposition
4.24
, let
M:(T(\mathcal{A})\times T(\mathcal{B}))^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
be a universal bimorphism. Taking a pushout of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(T(\mathcal{A})\times T(\mathcal{B}))^{\text{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\mathcal{A}|\times|\mathcal{B}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q},}}

note that as the left vertical arrow is bijective on objects, so is the right one. This allows us to regard
\mathcal{Q}
as a precontextual category with display maps and distinguished squares inherited from
\mathcal{P}
. We now have isomorphisms

\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(L(\mathcal{Q}),\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Precont}}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}^{?}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C})

natural in
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
. Under this correspondence, the functor
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
corresponding to a morphism
F:L(\mathcal{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a bimorphism precisely when for all distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a^{\prime},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, respectively, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial(M(a,b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial(M(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M(\partial a,b)\times_{M(\partial a,\partial b)}M(a,\partial b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M(\partial a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\times_{M(\partial a^{\prime},\partial b^{\prime})}M(a^{\prime},\partial b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(f,g^{\prime})\times_{M(f,g)}M(f^{\prime},g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces||}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces||}

is sent by
F
to a distinguished square. Letting
\mathcal{R}
be the precontextual category obtained from
L(\mathcal{Q})
by imposing that every square of the above form be distinguished, we have

\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(L(\mathcal{R}),-)\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Precont}}(\mathcal{R},-)\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};-).

We conclude that the composite

|\mathcal{A}|\times|\mathcal{B}|\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow L(\mathcal{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{R}\rightarrow L(\mathcal{R})

is a bimorphism that realizes
L(\mathcal{R})
as a tensor product of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.

Notation 7.4
.

We write
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
for the universal bimorphism
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow L(\mathcal{R})
.

We now show that from binary tensor products we can construct
n
-ary tensor products for all
n\geq 1
:

Proposition 7.5
.

For all
n\geq 1
, there exist tensor products (Definition
7.2
) of all sequences
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
: the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes(\cdots(\mathcal{A}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\cdots))

\displaystyle(f_{1},...,f_{n})

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{1}\otimes(f_{2}\otimes(\cdots(f_{n-1}\otimes f_{n})\cdots))

is a universal multimorphism.

Proof.

Let us prove the claim by induction on
n
. A tensor product of a length-
1
sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{1})
is given by the identity functor on
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, and binary tensor products have already been proved to exist. Let
n\geq 3
and assume that the claim holds for
1
, …,
n-1
. Consider contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. By the induction hypothesis, there exists a tensor product
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, so we can use Theorem
5.31
to obtain the following natural chain of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})

\displaystyle\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})

\displaystyle\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{T};\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{T},\mathcal{C}).

Thus
\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{T}
endowed with the isomorphism
\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};-)\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{T},-)
is a tensor product of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
.
∎

Notation 7.6
.

We write
\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}
for the universal multimorphism
\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes(\cdots(\mathcal{A}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\cdots))
from Proposition
7.5
.

Construction 7.7

(Symmetry isomorphisms)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
be contextual categories. By Proposition
5.18
, for each permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
we have a natural isomorphism
\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n};-)\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};-)
; by the Yoneda lemma, the latter is induced by a unique isomorphism

\beta_{\sigma}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}.

The same proposition shows that although the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}}

generally does not commute strictly, it does up to a canonical natural isomorphism
{\beta_{\sigma}\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}}\Longrightarrow\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}}\circ\underline{\sigma}}
, namely, the unique isomorphism realizing
\beta_{\sigma}
as a permutative morphism
28
28

28

This is an abuse of terminology: when we say that
\beta_{\sigma}
is a permutative morphism from
\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}
to
\mathcal{A}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}
, we mean that it is an arrow

(\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n},\;id,\;\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n},\;\sigma,\;\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma 1},...,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}})

in the category
\text{Perm}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})
from Definition
5.20
.
from
\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}
to
\mathcal{A}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\sigma n}
(see Definition
5.20
).

For
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
, we let
\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A}
be
\beta_{\sigma}
where
\sigma
is the non-identity element of
S_{2}
.

7.1
Associativity

We will now prove that the tensor product of contextual categories is associative up to coherent isomorphism.

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
be contextual categories. To define a comparison morphism
\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
, we start by considering the bimorphism

\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}:(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}.

By Theorem
6.8
(or, more directly, Proposition
6.5
), the functor

((-\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}-)\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}-):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}

is a multimorphism. On the other hand, by Proposition
7.5
, the functor

(-\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}}(-\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}-)):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})

is a universal multimorphism out of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
. It now follows:

Proposition 7.8
.

There exists a unique contextual functor

\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}

that sends
f\otimes(g\otimes h)
to
(f\otimes g)\otimes h
for all morphisms
f
,
g
,
h
in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
, respectively. We will denote it by
\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
and call it the
associator
. ∎

Our next goal is to prove that
\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
is an isomorphism. Note that we have no direct access to an analogous functor in the opposite direction. This is due to how the factors are associated in Proposition
7.5
, which in turn follows from the form of Theorem
5.31
.

The strategy will be to compare
\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})
and
(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
by means of a third object, namely,
\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
. This is motivated by the following observation: Construction
7.7
for
n=3
gives an isomorphism
\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\cong\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
, and for
n=2
it gives an isomorphism
\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\cong(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
. However, we cannot immediately conclude that the composite
\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\cong\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\cong(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
equals
\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
since symmetry isomorphisms generally do not preserve pure tensors; that will be done using the properties of shuffling diagrams and of permutative morphisms described in §
5.3
.

Proposition 7.9
.

\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Firstly, consider the universal trimorphisms (see Proposition
7.5
)

\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle(f,g,h)

\displaystyle\longmapsto f\otimes(g\otimes h)

\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle(h,f,g)

\displaystyle\longmapsto h\otimes(f\otimes g)

Letting
I:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
be the cartesian symmetry isomorphism, we obtain a shuffling diagram (see Definition
5.16
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

By Construction
7.7
, we have a factorization

\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\overset{\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\overset{I^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})

of
F
where
I^{\prime}
is an isomorphism. By construction,
I^{\prime}
is permutative (Definition
5.20
) with respect to
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
and
\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
.

On the other hand, consider the universal bimorphism
\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}:(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
. Letting
J:\mathcal{C}\times(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}
be the cartesian symmetry isomorphism, we obtain a shuffling diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\times(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

and, by Construction
7.7
, a factorization

\mathcal{C}\times(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\overset{\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\overset{J^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}

of
G
where
J^{\prime}
is an isomorphism. Also, by Proposition
6.5
, the functor

\displaystyle M:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle(f,g,h)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(f\otimes g)\otimes h

is a multimorphism with the following property: the outer composite natural isomorphism in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\times(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\times\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\times\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

that is,

\mu(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}):\;\;\overbrace{J^{\prime}\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}^{G\circ(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}})}\qquad=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel\Longrightarrow\overbrace{M\circ I^{-1},}^{\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}\circ(\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\times\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}})\circ I^{-1}}

defines a shuffling diagram
(M,\;J^{\prime}\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}},\;\mu(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}))
. Hence
J^{\prime}
is permutative with respect to
\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
and
M
.

It now follows that
J^{\prime}\circ I^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
is permutative with respect to
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
and
M
. As
\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
is also permutative, by Proposition
5.21
we have
J^{\prime}\circ I^{\prime}\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}=\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
. But from
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
being a universal trimorphism we conclude that
J^{\prime}\circ I^{\prime}=\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
and, in particular, that
\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
is an isomorphism.
∎

One of the requirements for
(\text{Cont},\otimes,\alpha,\ldots)
to be a monoidal category is that the
pentagon identity
hold:

Lemma 7.10
.

For all contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}))\otimes\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}))\otimes\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes((\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\alpha_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}}}

Proof.

Note that the diagram consisting of the inverses of the above morphisms commutes when restricted to pure tensors
((f\otimes g)\otimes h)\otimes i
, where
f
,
g
,
h
,
i
are arrows in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f\otimes g)\otimes h)\otimes i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f\otimes g)\otimes(h\otimes i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f\otimes(g\otimes h))\otimes i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\otimes((g\otimes h)\otimes i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\otimes(g\otimes(h\otimes i)).}}

But the functor
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}))
given by
(f,g,h,i)\mapsto f\otimes(g\otimes(h\otimes i))
is a universal multimorphism, so two contextual functors out of
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}))
are equal provided that they agree on pure tensors.
∎

7.2
Unitality

Recall that
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is the contextual category associated with the precontextual category
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}
whose underlying category is the poset
1\rightarrow 0
with
0
,
1
of length
0
,
1
, respectively. The universal property of
\mathcal{O}_{1}
— that is, being freely generated by a length-
1
object — and the equivalence
\text{Cont}\simeq\text{GAT}
imply that
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is isomorphic to the syntactic category of the
gat
generated by a single sort axiom
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
. This syntactic category admits an explicit description: it has a unique length-
n
object for each
n\geq 0
, namely, the equivalence class
[x_{1}:O,...,x_{n}:O]
, and a morphism
[x_{1}:O,...,x_{n}]\rightarrow[x_{1}:O,...,x_{m}:O]
corresponds to a function
\{x_{1},...,x_{m}\}\rightarrow\{x_{1},...,x_{n}\}
. In other words, the underlying category of
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is opposite to a skeleton of the category of finite sets. We will denote the unique length-
1
object of
\mathcal{O}_{1}
by
o
; its unique length-
n
object is then
o^{n}
. The canonical morphism
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{1}
sends
1
to
o
.

Note that we have isomorphisms

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}})\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{1};\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}})\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}_{1};\mathcal{C})\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1};\mathcal{C})

natural in
\mathcal{A}\in\text{Cont}^{\text{op}}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
. In particular, the Yoneda lemma implies that, setting
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}
and calculating the image of
\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}
along this chain of bijections, we have an isomorphism

\rho_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}

given on pure tensors by sending
a\otimes o^{n}
to
(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n})_{\varheartsuit}(a)
where
\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}
is viewed as a length-
1
object of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{A}}
. It is called the
left unitor
. In particular,
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}(a\otimes o)=a
.

Moreover, composing
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}
with the symmetry isomorphism
\beta_{\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{\mathcal{}}O_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}
yields an isomorphism, called the
left unitor
,

\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}.

Expanding the construction of
\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}
, it is straightforward that it sends
o\otimes f
to
f\otimes o
for any arrow
f
in
\mathcal{A}
. Since
\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}(-\otimes-):\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
, being a bimorphism, preserves distinguished powers in the first coordinate, we have

\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}(o^{n}\otimes f)=\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}((o\otimes f)^{n})=(\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}(o\otimes f))^{n}=(\rho_{\mathcal{A}}(f\otimes o))^{n}=f^{n}.

We now check the final requirement for
(\text{Cont},\otimes,\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
to be a monoidal category — the
triangle identity
:

Lemma 7.11
.

For any contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1})\otimes\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\lambda_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}}

Proof.

Since the three arrows are isomorphisms, we can equivalently verify

\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{B}}\circ(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\lambda_{\mathcal{B}})^{-1}=(\rho_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}})^{-1}.

This equality, in turn, follows from the fact that it holds when restricted to pure tensors
f\otimes g
where
f
,
g
are arrows in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
:

\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{B}}((\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\lambda_{\mathcal{B}})^{-1}(f\otimes g))=\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{B}}(f\otimes(o\otimes g))=(f\otimes o)\otimes g=(\rho_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}})^{-1}(f,g).

∎

Theorem 7.12
.

The category
Cont
, of contextual categories and contextual functors, equipped with the tensor product
\otimes:\text{Cont}\times\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
, associator
\alpha
, unit
\mathcal{O}_{1}
, left unitor
\lambda
, and right unitor
\rho
is a monoidal category. ∎

7.3
Symmetry

We will now prove that
(\text{Cont},\otimes,\ldots)
equipped with the isomorphisms
\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A}
is a symmetric monoidal category (Propositions
7.14
and
7.15
). As a consequence, the multicategory
\mathscr{Cont}
(Definition
6.8
) is symmetric and representable (see
[
22
]
,
[
30
]
).

The key for verifying the required coherence identities will be Proposition
5.21
on permutative morphisms.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
be contextual categories. Recall that, by Theorem
6.8
, the functors

\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}=(-\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}}(-\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}-)):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}),

\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}=(-\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{B}}(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}-)):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{B}),

\displaystyle((-\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}-)\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}-):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C},

\displaystyle((-\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}-)\otimes_{\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}}-):\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathcal{C}

are multimorphisms. The isomorphisms

\displaystyle\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}),

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}:(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}

are permutative with respect to the above multimorphisms.

Proof.

In the first case, we must check that there exists a natural isomorphism
\phi
as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

such that
(\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}},\;(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}})\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}},\;\phi)
is a shuffling diagram (Definition
5.16
).

By construction, the isomorphism
\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}
is permutative with respect to the bimorphisms
{\otimes_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{B}}
and
\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
. Writing
b\otimes^{*}c
for the image of
c\otimes b
under
\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}
, let

\eta=(\eta_{c,b}:b\otimes^{*}c\rightarrow b\otimes c)_{(c,b)\in\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{B}}

be the natural isomorphism that realizes
\eta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}
as a permutative morphism. We will verify that
\phi:(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}})\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}\Longrightarrow\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
defined as

(id_{a}\otimes\eta_{c,b})_{(a,c,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{B}}

has the desired property. Observe that for each
b
,
c
of length
\geq 1
in
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
, we have a length-
1
distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes^{*}c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(b\otimes^{*}c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(b\otimes c).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{b\otimes^{*}c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{b\otimes c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
h
is induced by
\eta
and functoriality of limits. As
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{A}\times(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})
is a multimorphism, by Proposition
5.23
it is a bimorphism in the sense of Definition
3.25
; it follows that for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
with
\ell(a)\geq 1
, the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\otimes(b\otimes^{*}c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\otimes(b\otimes c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\otimes\partial(b\otimes^{*}c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\otimes\partial(b\otimes c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a\otimes(b\otimes^{*}c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a\otimes(b\otimes c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a\otimes\partial(b\otimes^{*}c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial a\otimes\partial(b\otimes c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\eta_{c,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{b\otimes^{*}c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{b\otimes c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\eta_{c,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{b\otimes^{*}c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{b\otimes c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes h}

is such that the left and right faces are — by (i) from Proposition
3.26
— relative length-
1
display maps, and the induced comparison square between the respective gap maps,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\otimes(b\otimes^{*}c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\otimes(b\otimes c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(a\otimes(b\otimes^{*}c))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(a\otimes(b\otimes c)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{a\otimes(b\otimes^{*}c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\eta_{c,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{a\otimes(b\otimes c)}}

is — by (iv) from Proposition
3.26
— distinguished.

A proof in the case of
\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}
can be given similarly, with (iii) from Proposition
3.26
used instead of (iv).
∎

We are now ready to verify that
(\text{Cont},\otimes,\ldots,\beta)
is a symmetric monoidal category.

In what follows, we talk about permutative morphisms without explicitly referring to the multimorphisms that are part of their structure. This will not cause ambiguity as we will only deal with multimorphisms (provided by Theorem
6.8
) that, like
((-\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}-)\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}-):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{B}
, map in the canonical way a cartesian product to an iterated binary tensor product that uses the same factors in the given order, but associated arbitrarily.

Proposition 7.14
.

For all
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
, the isomorphisms
\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A}
and
\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
are inverses of each other.

Proof.

By construction,
\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
and
\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}
are permutative morphisms. As permutative morphisms are closed under composition,
\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}\circ\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
is permutative. Since
\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}}
is also permutative, Proposition
5.21
yields an equality

\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}\circ\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}

between bimorphisms
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
. As
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
is a universal bimorphism, we have
\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}\circ\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}}
.

Exchanging the roles of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in the above argument gives
\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\circ\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}=\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{A}}
.
∎

Proposition 7.15
.

For all
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\otimes(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}

commutes.

Proof.

Every morphism in the diagram is permutative: the components of
\alpha
and of
\beta
by construction, and
\text{Id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\beta_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
and
\beta_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}}\otimes\text{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}
by Lemma
7.13
. It follows that the composites, say
F
and
G
, of the two paths from
\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})
to
(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathcal{B}
in the diagram are permutative.

By Proposition
5.21
, the trimorphisms
F\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
and
G\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
from
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
to
(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathcal{B}
are equal. It now follows from
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}
being a universal trimorphism that
F=G
.
∎

8
A description of certain pushout-tensor maps

Every contextual category
\mathcal{C}
is such that
\mathcal{O}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(where
\mathcal{O}_{0}
is the initial contextual category; see Example
2.10
) can be decomposed as a transfinite composite of pushouts of morphisms of four particular kinds, which we describe below. Although this statement can be verified directly, it also follows from the equivalence
\text{Cont}\simeq\text{GAT}
and the fact that such four kinds of morphisms correspond to the four kinds of axioms out of which every
gat
is constructed: sort introduction, term introduction, sort equality, and term equality.

The four special kinds of contextual functors are the following, for
n\geq 1
:

(A1)

Following Example
2.10
, we have an inclusion morphism

\iota_{n}^{S}:\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}.

We will also write
\iota_{n}^{S}
for its image
\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}
under
L:\text{Precont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
. Syntactically, taking a pushout of a morphism
F:\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
along
\iota_{n}^{S}
corresponds to adding a sort in the context classified by
F
.

(A2)

Define
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
as the precontextual category obtained by freely adding a section
s:o_{n-1}\rightarrow o_{n}
of
o_{n}\twoheadrightarrow o_{n-1}
, and let
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}=L(\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}})
. We have an inclusion morphism

\iota_{n}^{T}:\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}},

whose image under
L
will also be denoted by
\iota_{n}^{T}
. Taking a pushout of
F:\mathcal{O}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
along
\iota_{n}^{T}
corresponds to adding a term whose context and sort are classified, respectively, by
F(o_{n-1})
and
F(o_{n})\twoheadrightarrow F(o_{n-1})
.

(A3)

Let
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}}=\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{n-1}^{\text{pre}}}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
; explicitly, it is freely generated by a diagram of length-
1
display maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o^{\prime}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{0}.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}

We let

\pi_{n}^{S}:\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}

be the codiagonal map, and its image under
L
will also be denoted by
\pi_{n}^{S}
. We write
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee}
for
L(\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}})
. Note that pushouts of this morphism encode sort equality axioms.

(A4)

Let
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++\;\text{pre}}=\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
; it is obtained from
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
by freely adding two sections
s
,
s^{\prime}:o_{n-1}\rightarrow o_{n}
of
o_{n}\twoheadrightarrow o_{n-1}
. We let

\pi_{n}^{T}:\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++\;\text{pre}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}

be the codiagonal map, and its image under
L
will also be denoted by
\pi_{n}^{T}
. We write
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++}
for
L(\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++\;\text{pre}})
. Pushouts of this morphism encode term equality axioms.

It is natural to ask how the tensor product of contextual categories interacts with pushouts of these morphisms: given contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
where
\mathcal{O}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{O}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
have been decomposed as transfinite composites of pushouts of (A1)-(A4), do we automatically get such a decomposition for
\mathcal{O}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
?

Note that this was the case by construction for the tensor product of generalized algebraic theories from
[
5
]
. We will now describe, independently, an entirely analogous phenomenon for the tensor product of contextual categories as defined in the present text. More precisely, we will calculate the pushout-tensor maps
F\widehat{\otimes}G
(Definition
8.1
) where
F
,
G
are pushouts of maps among (A1)-(A4). In fact, in all cases,
F\widehat{\otimes}G
is itself a pushout of a map among (A1)-(A4), and this matches the assignment

\{\text{axioms of }\mathbb{A}\}\times\{\text{axioms of }\mathbb{B}\}\longrightarrow\{\text{axioms of }\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\}

from
[
5
]
. The equivalence between these two points of view will be the essential tool for proving, in §
9
, that the syntactic tensor product from
[
5
]
matches the one from the present article.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
G:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be morphisms of contextual categories. We define the
pushout-tensor map

F\widehat{\otimes}G:(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B})\sqcup_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

as unique contextual functor making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B})\sqcup_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\otimes id_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\otimes id_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\otimes G}

We will now outline a description of
F\widehat{\otimes}G
where
F
,
G
are contextual functors among (A1)-(A4).

Remark 8.2
.

By Proposition
5.34
and the definition of the tensor product, for
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Precont}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Cont}
we have a bijective correspondence, natural in each argument, between bimorphisms

\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

and morphisms

L(\mathcal{A})\otimes L(\mathcal{B})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

This will be used repeatedly in what follows.

Description of
\iota_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{S}

Observe that for a contextual category
\mathcal{C}
, morphisms

(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n-1})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

correspond bijectively (and naturally in
\mathcal{C}
) to functors

H:(\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n-1})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m-1}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

whose restrictions to
\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n-1}
and to
\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m-1}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n}
are bimorphisms. Extending such an
H
to a bimorphism
H^{\prime}:\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
amounts to choosing an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
endowed with a display map
p:a\twoheadrightarrow H(o_{m-1},o_{n})
and a morphism
f:a\rightarrow H(o_{m},o_{n-1})
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m-1},o_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m},o_{n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

is a relative length-
1
display map, where
!
denotes, in each case, the morphism induced by
H
(note that the domain of
H
is a poset category). Such a choice is, in turn, uniquely determined by a choice of length
1
display map
a\twoheadrightarrow H(o_{m},o_{n-1})\times_{H(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})}H(o_{m-1},o_{n})
. This implies that we have a pushout diagram of contextual categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n-1})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{mn}^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{S}}

where the top arrow sends
o_{mn-1}
to
(o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1})\times_{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}(o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n})
and the bottom one sends
o_{mn}
to
o_{m}\otimes o_{n}
.

Description of
\iota_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T}
and
\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{S}

Let us start by describing
\iota_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T}
. For that, note that morphisms

(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

are in natural bijection with functors

H:(\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m-1}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n}}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{+\;\text{pre}}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

whose restrictions to
\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m}\times\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\text{pre}}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{+\;\text{pre}}_{n}
are bimorphisms. An extension of such an
H
to a bimorphism
H^{\prime}:\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
corresponds a choice of morphism
u:H(o_{m},o_{n-1})\rightarrow H(o_{m},o_{n})
such that, denoting by
s
the generating section of
p_{n}
in
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
,

•

u
is a section of
H(id,p_{n}):H(o_{m},o_{n})\rightarrow H(o_{m},o_{n-1})
, and

•

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m},o_{n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m},o_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(o_{m-1},o_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{m},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{m},id)}
