\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,s)}

commutes.

Observe the we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(o_{m},o_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(o_{m},o_{n-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(o_{m-1},o_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,p_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{m},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p_{m},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,p_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(id,s)}

where
a=H(o_{m-1},o_{n})\times_{H(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})}H(o_{m},o_{n-1})
and
v
is obtained by base change along
H(o_{m},o_{n-1})\twoheadrightarrow H(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})
. A routine calculation shows that precomposition with
v
defines a bijection

\{\text{sections of }H(o_{m},o_{n})\twoheadrightarrow a\}\cong\{\text{arrows }u:H(o_{m},o_{n-1})\rightarrow H(o_{m},o_{n})\text{ of the desired form}\}.

Therefore we have a pushout square of contextual categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{mn}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{m-1}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T}}

where the top morphism sends
o_{mn}
to
o_{m}\otimes o_{n}
, and the bottom one sends the generating section
s
to the arrow
s^{\prime}:(o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n})\times_{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}(o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1})\rightarrow o_{m}\otimes o_{n}
defined as the image under the inverse of the above bijection, taken with respect to the bimorphism
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}
, of
id\otimes s:o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1}\rightarrow o_{m}\otimes o_{n}
.

An analogous argument allows us to describe
\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{S}
via a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{mn}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n-1}^{S}}

Description of
\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T}

We have a bijective correspondence, natural in
\mathcal{C}
, between contextual functors

\mathcal{P}:=(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

and functors

H:(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

whose restrictions to
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
are bimorphisms. Since
\iota_{m}^{T}:\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}
and
\iota_{n}^{T}:\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
modify the underlying category without adding display maps or distinguished squares, every extension of a functor
H
as above along

(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}

is a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}},\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}})
to
\mathcal{C}
. But (
*
) realizes the category
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
as a quotient of
(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}}(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}})
by imposing commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(o_{m},o_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(o_{m},o_{n-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(o_{m-1},o_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(o_{m-1},o_{n-1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,s^{\prime})_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,id)_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,id)_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,s^{\prime})_{2}}

where
s
(resp.
s^{\prime}
) is the generating section of
o_{m}\twoheadrightarrow o_{m-1}
(resp. of
o_{n}\twoheadrightarrow o_{n-1}
) in
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}
(resp. in
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
), and we use the subscript
1
(resp.
2
) to indicate an arrow from
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
(resp. from
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
). In other words, a functor
H
of the above form extends to a bimorphism
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+\;\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
precisely when it maps (
**
) to a commutative diagram.

In order to describe this commutativity condition in terms of the maps (A1)-(A4), consider the (non-commutative) diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\otimes_{1}id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\otimes_{1}id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime}}

in
\mathcal{P}
where

–

\otimes_{1}
(resp.
\otimes_{2}
) indicates morphisms in the image of
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
(resp. of
\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{P})
.

–

The square consisting of
p_{m}\otimes id
,
p^{\prime}
,
id\otimes p_{n}
,
q^{\prime}
is distinguished.

–

The section
t
of
p^{\prime}
is the base change of the section
s\otimes_{1}id
of
p_{m}\otimes id
along
id\otimes p_{n}
. Similarly, the section
t^{\prime}
of
q^{\prime}
is the base change of the section
id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime}
of
id\otimes p_{n}
along
p_{m}\otimes id
.

–

u
is the gap map of the square consisting of
p_{m}\otimes id_{n-1}
,
p_{m}\otimes id_{n}
,
id_{m-1}\otimes p_{n}
,
id_{m}\otimes p_{n}
.

Although the equality
(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})=(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)
, which is equivalent to commutativity of (
**
), does not hold, the following weaker statement does:

Lemma
.

We have
u\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})=u\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
realize
a
as the fiber product
(o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n})\times_{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}(o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1})
, it suffices that the above equalities hold when composed with
p^{\prime}
and with
q^{\prime}
. We check this as follows:

\displaystyle p^{\prime}\circ u\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})=(p_{m}\otimes id)\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s)=id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime},

\displaystyle p^{\prime}\circ u\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)=(p_{m}\otimes id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)=(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(p_{m}\circ id)\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)=id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime},

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\circ u\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})=(id\otimes p_{n})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})=(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes p_{n})\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})=s\otimes_{1}id,

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\circ u\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)=(id\otimes p_{n})\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)=s\otimes_{1}id.

∎

Denoting by
k
the arrow in the statement of the lemma, consider the following distinguished square in
\mathcal{P}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{m}\otimes o_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

As
(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})
is a lift of
k
along
u
, there exists a unique section
w
of
u^{\prime}
such that
k^{\prime}\circ w=(s\otimes_{1}id)\circ(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})
. Analogously, there exists a unique section
w^{\prime}
of
u^{\prime}
such that
k^{\prime}\circ w^{\prime}=(id\otimes_{2}s^{\prime})\circ(s\otimes_{1}id)
.

Now, consider a contextual functor
F:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Since, in particular, it sends distinguished squares to pullback squares, it factors through
\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T}:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}
if and only if
F(w)=F(w^{\prime})
.

As a consequence, we have a pushout square of contextual categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m-1)(n-1)+1}^{++}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m-1)(n-1)+1}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{(m-1)(n-1)+1}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T}}

where the top morphism sends
o_{(m-1)(n-1)+1}
to
b
and the generating sections
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
of
o_{(m-1)(n-1)+1}\twoheadrightarrow o_{(m-1)(n-1)}
to
w
,
w^{\prime}
, respectively.

Description of
\iota_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{S}
and
\pi_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{S}

We will give a description of
\iota_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{S}
; the map
\pi_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{S}
can be studied analogously. Note that we have a bijection, natural in
\mathcal{C}
, between contextual functors

\mathcal{P}:=(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

and functors

H:(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

that restrict to bimorphisms from
\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}}
and from
\mathcal{O}_{m-1}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}
. Denote the tree of display maps defining
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee\;\text{pre}}
by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o^{\prime}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{0}.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}

For a functor
H
as above, say corresponding to a contextual functor
F
, we have that
H
extends (and, if so, uniquely) to a bimorphism
H^{\prime}:\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\text{pre}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
if and only if the morphisms

F(id\otimes p_{n}):F(o_{m}\otimes o_{n})\rightarrow F(o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1}),\qquad F(id\otimes p^{\prime}_{n}):F(o_{m}\otimes o^{\prime}_{n})\rightarrow F(o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1})

are equal. But observe that, as
(o_{m-1},o_{n})
and
(o_{m-1},o^{\prime}_{n})
are identified in
\mathcal{P}
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o^{\prime}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}

where the squares with top-left vertices
o_{m}\otimes o_{n}
and
o_{m}\otimes o^{\prime}_{n}
are relative length-
1
display maps. It can be extended to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o^{\prime}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Diamond}}}

where the inner square

is distinguished, and

,

are the respective gap maps of the relative length-

. As a consequence,

is obtained from

by identifying

and

; equivalently, we have a pushout diagram of contextual categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}^{\vee}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{mn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}}}

where the top morphism sends

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{mn}^{S}}

and

Let us describe

\mathcal{P}:=(\mathcal{O}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

and functors

H:(\mathcal{O}_{m}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++\;\text{pre}})\sqcup_{\mathcal{O}_{m-1}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{++\;\text{pre}}}(\mathcal{O}_{m-1}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

that restrict to bimorphisms from

and from
\mathcal{O}_{m-1}^{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+\;\text{pre}}
.

Let

and

be the sections of

in

(or

for the section of

\mathcal{O}_{n}^{+}

as above, say corresponding to a contextual functor

, extends (and, if so, uniquely) to a bimorphism

if and only if the morphisms

are equal.

Now, consider the (non-commutative) diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m-1}\otimes o_{n}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes p_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m-1)n+1}^{++}}}

and functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o^{\prime}_{n}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{0}.}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{n}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

the tree of display maps defining

, a functor

, extends (and, if so, uniquely) to a bimorphism

if and only if the sections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o_{m}\otimes o_{n}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{m(n-1)+1}^{++}}}

Degenerate cases

Arguing as above shows that for
m
,
n\geq 1
the maps

\iota_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{T},\;\;\pi_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\iota_{n}^{T},\;\;\pi_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{T},\;\;\pi_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{S},\;\;\pi_{m}^{S}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{S},\;\;\pi_{m}^{T}\widehat{\otimes}\pi_{n}^{T}

are isomorphisms. For each of these, the proof, which is left as an exercise, involves checking that the extension problem analogous to the ones in the previous cases admits a unique solution, implying that the domain and the codomain of the pushout-tensor map have the same universal property.

9
Comparing the syntactic and the categorical approaches

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be generalized algebraic theories. We will now sketch a proof that the comparison functor

\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})

constructed in
[
5
]
, which was verified in Proposition
3.28
to be a bimorphism from
(\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}))
to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
, is in fact isomorphic to the universal bimorphism
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}),\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})
from §
7
. Using that, we will conclude that the tensor product from
[
5
]
is part of a monoidal structure on
GAT
equivalent to the one on
Cont
obtained in §
7
.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be generalized algebraic theories. Then the functor
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
from
[
5
]
, Construction 6.5 is an isomorphism.

Sketch of proof.

Choose an ordinal
\alpha
and a sequence of theories
(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})_{\mu\leq\alpha}
such that

•

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{A}
;

•

if
\mu<\alpha
, then
\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}
is obtained from
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}
by adding a single axiom;

•

if
\mu\leq\alpha
is a limit ordinal, then
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}=\bigcup_{\mu^{\prime}<\mu}\mathbb{A}_{\mu^{\prime}}
.

Analogously, we choose for
\mathbb{B}
an ordinal
\beta
and a sequence
(\mathbb{B}_{\beta})_{\nu\leq\beta}
. For
\mu\leq\alpha
and
\nu\leq\beta
, write
\otimes_{\mu,\nu}
for the bimorphism
\otimes_{\mathbb{A}_{\mu},\mathbb{B}_{\nu}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu})
, and let

K_{\mu,\nu}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu})

be the induced contextual functor. Let
P
be the subset of the (poset) product
(\alpha+1)\times(\beta+1)
consisting of the pairs
(\mu,\nu)
such that
K_{\mu,\nu}
is an isomorphism. We will prove that
P=(\alpha+1)\times(\beta+1)
by well-founded induction
29
29

29

The fact that
(\alpha+1)\times(\beta+1)
is well-founded follows from ordinals and products of two well-founded sets being well-founded.
: it suffices to verify for each
(\mu,\nu)\in(\alpha+1)\times(\beta+1)
that if
(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\in P
for all
(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})<(\mu,\nu)
, then
(\mu,\nu)\in P
. We have the following cases:

•

\mu
or
\nu
is a limit ordinal (possibly
0
). As
K_{\mu,\nu}
is a colimit of the functors
K_{\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}
for
(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})<(\mu,\nu)
, if the latter are isomorphisms, then so is the former.

•

Given successor ordinals
\mu+1\leq\alpha
and
\nu+1\leq\beta
, assume that
(\mu,\nu+1)
,
(\mu+1,\nu)\in P
. We have a commutative diagram of contextual categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu,\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\iota_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I_{\mu,\nu}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I_{\mu,\nu})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu,\nu+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I_{\mu,\nu+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu+1,\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\iota_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I^{\prime}_{\mu+1,\nu})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu+1,\nu+1}}

This yields a further commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\iota_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I_{\mu,\nu}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I_{\mu,\nu})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{m}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I_{\mu,\nu+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\iota_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(I^{\prime}_{\mu+1,\nu})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu+1,\nu+1}}

where:
\mathcal{D}
is the pushout of
\iota_{\mu}\otimes id_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})}
and
id_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})}\otimes\iota_{\nu}
;
\mathbb{D}
is the pushout of
I_{\mu,\nu}
and
I^{\prime}_{\mu,\nu}
;
F
and
G
are the corresponding gap maps; and
K^{\prime}
is induced by the universal properties of
\mathcal{D}
and of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})
.

Since
K_{\mu,\nu}
,
K_{\mu+1,\nu}
and
K_{\mu,\nu+1}
are isomorphisms, so is
K^{\prime}
. Thus for
K_{\mu+1,\nu+1}
to be an isomorphism it suffices that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu+1,\nu+1}}

be a pushout square. Now, recall that we have given explicit descriptions of both
F
and
G
, each of which is split into sixteen cases: we need to take into account which of the four kinds of axioms is added via
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}
, and similarly for
\mathbb{B}_{\nu}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
.

A description of
F
was given in §
8
using the universal property of the tensor product of contextual categories in terms of bimorphisms.

On the other hand, a description of
G
— or, equivalently, of the morphism of theories
\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
that presents it — is given by the construction of the tensor product of theories; see
[
5
]
, §2. Precisely, in the notation of
[
5
]
, if a judgment
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) is the axiom added via
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}
(resp. via
\mathbb{B}_{\nu}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
), then
\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
is obtained from
\mathbb{D}
by adding as an axiom the judgment
J\odot J^{\prime}
. This allows us to express
G
as a pushout of one of the maps (A1)-(A4) from §
8
.

To compare
F
and
G
, we start by expressing
F
as a pushout of a morphism
\varphi:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
among (A1)-(A4). This yields a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mu+1,\nu+1}}

By the pasting lemma for pushouts, to conclude that the right square is cocartesian it suffices to check that the outer composite one is cocartesian. A routine but lengthy calculation, whose details we have omitted, shows that, in each of the sixteen cases,
\varphi
and the composite
\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\overset{K^{\prime}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})
specify the axiom added via
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
; this, in turn, implies that the outer square is cocartesian.

To illustrate this idea, suppose that
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}
adds a sort axiom
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})
and
\mathbb{B}_{\nu}\subset\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
adds a sort axiom
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})
. In terms of the associated contextual categories, the length
1
display maps
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow[\textbf{X}]
and
[\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow[\textbf{Y}]
, where
\textbf{X}^{\prime}
,
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
are the respective extended contexts, are freely adjoined to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu})
,
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu})
to form
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})
,
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})
. Then, letting
m=\ell(\textbf{X})
and
n=\ell(\textbf{Y})
, by §
8
we have a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m+1)(n+1)-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m+1)(n+1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{(m+1)(n+1)}^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

where the top morphism sends
o_{(m+1)(n+1)-1}
to
([\textbf{X}^{\prime}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}])\times_{[\textbf{X}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}]}([\textbf{X}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}^{\prime}])
, and the bottom one sends
o_{(m+1)(n+1)}\twoheadrightarrow o_{(m+1)(n+1)-1}
to
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow([\textbf{X}^{\prime}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}])\times_{[\textbf{X}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}]}([\textbf{X}]\otimes[\textbf{Y}^{\prime}])
. It follows that in the composite square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m+1)(n+1)-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{(m+1)(n+1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{(m+1)(n+1)}^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

the top morphism sends
o_{(m+1)(n+1)-1}
to
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\times_{[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]}[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]
, and the bottom one sends
o_{(m+1)(n+1)}\twoheadrightarrow o_{(m+1)(n+1)-1}
to
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\times_{[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]}[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]=[\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})]
. But since
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\mu+1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\nu+1}
adds as an axiom the sort judgment

J\odot J^{\prime}=(\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\vdash U\otimes V\;\mathsf{sort}),

whose associated display map is
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow[\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})]
, we conclude that the latter square is cocartesian, as desired.

∎

Theorem 9.2
.

The tensor product of generalized algebraic theories, as defined in
[
5
]
, extends to a functor
\otimes:\text{GAT}\times\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{GAT}
whose action on arrows is as follows: for morphisms of theories
F:\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
and
G:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
,

F\otimes G:\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime}

is the unique morphism whose associated contextual functor makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B}^{\prime}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(F)\times\mathcal{C}(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime},\mathbb{B}^{\prime}}}

Moreover,
\otimes
is part of a closed symmetric monoidal structure on
GAT
.

10
Compatibility with the
Cat
-enrichment

Recall that we have a strict
2
-category – or
Cat
-enriched category –
\mathbf{Cont}
whose underlying
1
-category is
Cont
and whose
2
-cells are the natural transformations between contextual functors. We will now show that this enrichment is compatible, in a certain sense, with the monoidal structure
\otimes
on
Cont
.

Following Notation
5.24
, for precontextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and a contextual category
\mathcal{B}
we write
\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\mathcal{B})
for the category of multimorphisms
(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
and natural transformations between them. In §
3.2
, we constructed for each
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
and
K\in\text{Cat}
a contextual category
\mathcal{B}^{K}
equipped with an isomorphism

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{B}^{K})\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{B})^{K}

natural in each argument; we also saw that
\mathcal{B}^{K}\cong\mathcal{B}^{K_{\text{pre}}}
where
K_{\text{pre}}
is a precontextual category obtained by regarding each object of
K
as a length-
1
object (see Proposition
3.17
).

Lemma 10.1
.

For contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
, the functor

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B};\;\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\;\mathcal{C})

given by precomposition with
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
is an isomorphism of categories.

Proof.

By definition, (
*
) is bijective on objects. Also, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{[1]})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}^{[1]})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Fun}([1],\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Fun}([1],\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\;\circ\;\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\texttt{**})}

where the bottom map is the action of (
*
) on arrows, the left one is the action on objects of
\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C})^{[1]}\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{[1]})
, and (
**
) sends a bimorphism
F:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{[1]}
to the natural transformation corresponding to the composite
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{|}C^{[1]}|\xrightarrow{U}|\mathcal{C}|^{[1]}
.

We claim that (
**
) is bijective, which will imply that (
*
) is bijective on arrows and thus an isomorphism of categories. Note that Theorem
5.31
yields a bijective correspondence between bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{[1]}\cong\mathcal{C}^{[1]_{\text{pre}}}
and trimorphisms
([1]_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. But since
[1]_{\text{pre}}
has no distinguished squares or objects of length
>1
, such trimorphisms are precisely the functors
F:[1]_{\text{pre}}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that, denoting the distinguished terminal object of
[1]_{\text{pre}}
by
z
,

•

F(z,-,-):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is constant on
1_{\mathcal{C}}
;

•

F(0,-,-)
,
F(1,-,-):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
are bimorphisms.

As there is no constraint on the natural transformation
F(0,-,-)\Rightarrow F(1,-,-)
induced by
0\rightarrow 1
, the map

\text{Hom}([1]_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\text{Fun}([1],\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\;\mathcal{C}))

is an isomorphism.
∎

More generally, we have:

Proposition 10.2
.

For
n\geq 1
and contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{C}
, the functor

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{C})

given by precomposition with
\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}}
(see Notation
7.6
) is an isomorphism of categories.

Proof.

We work by induction on
n
. The statement is trivial for
n=1
. Given
n\geq 2
, assume that the claim holds for
1
, …,
n-1
, and consider contextual categories
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{C}
. Then we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\;\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{C})

(Lemma
10.1
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})

(Theorem
5.31
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n},\;\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}})

(induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},...,\mathcal{A}_{n};\;\mathcal{C}).

(Theorem
5.31
)

It can be verified in a straightforward way that the composite isomorphism is given by precomposition with
\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}}
(we are essentially promoting the bijections from the proof of Proposition
7.5
, which are used to define
\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{1},...,\mathcal{A}_{n}}
, to isomorphisms of categories).
∎

Construction 10.3
.

Consider contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Given contextual functors and natural transformations as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}^{\prime},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

whiskering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\varphi^{\prime}}

yields a natural transformation
\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(\varphi\times\varphi^{\prime}):\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(F\times F^{\prime})\Rightarrow\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(G\times G^{\prime})
. As the source and target functors are bimorphisms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, it follows from Proposition
10.2
that
\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(\varphi\times\varphi^{\prime})
extends uniquely to a natural transformation
F\otimes F^{\prime}\Rightarrow G\otimes G^{\prime}
. We denote it by
\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}
. More explicitly,
\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}
is completely characterized by the statement that

(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})_{a\otimes a^{\prime}}=\varphi_{a}\otimes\varphi_{a^{\prime}}

for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
or, equivalently, that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{\prime}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\otimes F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\otimes G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\varphi^{\prime}}

commutes. Pasting such diagrams yields equalities

\displaystyle(\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime})\circ(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\psi\circ\varphi)\otimes(\psi^{\prime}\circ\varphi^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(1_{R}\otimes 1_{S})\circ(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime},

\displaystyle(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})\circ(1_{R}\otimes 1_{S})

\displaystyle=\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}

whenever the arguments are composable as indicated, where
\circ
denotes vertical composition of natural transformations and
1_{R}:R\Rightarrow R
(resp.
1_{S}:S\Rightarrow S
) is the identity of a contextual functor
R
(resp.
S
). Similarly, we have three equalities concerning horizontal composition.

These relations imply that
\otimes:\text{Cont}\times\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
extends, via the above construction, into a strict
2
-functor
\mathbf{Cont}\times\mathbf{Cont}\rightarrow\mathbf{Cont}
. A direct calculation shows that the natural transformations
\alpha
,
\lambda
and
\rho
from §
7
are in fact (strict)
2
-natural transformations between the appropriate strict
2
-functors.

Remark 10.4
.

We know from §
3.2
that
\mathbf{Cont}
is
Cat
-powered. Now, we can also check that it is
Cat
-tensored: for
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\mathbf{Cont}
and
K\in\text{Cat}
, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{Hom}(L(K_{\text{pre}})\otimes\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(L(K_{\text{pre}}),\mathcal{A};\;\mathcal{B})

(Lemma
10.1
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\;\mathcal{B}^{L(K_{\text{pre}})})

(Theorem
5.31
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\;\mathcal{B}^{K_{\text{pre}}})

(Proposition
5.34
)

\displaystyle\cong\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\;\mathcal{B}^{K}).

(Proposition
3.17
)

Remark 10.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
be contextual categories. Working in a Grothendieck universe
\mathscr{U}^{+}\ni\mathscr{U}=\text{Ob}(\text{Set})
, Theorem
5.31
and Proposition
10.2
provide isomorphisms between the category of family-valued models
\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\text{Fam})
and

\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A};\;\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{B}}),\quad\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{B};\;\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}),\quad\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\;\text{Fam}).

However, using this to derive a statement about
Set
-models of the underlying clan of
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
is a subtle matter (see the discussion in
[
34
]
, Remarks 2.17 and B.54) beyond the scope of the present article; we will study it in forthcoming work.

10.1
The embedding
\text{Cat}\rightarrow\text{Cont}

It can be helpful to think about the above results in terms of the embedding of
Cat
into
Cont
given by composing

\text{Cat}\overset{(-)_{\text{pre}}}{\longrightarrow}\text{Precont}\overset{L}{\longrightarrow}\text{Cont}.

Given
I
,
J\in\text{Cat}
, as
I_{\text{pre}}
and
J_{\text{pre}}
have no distinguished squares or objects of length
>1
, the composite

I_{\text{pre}}\times J_{\text{pre}}\longrightarrow(I\times J)_{\text{pre}}\longrightarrow L((I\times J)_{\text{pre}}),

say
\kappa
, is a universal bimorphism out of
(I_{\text{pre}},J_{\text{pre}})
. Now, Proposition
5.34
implies that there exists a unique bimorphism
\kappa^{\prime}:L(I_{\text{pre}})\times L(J_{\text{pre}})\rightarrow L((I\times J)_{\text{pre}})
such that
\kappa=\kappa^{\prime}\circ(\iota_{I_{\text{pre}}}\times\iota_{J_{\text{pre}}})
(where
\iota_{\bullet}:\bullet\rightarrow L(\bullet)
is the reflection morphism), and, in fact,
\kappa^{\prime}
is also a universal bimorphism. This induces an isomorphism
\mu_{I,J}:L(I_{\text{pre}})\otimes L(J_{\text{pre}})\rightarrow L((I\times J)_{\text{pre}})
which, by construction, is uniquely characterized as a contextual functor by the statement that, letting
\iota^{\prime}_{K}
be the composite
K\hookrightarrow K_{\text{pre}}\overset{\iota_{K}}{\rightarrow}L(K_{\text{pre}})
for
K\in\text{Cat}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(I_{\text{pre}})\otimes L(J_{\text{pre}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L((I\times J)_{\text{pre}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\times J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{I,J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}_{I}(-)\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{J}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}_{I\times J}}

strictly commutes. A syntactic version of this isomorphism was described in
[
5
]
, §3.2. Note that we also have an isomorphism
\eta:L(*_{\text{pre}})\cong L(\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{pre}})=\mathcal{O}_{1}
, where
*
denotes the terminal category.

The isomorphisms
\mu_{I,J}
(
I
,
J\in\text{Cat}
) and
\eta
realize
L(-_{\text{pre}}):\text{Cat}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
as a strong monoidal functor — see
[
31
]
, XI, 2 — with respect to
\times
and
\otimes
; to verify the required coherence equalities, we use the fact that a morphism out of
L(I_{\text{pre}})\otimes L(J_{\text{pre}})
can be recovered from its action on pure tensors
f\otimes g
for
f\in\text{Ar}(I)
,
g\in\text{Ar}(J)
. Moreover:

Proposition 10.6
.

The strong monoidal functor
(L(-)_{\text{pre}},\mu,\eta):(\text{Cat},\times,\cdots)\rightarrow(\text{Cont},\otimes,\cdots)
is symmetric: for
I
,
J\in\text{Cat}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(I_{\text{pre}})\otimes L(J_{\text{pre}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(J_{\text{pre}})\otimes L(I_{\text{pre}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L((I\times J)_{\text{pre}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L((J\times I)_{\text{pre}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{I,J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{L(I_{\text{pre}}),L(J_{\text{pre}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{J,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L((\beta_{I,J})_{\text{pre}})}

commutes.

Proof.

Below, we denote
\iota^{\prime}_{K}(h)
by
\underline{h}
for
K\in\text{Cat}
,
h\in\text{Ar}(K)
.

Since
I_{\text{pre}}\times J_{\text{pre}}\rightarrow L(I_{\text{pre}})\otimes L(J_{\text{pre}})
is a universal bimorphism, it suffices to prove that the two composites coincide when applied to
\underline{f}\otimes\underline{g}
for all
f\in\text{Ar}(I)
,
g\in\text{Ar}(J)
. The key point is that, as explained in Remark
5.19
, the shuffling construction from Proposition
5.15
acts trivially on tuples of length-
1
objects (or of arrows between such objects): using that every object in the image of
\iota^{\prime}_{I}
or
\iota^{\prime}_{J}
has length
1
, we calculate

\displaystyle\mu_{J,I}\big(\beta_{L(I_{\text{pre}}),L(J_{\text{pre}})}(\underline{f}\otimes\underline{g})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu_{J,I}\big(\underline{g}\otimes\underline{f}\big)

(Remark
5.19
)

\displaystyle=\underline{(g,f)}

\displaystyle=L((\beta_{I,J})_{\text{pre}})\underline{(f,g)}

\displaystyle=L((\beta_{I,J})_{\text{pre}})\big(\mu_{I,J}(\underline{f}\otimes\underline{g})\big).

∎

Note that
L(-_{\text{pre}}):\text{Cat}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
is a left adjoint: letting
\mathbf{Ob}_{1}:\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Cat}
be the functor that sends a contextual category to its full subcategory spanned by the length-
1
objects, we have isomorphisms

\text{Hom}_{\text{Cat}}(I,\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{A}))\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Precont}}(I_{\text{pre}},\mathcal{A})\cong\text{Hom}_{\text{Cont}}(L(I_{\text{pre}}),\mathcal{A})

natural in
I\in\text{Cat}^{\text{op}}
and
\mathcal{A}\in\text{Cont}
. The right adjoint
\mathbf{Ob}_{1}
can be equipped with a structure of (lax) monoidal functor. For
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
, the bimorphism
\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
sends pairs of length-
1
objects to length-
1
objects, so it restricts to a functor

\otimes_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{1}:\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{A})\times\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{B})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}).

We also have an isomorphism
\eta^{\prime}:*\rightarrow\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{1})
where
*
is a terminal category specified as the unit of
(\text{Cat},\times,\cdots)
. A routine calculation yields the coherence conditions required for
(\mathbf{Ob}_{1},\otimes^{1}_{\bullet,\bullet},^{\prime}\eta)
to be a monoidal functor (we use that the isomorphisms
\mathcal{A}\otimes(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})\cong(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\otimes\mathcal{C}
preserve pure tensors, and that
\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}\cong\mathcal{A}
send
o_{1}\otimes a
and
a\otimes o_{1}
, respectively, to
a
). In fact, arguing as in the proof of Proposition
10.6
shows that
(\mathbf{Ob}_{1},\otimes^{1}_{\bullet,\bullet},\eta)
is symmetric.

Remark 10.7
.

The pairs
(\mu,\eta)
and
(\otimes^{1}_{\bullet,\bullet},\eta^{\prime})
realizing
L(-)_{\text{pre}}
and
\mathbf{Ob}_{1}
as symmetric monoidal functors are actually determined from each other via Kelly’s theory of doctrinal adjunctions applied to the
2
-monad on
Cat
whose algebras are the (possibly symmetric) monoidal categories. We refer to
[
27
]
for an explanation of this correspondence.

This allows us to recover, by
[
36
]
, Th. 3.7.11, the fact that
\mathbf{Cont}
is tensored and powered over
Cat
(that is, we use the monoidal functor
\mathbf{Ob}_{1}
to transfer the self-enrichment of
Cont
, given by its closed structure, to a
Cat
enrichment). Also, Cor. 3.7.12 shows that
L(-_{\text{pre}})\dashv\mathbf{Ob}_{1}
then becomes a
Cat
-enriched adjunction.

More can be done using that
L(-_{\text{pre}})
and
\mathbf{Ob}_{1}
are symmetric. Firstly, by
[
24
]
, Prop. 3.1.11 and Th. 3.3.2, the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
Cont
gives rise, in a canonical way, to a symmetric monoidal structure on the latter in the
Cont
-enriched setting — see
[
24
]
, Def. 1.4.2 and Def. 1.4.13. Now, by
[
24
]
, Th. 2.4.10, the
Cat
-enrichment of
Cont
and the symmetric monoidal structure
(\otimes,\cdots)
can be canonically joined into a symmetric monoidal
Cat
-enriched structure on
\mathbf{Cont}
.

To illustrate this approach, let us use it to obtain the natural transformations from Construction
10.3
. For contextual categories
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, we have an “internal tensor product of arrows” morphism (appearing as
Ten
in
[
28
]
, §1.6)

T:\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}

obtained by transporting the composite
(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})\cong(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}})\otimes(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{ev_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\otimes ev_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, where
ev_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
and
ev_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
are the evaluation maps, along the bijection

\text{Hom}((\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}});\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\cong\text{Hom}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}};(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}).

Then we get a chain of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{B})\times\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime};\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime};\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}})\times\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ob}_{1}((\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{1}_{\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{B}^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Ob}_{1}(T)}

Its composite is of the desired form, and checking that it matches Construction
10.3
can be done by tracking the action of
T
on length-
1
pure tensors.

*
